### A.2 Project / Programme Executive Summary (max 300 words)

Please provide a brief description of the proposed project/programme, including the objectives and primary measurable benefits that will be achieved as a result of the project/programme in the area where it is implemented.

1. The proposed project will enhance the ability of communities, especially that of the smallholder subsistence farmers to address climate-induced shortages of irrigation and drinking water by improving the resilience of farm and land management practices and climate proofing the underlying ecosystems in the Knuckles / Amban Ganga highlands and surrounding areas. This will be achieved through a participatory approach to planning and implementation of agriculture and value-added livelihoods in the area while protecting and complementing the public investments of Monorail, Watawala and other infrastructure projects in the region. The project will also seek to reduce the risks related to increased temperatures, changes to rainfall quantity and intensity, and the impacts of extreme weather events such as floods and droughts on the livelihoods of small-scale farmers and their families who are contributing to different aspects of water supply and demand in the project area which increases the vulnerabilities of small-scale farmers, plantation operations and the natural ecosystems on which they depend.

2. Key project activities will comprise participatory governance and adaptive management approaches to climate adaptation and mitigation, including the development of an agricultural water supply and affordable renewable energy, participatory selection and implementation of best-fit climate-adaptive land management options (e.g., agroforestry, soil conservation, etc.), capacity building for small-scale farmers, access to finance, fast business enterprises and standards and market access. The six-year project aims to induce transformative change and build the resilience of small-scale farmers in the project area by providing them with a participatory exit planning as a resilience model. The project will also facilitate the development of a participatory exit strategy to build the capacity of local communities, including women, for sustainable livelihoods that are climate-resilient.

The primary measurable benefits will include: 13.5 million people (51% in women) who will benefit from the adoption of diversified and resilient agricultural practices, improved access to water resources, enhanced natural ecosystems and natural ecosystems protected and strengthened in response to climate variability and change.

## Description of Financial Elements of the Project / Programme

Project financing and financial resources are required to ensure a projection covering the period from financial disbursement through final maturity of the proposed GCF financing with detailed assumptions and rationale; and an indicative breakdown of total project costs and GCF financing by sub-component in local and foreign currencies. The financial instrument(s) will overcome barriers and achieve project objectives, and leverage public and/or private finance.

### 5.1. Description of Financial Elements of the Project / Programme

#### 3. The Government of Sri Lanka requests a GCF contribution of $ 29.7 M and will leverage (co-finance) an additional $ 9.3 M to develop systems in three key areas: natural resource management, introduction and adoption of climate-resilient practices for resilient livelihoods, and strengthening of institutions to meet the challenges of climate change.

#### 4. Limited financial capacities in communities and the Government hinder the investments in adaptation to climate change. The capacity of farmers and plantation workers to adapt their livelihoods will be severely reduced. This investment project will fill gaps in Government investments and fast track the adaptive management practices needed in the country towards climate resilience while generating lessons that will be useful, regionally and globally.

#### 5. Current land use practices in the upper catchments are vulnerable to high intensity rainfall and winds and the crops are subjected to high temperatures, more intense, less frequent rainfall events leading to more frequent droughts and floods. The current land use practices also result in soil erosion during the time of flowering. The farmers and plantation workers in this area are already under the stress of poverty. Without significant investments in adaptation measures, they will be unable to adapt their livelihoods along with value addition for their produce; they will be subjected to the extensive impacts of climate change. Introduction of climate-resilient practices and technologies will help them to enhance their resilience levels and enhance the income of the communities, technologies to reduce harvest losses and predict climate variability will also be introduced in this project area. The project will also introduce adaptation measures including damal fluctuations to the changes in fauna, flora and the water cycle elements.

#### 6. Fragmented expertise and weak coordination among different Government entities, lack of fine scale understanding of land use practices, inadequate representation of administrative and hydrological boundaries/units and farmers and other producers not receiving stable and adequate prices for their efforts are system related challenges.

### Key Challenges and barriers to be addressed during the project include:

- Living on the margins of poverty, subsistence farmers do not understand the climate risks, consequences and how they can be managed. The capacity of these farmers is further weakened by working equipped with information, technologies and access to markets.
- Limited financial capacities in communities and Government hinder investments in adaptation to climate change including coordination and access to resources have weakened the capacity of farming communities, plantation workers and private sector value chain actors to reach the sustainability levels needed for climate resilience.
- Access to technologies and best practices to improve the climate resilience including the capital required to implement these technologies are limited. This includes water harvesting, land management and natural infrastructures, sediment load reduction to tanks and reservoirs by stabilizing slopes and road side drainages, and value chain development-based products, services and innovation.
- Policies and systems to re-invest the benefits (hydropower generation, savings from disaster risk reduction, etc.) are not in place. This will be addressed through the project by introducing catchment protection, education and awareness and insurance; and
- Slow development of monitoring led decision making, especially monitoring and decision support systems to strengthen the planning and implementation of climate resilience measures.


---
**Page 9**
---

8. GCF resources will be used in the project area to develop systems in three key areas: namely, natural resources management in selected water basins, introduce and adopt sustainable financing for resilient livelihoods, and promote climate-smart agriculture.

The project will target sustainable farming communities, plantations and selected value chain crops (rice, maize and other high-yielding value chains such as strawberry and pepper). It will ensure that these value chains benefit from the use of water resources in a more efficient manner as well as from water resource management techniques, which contribute to the mitigation of climate risks.

10. Ecosystem benefits achieved through the project interventions will be recycled back into the system by way of enhancing the revenue base for the Government by hydropower generation and using taxes and revenues to invest back in catchment protection.

11. The identified co-financing valued at USD M 9.2 is from the Ministry of Mahaweli Development & Environment (MMDAE). This amount will be contributed by different divisions of the Ministry, USD M 5.93 will be contributed by the Mahaweli Authority and USD M 3.27 will be contributed by other divisions including 1.1.1, 1.1.2, and 3.2.2 plus cost sharing during programme management - PMI. Mahaweli Authority (MA) has committed to contribute USD M 5.93 out of which USD M 4.80 will be generated from hydropower generators within the project area as Payment for Ecosystem Services (PES) paid to farmers and USD M 1.13 will be generated by the MA as a result of cost sharing during programme management. The utility purchasing the hydropower receives it is 5% of total payable as PES charge due to the provision of water, before tax. The remaining USD M 0.42 will be contributed from other divisions of the Ministry (MMDAE) under the Ministry contributes USD 300,000 in terms of technical knowledge transfer in reforestation, identification and implementation of climate-smart agriculture techniques as well as promotion of new technologies. FD co-financing also includes the use of Forest Department facilities in Nuwara Eliya and Kandy.

12. The co-financing will further improve during the project implementation through private sector collaborations, additional contributions by government agencies such as the Tourism Board, Export Development Board, and Department of Agriculture etc where potential co-financing levels were not considered at this stage.

13. The GCF financing will be complementary to investments in the agriculture sector in the irrigation area by UNDP/IFC/Norway (USD M 20) and World Bank (USD M 5). This is a total of USD M 25 (see the details in paragraph # 9(2)). Both projects benefit from the additional water generated in the catchment by this investment in project and this project will benefit from water use efficiency investments by UNDP/IFC/Norway and the World Bank.

14. The economic analysis delivered an Internal Rate of Return (IRR) of 21%. The sensitivity of a 10% increase of costs and benefits was also tested, which resulted in an IRR of 18%. In the worst-case scenario, where cost increases and benefit reductions were both applied simultaneously, the project still has an IRR of 15%. The Net Present Value (NPV) of Benefits are higher than the NPV of costs indicating that the proposed project has a healthy economic analysis with a minimal investment risk of Knuckle.

In addition to the adaptation benefits computed in the economic analysis, this investment also generates significant co-benefits. These include reduction in greenhouse gas emissions, carbon sequestration and reduction of land degradation. The estimated mitigation as tons of carbon equivalents is presented in detail in the annexure.

The project is implemented with extensive technical assistance from ICRF and including share of cutting-edge technologies, information delivery methods and decision support systems that are time tested for climate change adaptation and resilience building. These include mobile phone applications developed jointly with telecom operators (Dialog Asia and Sri Lanka Telecom), marketing chains (Keels, Cargills and others), insurance schemes (Sri Lanka Reinsurance, Sinolanka Insurance, etc.), and private sector partnerships (Public and Private) to promote a range of products and services that are new to the project area to add value to the

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## C1. Strategic Context

### Geographical context and climate change vulnerabilities

#### 18. Climate change is impacting Sri Lanka through changes in rainfall, temperature and wind.

As described in Section 2 of the Feasibility Study, the temperature components are increasing while rainfall is becoming more variable with rain falling in a smaller number of high intensity events that are erosive. The people living on the island will be affected by these changes as they impact agriculture, water resources, economic and industrial use and electricity generation. The national water collection and delivery system is threatened by climate change through increased flooding and landslides, which can cause blockages across the country resulting in an increased impact of flooding and landslides in the wet zone, increased frequency and severity droughts in the dry zone and an overall reduction in water availability (noted due to changes in rainfall patterns, temperature, evaporation rates, soil moisture retention capacities and wind speed).

#### 19. The key climatic driver for accelerating degradation of catchments is intense rainfall.

Events over 25 mm/h are becoming more erosive. Climate records from 1965 to 2015 show trends in increasing temperature, reduction in the number of rainy days, and an increase in the number of heavy rainfall events. The frequency of extreme weather events has increased significantly since 1980.

Also, the changes to the temperature gradient from lowlands to highlands due to altitude and changes in wind regimes due to climate change has shifted the temperature related variables (ambient, minimum and maximum temperatures) towards higher altitudes.

#### 20. The climate influence (less rainfall and high intensity and increasing temperature) on the hydrology and ecosystems have prompted multiple mitigation measures including strengthening the canopy structure in the catchments, improving water harvesting and storage facilities, promoting rainwater harvesting and taking measures to increase the availability of groundwater; water that is available for services including drinking and water to be provided as surface or groundwater for agriculture and enterprises.

#### 21. This requires a major change in the way the highlands and upper catchments are managed, both in technical (conservation and landscape management) and behavioral (community to agency functions) terms. The project will focus on increasing the availability of water through rainwater harvesting, harvesting of rainwater (surface and ground) and as a reserve to ensure adequate supply of water for multiple uses in both highland and upland areas covered by this investment.

#### 22. Site specific climate information were developed and presented in detail as part of the preparation of this project proposal. The data are summarized in Annexure A and will be added to the section in the summary in the next section, using downscaled data from an ensemble of 19 global climate models for three emission scenarios.

#### 23. The analysis focused on results of Representative Concentration Pathway 4.5 (medium scenario) and summarized ensemble results according to guidelines in the IPCC fifth assessment* as being likely where >60%, or more models show the same trend and unlikely if <33% or less do so. This indicates a consistent pattern across all models, which is useful for planning purposes such as upstream and downstream project areas (see map below). More rainfall coupled with greater monthly variability indicates a likelihood of increasing productivity and threat to water infrastructure and productivity.

### Counts of ECMAs that predict monthly increases in precipitation by the 2050s compared to the baseline centred on 1973. Black, blue and green colours indicate high rainfall while red-yellow colours are unlikely; grey shades indicate a lack of consensus amongst models.

#### Map of baseline annual precipitation of Sri Lanka included selected points (weather stations) used for predictions (dots). The colour scheme corresponds to the climatic zones of Sri Lanka with the dry zone split into three zones: the wet zone is shown in dark blue (<1,750 mm), the intermediate zone is shown in light blue (1,750-2,500 mm) and the wet zone is shown in dark blue (>2,500 mm). Map scale bar indicates the locations of weather stations used for project predictions.

#### Climate models were used to predict changes to climate projections for the project area by Kindt (2016) attached separately to this PDF as the Climate Analysis. Kindt (2016) used RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 scenarios, which are two representative concentration pathways to global warming. The models also consider the main agricultural seasons (Yellow background = Pre-monsoon Season; Blue background = North-East Monsoon Season; Green background = First Inter-monsoon March to April; Grey background = Second Inter-monsoon May to September; Blue background = Second Inter-monsoon October to November – Yellow background).

*Shrinath Jayasankara, Themiya Dharmika and Roshan Herath. 2017. Observed Trends, Future Climate Change Projections and Possible Adaptation Strategies. Nawaaradhana Climate Change Magazine of Sri Lanka 14:96-151
*Madraswamy et al. 2011
*Dr. Rassied Kindt, Senior Ecologist, World Agroforestry Centre, Nairobi

**Page 17**

27. Agricultural seasons for paddy cultivation in the upland catchment and irrigated downstream areas are April – May (sowing period during Yala season); June – July (growing period during Yala season); October – November (sowing period during Maha season) and December – January (growing period during Maha season). The Yala season corresponds to the South-West Monsoon, which occurs from May to September. The Maha season corresponds to the North-East Monsoon, which occurs from December to February (brown background in the figure). The precipitation patterns are expected to change significantly with respect to the current climate and rainfall variability during the rice and maize growing periods during the Maha season. In most locations, including upland and lowland project areas, May precipitation is projected to decrease in the rice and maize sowing periods of the Yala season (April – May). This will have a significant impact on the yield of these crops. However, absolute magnitudes of precipitation increases are relatively small and therefore, lowland locations may not experience any changes or even decreases in rainfall during the growing irrigation schemes). ### Section 32: Predicted Changes to Future Rainfall

These predicted changes to future rainfall are of concern when considering the increase in soil erosion prevalence between 2002 and 2012 (calculated from analysis of MODIS satellite imagery using universal algorithms by the IORAF Geospatial Centre). The use of high-resolution imagery linked to local ground truth data has been used to create a more precise baseline for monitoring change during the project. It is clear that erosion is already a problem in hilly areas and it will likely get worse if actions are not taken to control it.

### Section 33: Satellite Image Analysis

Satellite image analysis spanning 2002 and 2012 (MODIS satellite imagery based on the land cover classification) indicates considerable reduction in soil organic carbon in Sri Lanka, particularly in the upland areas of the project area. Land degradation has permitted the expansion of low-lying areas where it is associated with reduced agricultural productivity. These effects on local food security are evident.

### Section 34: Downstream Agriculture

There is a clear link between erosion in upstream catchments and irrigation supply to downstream agriculture through sedimentation that is reducing the capacity of reservoirs such as the Kandalama Reservoir in the central hills now has only about half its original capacity (with 47% loss of capacity from 1990 to 2015). Similarly, the Polgolla reservoir has lost 43% of its capacity between 1985 and 2016. It is estimated that Victoria is losing 2-3 MCM of capacity per year (data from MABIF).

### Section 35: Plantation Workers

The plantations in the central highlands are generally owned by the Government and given on long-term leases to estate companies. The socioeconomic dimensions including low labour productivity and minimum wage legislation has led to the abandonment of degraded low productive lands or a shift to other land uses such as tea, rubber, and oil palm. These workers are not aware of either landscape or climate knowledge nor are they aware of the diffuse damage caused by poor land use practices. This scenario leaves workers with neither enough income to buy food to feed their families nor resources to adapt to the impacts of climate change.

### Section 36: Irrigation Water

The farmers in the downstream areas impacted due to reduction in recharge/irrigation water and enhanced salinity in irrigation facilities due to poor land management in the plantation sector. This factor is impacting Government investments to provide irrigation water to the dryland areas in the downstream areas.

### Impacts on the Wet Season Paddy Production in Southern Sri Lanka

The wet season paddy production was severely affected by droughts and floods, particularly in southern parts of the country. The impacts were more pronounced for smallholder farmers who had invested in irrigation systems but faced challenges due to water scarcity.

#### Extended Dry Periods (2015-2017)

The extended dry periods from 2015 to 2017 delayed the maturation of rice crops, leading to reduced yields. As a result, many farmers have shifted their focus towards alternative livelihood activities or migrated in search of better opportunities.

#### Choice of Rice Varieties

Farmers often choose short-duration rice varieties (3 months) like "Bala" and long-duration varieties (5-6 months). However, the choice is influenced by factors such as water availability at planting time. Many farmers cultivate long-duration varieties that provide higher yields but are more susceptible to drought stress.

#### Mahaweli Irrigation System

Managers of the Mahaweli irrigation system face challenges due to the cultivation of long-duration rice varieties and the need for improved adaptive capacity in the system. The following measures are needed:

- **New or Restored Reservoirs**: To improve water holding capacity.
- **Water Management Practices**: To enhance operational efficiency.
- **Sedimentation Control**: To maintain reservoir capacity and improve water holding.
- **Decision-Making Tools**: For crop selection and practices.

### Possible Adaptation and Mitigation Measures

To mitigate the impacts of drought, it is essential to employ a range of adaptation measures. These include:

1. **Improved Water Management Practices**: To enhance operational efficiency and reduce environmental impact while increasing production of major cereals (maize, wheat, and rice).
2. **Nutrient Management**: To improve fertilizer use efficiency.
3. **Drought Tolerant Varieties**: To increase the yield variability of staple crops.

### C2. Project / Programme Objective Against Baseline

#### Climate Change Drives Threatening Water Availability via Erosion and Siltation

52. Natural forest in the Central Highlands impacted by plantation agriculture, human settlements, and development has increased soil erosion, adding large amounts of sediments to river tributaries and then to reservoirs. This has led to a significant increase in sediment loads in rivers, which is estimated at long-term erosion rates of about 13-30 t/m2 per annum with local sediment yields as high as 1000–7000 t/km2-y.

53. A simulation conducted by Rupanatha University using Revised Universal Soil Loss Equation for one of the sub-watersheds within the project area (Feasibility Study Chapter 3 Section 3.15) indicated possibilities in reducing sediment loads (erosion potential) with improved land cover as proposed in the project. Simulations showed that this would translate to a reduction of erosion potential from 8–40 t/km2 per annum at 0-21 km2 per annum.

### Negative impacts included decline in soil fertility, reduction in soil depth, rapid sedimentation of hydropower reservoirs, sedimentation in agricultural lands, sedimentation in rivers and floods in the lowlands, increased risk of flooding, increased damage to the infrastructure, and the change or lower quality of waterways and ecological systems.

### One of the factors that will be important, and to be addressed during the project implementation is the fact that the short-term sediment transport is more related to the flow (intensity of rain) rather than sediment load. This means that sediment transport can be reduced significantly in the lowlands even when more data is available, highlighting the need to avoid maximum flows by modifying land and sediment transport conditions via adaptive measures. Some of the initial thinking is available through van et al 2017<sup>1</sup>, <sup>2</sup>.

### Climate change drives threatening agricultural productivity

#### Agricultural productivity in the downstream areas in Sri Lanka's dry zone are vulnerable to a combination of increasing seasonality of rainfall, resulting in fewer rainy days and longer intervals between them (described above) and increased temperatures. There is agreement amongst GCM predictions that temperature will increase by 1-2°C over the next century with an associated increase in evaporation through increasing potential evapotranspiration (PET). In the project area, the likely decreases in rainfall during one of the driest months (May) are expected to be more than offset by increases in PET. The increases in PET in the Yala season (May) are likely to depress yields and increase irrigation water demand (see graphs below). This is particularly problematic for the dry zone where the majority of the population lives, with downstream agricultural areas where drought is already a problem. Other studies with five GCMs (Loyd et al., 2017<sup>2</sup>) also suggest that the increase in PET for the South-West monsoon season was expected to increase water stress for the North-Eastern monsoon, which is not expected to decrease, which would further exacerbate problems of high rainfall in highland catchments and drought in the dry zone. Climate change impacts on livelihoods, food security and ecosystem services

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 12 OF 124

60. Loss of soil carbon often associated with unsustainable agricultural practices and exacerbated by removal of topsoil is now seen as a global problem undermining soil health, water retention and fertility.

There are two key issues for this project:

- Firstly, climate induced erosivity of rainfall results in the removal of topsoil that is high in carbon contributing to the reduction of soil carbon on the catchment while reducing nutrient availability. This leads to reduced soil organic matter content and a lower water retention capacity of soil, exacerbating flashiness of catchments, unable to hold water when it rains.

61. Drought impacted over one million people (1,041,900) in Sri Lanka in 2017 and 855,307 in 2016 mainly through failure of irrigated rice in dry zone farming areas. Across Sri Lanka officials estimate put the 2017 aggregate rice output at 12.5 million tonnes (17.7 million tonnes, miller basis), which is 43% less than the output recorded for 2016 and 89% below the five-year average of 23.5 million tonnes. The impact of a severe drought at the end of 2016 and early 2017, which compromised water availability for irrigation for the dry zone farmers in the project area, was compounded by the fact that most of these farmers planted what was planted, widespread crop losses and reduced yields. Yields of other crops, including maize, various pulses, chilies and onions, mainly grown under rainfall conditions, were also heavily reduced by the drought.

62. The highland rice farmers and the downstream farmers in the project area are mostly subsistence farmers, because unlike other dry zone farmers who have the opportunity to grow two crops per year due to perennial water availability, these farmers can only grow one crop per year as they do not have perennial water availability. These farmers in the project area are unable on average to complete one crop per year on all of their paddy land and generally produce a yield that is less than 50% of what it should be for the variety being grown, with no surplus to sell. Climate change threatens the farmer's ability to grow enough rice for their annual household consumption and many have no other income source to broker their food security. Most farmers in the project area are also not provided with any form of crop protection assistance (Samarthya) that is provided with food in kind as support to all subsistence farmers.

63. Knuckles area highland paddy farmers carry out cultivation by rainfed or by tapping of stream water from the catchment areas during the dry season, which starts in April 2017 and ends in October for the rice crop, as required water was not available at the critical point in the plant life cycle. The choice of planting short duration rice varieties (3 months – "Bale wa") or long duration rice varieties (5 months – "Mawalwa") is determined by the availability of water during the dry season and the time period for planting. Many farmers choose long duration rice varieties, which provide a higher yield, bearing on the weather conditions in April 2017 when there was no rain to tap from the catchment areas but changing climate patterns are resulting in only short duration rice producing a reasonable yield.

## Baseline efforts and investment

64. Moragahakanda Multi-Purpose irrigation and power generation project at the cost of 94 USD Million (proportional amount for the project area out of the total investment of 370 USD Million) by the Ministry of Mahaweli Development and Environment with ADB as the funding source is the baseline project in the project area. The project was completed in 2018 and the water supply to the project area is SCF (as apportioned). The purpose is to support downstream farmers with irrigation and drinking water to ensure food security, while also generating power for the country's energy needs. However, it faces challenges: Water resources from the Knuckles upper catchment, in the form of surface stream and ground water is one of the key inputs to the Moragahakanda scheme. The volume and frequency of water supply are dependent on rainfall patterns which have been erratic over recent years. This has resulted in intensely driven erosion and resultant silting reducing the water holding capacities of structures in the Ambukadawela reservoir, which is a major source of water for the project area. The baseline investment is completed in 2018 and the current inputs in the project area is an investment of

Smallholder plantations have reduced their investments in infrastructure due to financial difficulties. This will be a complementary initiative to GCF investment projects. Improvements in infrastructure and investments in the plantations contribute towards land degradation. ### Gaps in Baseline Policy Environment

#### Project will support the policy improvements to ensure mainstreaming of climate resilience in land use planning and implementation of national biodiversity plans etc. Illustrative areas include:

- **Policy on data generation and sharing to strengthen decision-making on climate resilience and support science-based decision making:** Currently there is limited organized effort to collect data on the minimum set of variables required for modeling and simulation to support policy and decisions. Limited data availability also hampers the development of a national baseline scenario, which in turn limits the ability to assess the impacts of climate change on water resources, the climate resilience needs, weather, soils, stream flow and water quality (sediment, fecal coliform, BOD, COD etc.). The project will support the use of existing data sets and encourage the use of harmonized datasets.
- **Policy framework to formalize the use of PES in land conservation and ecosystem management:** Currently the concept of PES is designed for the benefit of state agencies and to receive funds to national coffers. The communities who contribute to conserve and manage the ecosystems providing the environmental services are not recognized as stakeholders in this process.
- **Policy articulating the potential land uses and limitations in the central hills:** Present land use of central highlands is affecting the canopy structure that is directly relevant to rain capture. Some poor land uses are associated with land degradation, silation of reservoirs and connected to climate resilience and the disasters such as droughts, landslides and floods.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## C3: Project / Programme Description

### Project objective, outcomes and impacts (Theory of Change - TOC)

The project area links a critical climate sensitive upstream catchment (that we refer to collectively as the sub-catchments) with downstream vulnerable low-lying areas that are dependent on water for irrigation. The key problem that the project addresses is land users' lack of capacity to adapt to climate change, which lies at the nexus of a series of worsening climate change impacts and a series of barriers and constraints to addressing them interact and are interlinked, creating a vicious spiral of upstream catchment degradation and downstream vulnerability. This situation has been further exacerbated by stressors stressed by higher temperatures and more frequent and severe droughts. This results in climate change negatively impacting livelihoods and food security in both the upstream and downstream rural populations.

The project makes direct interventions in the upstream area to help people adapt to climate change by managing their land and water more sustainably and profitably. This improves livelihoods of people in the sub-catchments, which are predominantly smallholder farmers (682,049 ha) as well as through environmental protection of the catchment (168,243 ha) to maintaining the irrigation water supply to the downstream vulnerable low-lying areas (459,654 ha). The project also provides training and support with advice on climate smart agriculture to ensure that efficient use is made of the water in downstream agriculture thereby benefiting the very climate vulnerable tiny land poor population (480,604 female and 480,642 male). The project therefore has an overall reach of 472,319 ha and 1,542.10 people.


---
**Page 31**
---

75. The project objective is to generate resilient livelihoods and improved food security for subsistence farming through capacity to adapt to climate induced change in critical upland and downstream catchment communities in Sri Lanka, including protection of the ecosystem service flows that connect them. The project interventions will also improve the canopy structure of the vegetation in the upland areas, thus enhancing water retention.

76. This investment project contributes to three GCF adaptation impact areas:

- increased resilience of the most vulnerable communities
- increased resilience of health, well-being and water and food security and 
- increased resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services

77. To achieve this, actions are required both to promote sustainable land use and profitability of farming and pastoral activities in upland areas (the immediate project results), as well as to promote efficient use of irrigation water and climate adapted agriculture in the downstream area (the intermediate project results).

78. Downstream area hydropower operators and farmers and industries related to agriculture receive their water needs from the upland catchments. Govt willingness to develop a PES schemes is a clear recognition that there are trade-offs between upstream and downstream interests, and government investment to maintain catchment protection in the upstream. Both upstream and downstream populations have been identified as vulnerable groups (the immediate project results). A nationally WFP estimate that 22% of people are undernourished, despite the overall progress in terms of economic development.

79. In addition to the adaptation benefits, this investment project is based on modification of its canopy structure through agroforestry efforts, that will reduce land degradation and will provide a range of mitigation benefits. These were not considered in the economic analysis but estimated separately as complementary benefits using EX-ACT tool of the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations.

80. Catchment protection and adoption of climate adapted agricultural practices while necessary are not enough on their own to tackle climate adaptation. For sustainability and success, the adaptation interventions need to be integrated with other development efforts that will increase revenue from land use to make it sustainable. The growth of national and global markets for green products and services is a key driver in this regard. However, these markets alone may not be able to sustain. To address this, the investment project focuses on assisting Govt., in overcoming barriers and constraints that land users face in adapting to climate change with the following main thrusts on:

- assisting people to adopt improved natural resource management (component 1 with outputs 1.1 and 1.2) through a combination of agroforestry, water harvesting and conservation of irrigation water and food security in the downstream area (the green cluster of nodes in the center of the diagram)
- putting in place sustainable financing (component 2 with outputs 2.1 and 2.2) to enable people to invest in climate resilient land use; through value chain upgrading and establishment of payment for ecosystem services schemes (the two blue cluster of nodes either side of the central green cluster of nodes in the diagram)
- providing capacity building and institutional strengthening (component 3 with output 3.2) to develop appropriate governance mechanisms, information systems and rural advisory services capable of delivering appropriate climate change adaptation interventions in the project area (the salmon colored nodes on the left and right-hand sides of the diagram).

### Section B1
The project design and the Theory of Change (TOC) in the proposed project is organized under three main components with Component 1: Natural Resources (Given with outputs 1.1 and 1.2), Component 2: Sustainable Finance (Blue with outputs 2.1 and 2.2) and Component 3: Institutional Support (Brown with outputs 3.3).

### Section B2
The project objectives are described as resilient livelihoods and improved food security of subsistence farming communities capacity to adapt to climate change in critical upstream and downstream rural communities, including protection of the ecosystem services that connect them. In the Theory of Change (TOC) diagram below, the outputs for each component are presented in a logical sequence.

The natural resource management group (NRM) outputs 1.1 and 1.2 are presented in green and aim to advance NRM through capacity building and improved governance of natural resources. ### Summary of Components

#### Outcomes (O)
65. To better and sustain climate-adaptation through better management of natural resources, in addition to direct land use interventions with farmers and other land users (Component 1), the project will establish a framework for catchment restoration and upgrading options. This is part of a PES scheme to support catchment protection (Component 2). To achieve this, the project will also work with institutions through developing governance mechanisms that reconcile non-congruent hydrological and administrative boundaries. The project will also develop and implement a suite of tools including rural advisory services, that are required to enable land users to adapt to change (Component 3).

66. This component focuses on improving land and water management with two key outcomes: safeguarding ecosystem goods and services generated in the upstream Knuckles catchment area (Output 1.1); and increasing productivity and climate resilience of primary production in both the upstream catchment area and the downstream command area to which it supplies irrigation water (Output 1.2).

67. This output focuses on providing locally relevant information and resources to effect best practices in catchment restoration and protection thereby addressing the key constraints:
a) locally appropriate information on climate change, land and water management practices and agricultural technologies; and
b) securing access to irrigation reliability for downstream irrigation.

These are addressed through several interventions such as:
i. streamside protection and drainage management along roads;
ii. rehabilitation of degraded areas, including parks, ponds and irrigation networks; and
iii. restoration of forest mosaic landscapes.

Key behavioral changes induced here are for local communities and land users to adopt catchment restoration practices that are locally feasible by recommendations co-adapted to local circumstances, co-adopted with local stakeholders to their circumstances and in the long-term sustaining a continuing culture of innovation and adaptation through local stakeholders co-learning about the process of adaptation rather than only following externally provided prescriptions.

### DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 8 OF 124

**Output 1:** This output rests on two key premises: that water yield of catchments can be sustained through catchment protection, so that Output 1.1 will deliver a greater and more reliable quantity of water for downstream users; and that the quality of this water can also be improved by better management practices through improved irrigation methods and climate adapted cropping choices and management (including those to reduce soil erosion). The premise is that the current agricultural practice in the upstream catchment area is often not well adapted to climate change (more intense rainfall events and increasing wind events and speeds) and so faces low and declining productivity. This output will address these issues by providing information on how smallholder farmers can be provided climate information and relevant climate adaptation options focused on improving water resource management. These include:

- Increasing the intensity of irrigated rice in both upstream and downstream areas;
- Promoting sustainable intensification of smallholder production; and
- Restoration and sustainable intensification of plantations (some sunk investment in restoration from previous projects).

A key focus will be on restoring plantations to productive landscapes that can sustain the nutrition of estate workers and their families through development of forest foods, as well as reversing land degradation through soil and water conservation while operating at adequate profit level to make them sustainable. The project will also provide a demonstration site for these landscape restoration efforts. Open landscapes will be set up on this site to demonstrate their practicality. Climate-proof tree species choices will be made in consultation with the local communities, who are the key stakeholders as it requires behavioral changes in the rural advisory services and amongst farmers to come together in a partnership so, on one hand to provide customized options to farmers, and on the other, to evaluate the performance of these options across contexts and use smartphone Apps to record and share these data and perceptions.

#### Component 2

This component is focused on sustaining improved land management through the establishment of sustainable financing. This involves two major outputs: upgraded value chains developed with the private sector (Output 2.1) and increased access for smallholder farmers to water resources from upstream catchment to fund catchment protection (Output 2.2).

**Output 2.1:** Improved and more efficient green value chains and increased links to new markets developed

This output spans both upstream and downstream target beneficiaries by providing opportunities to sustain adaptive management through capturing enough resources to make local agriculture and associated businesses sufficiently lucrative to survive and prosper. This principally addresses the constraints of:

- Farmers and other producers often receiving low prices for their produce;
- Insufficient resources being available to invest in land restoration, protection and innovation to achieve sustainable production; and
- Lack of opportunities to include innovative internationally recognized mechanisms and standards to enhance the income using culture, heritage and the area.

It involves a number of activities including:

i. Domestic value chain mapping and green market assessments.
ii. Enterprise and institutional development to exploit green growth opportunities;
iii. Strengthening of local institutions for sustainable land management; and
iv. Taking advantage of new concept such as IUCN "Green Listing."

## Output 1: Improved land and water management in upstream catchment areas to safeguard production of environmental goods and services

### Activity 1.1.1: Streamside protection and drainage management along roads undertaken (IUCN)

96. These are conservation measures focused on the linear stream and road networks. This will include vegetation management to control run-off and enhance infiltration (this will have benefits for reducing methane emissions from livestock, as well as improving water quality in streams). Other activities such as soil health management (soil C, water retention, basic function) and focus on streamside management—led by the local communities—will be implemented. These measures will also include erosion control and will also be implemented along roads and in relation to other soil disturbing construction activity (to prevent runoff from entering streams). The project will also work with the communities (and the local authorities) with community management to maintain dikes and avoid soil slip. The water harvested in this way will be directed into the village tanks, ponds and irrigation networks.

97. Roads are a source of huge volumes of runoff that can be safely collected and stored either above or below ground for multiple uses including recharge of groundwater; to support the establishment and growth of trees and other vegetation; as well as for use in livestock watering, aquaculture, fisheries and crop production. However, to gain full from roads, the challenges arising from governance, social access and management will need to be addressed. Policies makers, planners and communities will first be sensitized to the importance and possible benefits of road water harvesting. The project will also work with other stakeholders in the Netherlands, who are currently implementing a global Roads for Water and Resilience project[7] and the Global Alliance for Road Water Harvesting (GARWH) to support the implementation of this activity, to foster learning and exchange of knowledge. This alliance brings together implementers, trainers, researchers, funders and policy makers involved in making roads work for natural resources management and resilience.

98. Rehabilitation and development of approximately 100 village level ponds and irrigation channels including both direct rainwater-harvesting and tapping stream networks. This will include water flow and quality.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Activity 1.3: Restoration of Forest Mosaic Landscapes undertaken (SUCN)

This component focuses on restoring degraded forests within protected areas and forest fragments, re-growing forests in priority areas for the supply of ecosystem services, especially for watershed protection, and the planting of trees outside forests to restore such areas. The project will employ a three-pronged Restoration Framework (FLR) including: (i) inclusive and developed natural resource management, which is based on the principles of the Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR) initiative; (ii) land use planning through multi-sectoral and multi-level governance, with a focus on integrating forest restoration into existing development plans across the landscape to optimize livelihood and environmental outcomes. FLR is more than just planting trees in degraded areas. It is about restoring entire landscapes to a restoration trajectory to meet present and future needs and to offer multiple benefits and land uses over time. This includes: (a) re-establishing natural regeneration of native tree assemblages to restore degraded forests, and various agroforestry interventions, including climate smart agriculture; (b) developing and implementing forest-based ecosystem services and non-forest service provisioning of existing timber plantations (covering over 2,000 ha in the upstream catchment area); and (c) re-establishing native tree assemblages to meet the social, economic, cultural, and environmental needs of people that were previously planted and have been degraded with degradation of ecosystem services in key parts of the Knuckles upstream catchment area.

### Activity 1.2.1: Increasing cropping intensity of irrigated rice in both upstream and downstream areas (MMD4E)

#### Sub-activity 103:
This activity focuses on the development of smart farming techniques to grow appropriate climate-adapted varieties and make efficient use of irrigation water and fertilizer (maximizing use of organic inputs and recycling) as well as employing integrated pest control based on real-time weather and pest incidence data. The project will focus on increasing rice yields in both upstream and downstream areas within the project area, over 90% of which is in the downstream command area (see Map below), represents 18% of the total across Sri Lanka%.

- Rice varieties are categorized into three types according to the time taken for maturity: short duration (up to 3 months), medium duration (3–4 months), and long duration (4.5–6 months) with the short and medium duration comprising over 90% of the rice production in Sri Lanka. There are two cropping seasons, Maha (October–December) and Yala (April–June). The project will focus on increasing the use of water-saving irrigation (TI) and continuous flooding irrigation (CFI) result in a high-water loss. Water saving irrigation methods include drip irrigation (DI), sprinkler irrigation (SI), and alternate wetting and drying (AWD).

#### Sub-activity 105:
The System of Rice Intensification (SRI) that the project will promote is a climate-smart, water-saving crop system that is being utilized by more than 10 million smallholder farmers in over 55 countries. The innovative SRI method involves using less fertilizer and seed, reducing irrigation to once or twice per week, inputs and water. In contrast to the water-intensive continuous flooding method, that causes anaerobic conditions and reduces soil aeration, SRI uses intermittent flooding to maintain aerobic conditions while retaining the retention capacity of the soil. The technology makes it possible to transplant 8–12 days earlier at wider distances between plants (30 cm) than conventional practices. This allows for better root development, which retains nutrients and sunshine, producing stronger stalks and more tillers. SRI methods have reduced the irrigation requirement by up to 50% compared with conventional practices. In addition, SRI practices produced a 49% higher grain yield with 14% less water than under conventional practices.

#### Sub-activity 106:
In addition to promoting SRI, the project will provide customized advice for enhancing rice productivity using appropriate varieties for each context, storage and processing to maintain quality as well as supporting market access. The project will also promote climate-smart agricultural practices. Climate change forecasts showed an increase in agricultural income in a system with varying crop costs and returns in

### Sri Lanka by allowing more diversified crop choices**. This activity will involve the following four key elements:

(i) Maximizing the amount of irrigation water (higher WUE) through better irrigation management and variety choice
(ii) The introduction of SRI method will reduce the amount of water needed to grow rice, as well as reducing methane emissions.
(iii) The soil is kept moist but not continuously saturated, minimizing anaerobic conditions which can suppress the growth of beneficial soil organisms. Baseline information on the amount of water being used with the current flood irrigation method will be calculated across the different rice varieties (short and medium duration) and compared to the amount of water needed for SRI. The difference in water use between these two systems, together with grain yields produced, water use efficiencies will be derived across the three systems. This will be compared against the baseline data for methane emissions across the different watering systems and varieties and will also be determined.

(iv) Achieving a cropping intensity greater than or equal to 2, with enhanced productivity

#### (i) Nearly 72% of rice in Sri Lanka is grown during the wet season in dry areas where water resources are extremely limited. The SRI method has been developed as an alternative for farmers to use for each season.
- A baseline survey on the current varieties widely used by farmers and their potential yields will be carried out. This will include a detailed analysis of the soil type, its fertility status, pH level, organic matter content, the abundance, diversity and activity of soil organisms: bacteria, fungi, earthworms and other soil biota that impede soil fertility will be enhanced and all contribute to plant growth and health and consequently productivity.
- The SRI method is a simple technique which involves planting rice seedlings in small holes in the soil leaves, quickly, shallow and carefully. To avoid trauma to roots and to minimize transplant shock. Rice plants are planted at 20 cm apart within rows and 30 cm between rows. This allows the young plants to keep all leaves photosynthetically active.
- Applications of fertilizers of organic origin (such as plowing in compost, manure or other organic matter) will be encouraged. SRI provides as much organic matter as possible to the soil; while chemical fertilizer gives positive results on yields, it is not sustainable and has negative effects on the environment. It also helps to improve soil texture and structure, which in turn raises water retention.
- Weed control methods such as hoeing or mechanical hand weeder will be promoted that helps aerate the soil and may outperform herbicides or crop protection chemicals. The SRI method is a sustainable approach to rice production.
- Other practices include adjusting the planting window and use short duration varieties in response to weather forecast information, using appropriate tools for transplanting and harvesting, and training farmers on best practices and trainings organized. The validated APSIM-Oryza module will be used to evaluate change in the potential areas for rice cultivation across the project area in response to predicted climate change scenarios**.

#### (ii) Boosting productivity through SRI

- Climate change has shifted the rainfed period over the typical dry harvesting period, and thus harvesting rice has become a challenge for farmers because of fewer sunny days, and also the early arrival of high forest fires have farmed wet rice, but they still continue to face challenges to dry the harvested grains. The project will promote the use of drying beds, which are simple structures made from bamboo or other locally available materials that ensure the right conditions air of drying (long slow as opposed to short and fast) and also provide shade for the grains.
- Drying beds can be constructed in areas where there is a need to dry large quantities of grain. These would act as temporary storage facilities. Heated air driers will be recommended for use on fixed bed batch driers, where farmer

### 10. Diversity (through enhancement of what is available across landscapes) and wind issues

**110. Increase in wind events and speeds associated with climate change, in the interim season between rice crops, during which farmers try to cultivate a cash crop (typically a 3-month period in which beans, sorghum, maize, or other grains are grown). To mitigate this risk, it is recommended that farmers establish windbreaks or switch to wind-resistant short-term crops will be promoted. Participatory development of new varieties of these crops will be encouraged and the promotion of intercropping with base and non-staple crops, groundnuts or other locally available and marketable crops like clovers, peppers, and cardamom. Attention will also be given to perennial crops and annuals such as Canary peas, lucern, mangoes, bananas, etc., which can be grown in the same field during this period. The use of intercropping technology and value addition will therefore, be a key element in this regard. Considering the limited crop diversity that exists at present, it is recommended to promote the cultivation of vegetables as an addition option provided. Vegetables can also be processed to facilitate long- to mid-term storage to avoid post-harvest losses and ensure year-round availability which reduces the impact of reduced prices caused by seasonal excesses in production of these commodities.**

### Activity 1.2.2: Intensification of Sustainable smallholder production (MMD&E)

**111. This will focus on options for sustainable intensification of home gardens, analog forests, spice gardens and annual horticultural crops in the up-stream catchment. Interventions include climate proofing of tree species, intercropping with vegetables, use of multi-study systems and integrated pest management and agronomic management.**

#### 12. Smallholder farmers are the predominant land users in the upstream catchment area, producing food for their families and providing essential services from which smallholder farmer dependents and manage such systems by nurturing trees, along with crops and livestock, on their farms and homesteads. These systems provide a wide range of ecosystem services.

* A prominent component of 'trees outside the forest', smallholder tree-farming systems are primarily 'yard' or 'household' gardens that are managed for food production and consumption.
* These systems can also be used to reclaim degraded public land, as well as to produce timber and other non-timber forest products (NTFPs). Examples of such commodities include forest-like systems (Analog Forestry) where selected species are integrated in natural and secondary forests. The use of these systems is encouraged for the production of a wide range of commodities for home consumption, enhance income through market sales; and reduce risk. Smallholder tree-farming systems can be combined with other production systems.

#### 13. In the up-stream catchment area, the most prominent and diverse smallholder systems include home gardens, spice gardens, annual horticultural crops, and analog forests. Home gardens are species-rich, tree-based systems usually occupying lands immediately surrounding or adjacent to the household and are used for food production and consumption.

* Three components of these systems yield fruits, vegetables, medicines, spices, timber, poles, fuelwood, and other non-timber forest products (NTFPs). These systems provide a wide range of ecosystem services.
* The advent of rural infrastructure and market-economics provides an opportunity for home gardeners to diversify their production. This can be achieved through the sale of surplus produce for cash consumption; and medical/eco-tourism. Spice gardens can be considered a type of home garden focused on commercial production, with the main objective being the production of spices for sale.
* Examples of crops commonly cultivated in spice gardens include cinnamon, pepper, cardamom and cloves. Spice gardens also include timber, fuelwood, poles, and other tree products.

#### 14. As the name implies, analog horticultural systems focus on annual crop production. Crops commonly cultivated in these systems include kidney beans, beets, chilies, okra greens, green onions, cabbages, bitter gourd, tomatoes, onion, garlic, and sweet potatoes. These crops are produced for both household consumption and market sales. Individual farms cultivate a diversity of crops under low input

and rainfed conditions. While focused on annual horticultural crops, these systems also contain tree components that provide both service and production roles. The diversity of tree species is like home gardens.

### Analysing forests as an ecosystem restoration approach:

Analysing forests as an ecosystem restoration approach, which originated in Sri Lanka, seeks to model the process of natural forest development and forest service functions in [re]establishing a sustainable crop-livelihood system. This approach integrates traditional ecological knowledge with modern science through a synthesis of traditional and scientific knowledge, to optimize the production and service potential of the smallholder farm ecosystem. It is based on the premise that forests are not only important for maintaining ecosystem diversity and services. While focused on ecosystem restoration, analysing forests are also designed to provide economic benefits. Annual species are also components in analysing forests.

### Home gardens as a component of sustainable land management:

Home gardens are an integral part of smallholder farming systems and are often viewed as a complement of smallholder systems. From horticultural systems focused on annual crops, to home gardens and spice gardens representing permanent (tree) farming systems, to analysing forests being a forest managed system, the diversity of tree species is like home gardens. Home gardens can be managed with sustainable management. A difference being that home gardens, spice gardens and annual horticulture are managed in an intensive manner, while analysing forests focus on ecosystem restoration with livelihoods as the secondary objective.

### Farmers generally simultaneously pursue tree and annual crop cultivation only where these can be profitably integrated into their existing farming systems:

Farmers generally simultaneously pursue tree and annual crop cultivation only where these can be profitably integrated into their existing farming systems. Annual crops are usually grown in a relay or annual crop yields through appropriate cultivation practices, weed control and fertilization also benefit the productivity of trees. Farmers are interested in intensifying management to improve their production system. However, most farmers have limited knowledge on how to apply sustainable practices and productivity. Farmers are interested in intensifying management to improve their production system. However, most farmers have limited knowledge of and access to quality germplasm, particularly for tree crops, limited experience and capacity in intensive tree crop management, and limited information on and access to value-chain.

### To assist farmers to improve the sustainable intensification of their home gardens, spice gardens, annual horticulture systems, and analysing forests, the following options will be offered within the project contexts:

In other words, the selected options *will* offer farmers a menu of management practices relevant to their specific conditions. As conditions vary in terms of productivity and productivity, labor capital and other production resources, each farmer can choose one or more of these management options that best-fit their specific conditions. Key option offered will include:

- Enhancing species diversity to reduce climate, biophysical and market vulnerability
- Strengthening the genetic base by selecting high-quality seedlings of priority climate-resilient species, varieties and cultivars that match local biophysical and soil conditions;
- Developing a nursery system for producing quality seedlings. This option will specifically produce high quality seedlings of priority species.
- Promoting intercropping and/or interplanted spacing; removing unproductive trees, remove low value species, and increase the vigor and productivity of the remnant stand;
- Promoting agroforestry systems to diversify crops, improve tree vigor and productivity, and increase light levels for understory intercrops;
- Introducing cover cropping with annual crops to improve overall system productivity;
- Promoting the production and use of organic fertilizers to improve soil fertility, soils, improve water recharge, and produce products for the green economy.
- Establishing a rotation system using high-value tree crops to improve water use efficiency and increase tree vigor and productivity; and
- Introducing agroforestry systems.

### This menu of options will remain flexible and dynamic through the project period:

With additional best-fit options identified collaboratively with farmers and project partners as they are rolled out and their performance is evaluated. ### Activity 1.2.3: Restoration and intensification of sustainable plantations (IUCN)

This intervention will include tea, coconut, rubber, timber and large-scale cultivation of spices and include diversification into other crops, such as coffee and the development of forest foods to address the food security of estate worker families, some of whom have entered into contract farming arrangements with estate companies.

The Sri Lankan plantation sector cultivates globally traded perennial commodities. The sector is a long-term contributor to national income, an important source of foreign exchange earnings, and significant contributions to the national GDP and a significant source of foreign exchange. Major plantation crops in the country are tea, rubber, coconut, timber, coffee, spices (e.g., pepper, cinnamon, cardamom), and nutmeg—and to a lesser extent fruits, vegetables and other food crops, predominantly for domestic consumption. The sector has been in decline since 2010, with production falling by more than half between 2010 and 2015. Export earnings increased with agricultural exports comprising one quarter of the total.

The plantation sector is comprised of 20 privately managed regional plantation companies and three state-owned companies. Despite the positive macro-economic outlook, most plantation companies face challenges in terms of financial sustainability, limited access to credit, poor management practices, and limited access to long-term credit. Many plantations are of a medium size, with an average age of around 50 years. The sector has been further exacerbated by a history of unsustainable land management, which caused soil erosion, silting of waterways, and the loss of biodiversity. As a result, the plantation sector generally lacks the financial resources and confidence to invest in rehabilitation activities that would improve its sustainability.

As a result, the plantation sector generally lacks the financial resources and confidence to invest in rehabilitation activities that would improve its sustainability. The majority of the income generated by the plantation workers and their dependent families, whose welfare cannot be improved independently of the economic performance of the plantations, is spent on basic needs such as food, clothing, shelter, education, employment of plantation workers, further putting into stress this population that was already at the burden of limited alternative employment opportunities. The majority of these communities live in remote rural areas and lack access to basic infrastructure. Their location in remote rural areas allow them limited alternative employment and livelihood options, therefore they become prime candidates to experience climate risks as well.

To assist the plantation sector and plantation workers to achieve restoration and sustainable management practices, the project will offer a menu of management options that are relevant for different contexts. As under Activity 1.2.2, the options will offer plantations and plantation workers a menu of management options that are relevant for their specific context. The project sub-team bas will be able to choose and adopt the management options that best fit their specific conditions to improve climate resilience and increase productivity, efficiency, competitiveness, environmental sustainability and profitability. This will include the options offered under Activity 1.2.2. Utilizing those options, the project will also aim at increasing the production of high-value crops such as tea, coffee, spices, and a range of nutritious and high-value superfood species that target domestic and export demand in the green

**C**

125. The options offered to plantation workers will focus on strengthening their food security and livelihoods by establishing food forest production systems on land allocated to the workers by the plantation where they are employed, as well as in areas of their choice within the vicinity of the plantations. Utilizing those options, the project will promote the establishment of diverse multipurpose systems that combine trees with other plants such as vegetables, fruits, and herbs. Such diversified agroforestry systems are linked to nutritious diets, enhanced livelihoods for farmers and climate-resilient farming systems. These systems support ecosystem diversity and nutrient adequacy, which support the overall micronutrient status of food crops leading to better health outcomes. The combination of perennial and annual crops will be the best combination of foods with nutrient density, energy content, and bioactive compounds such as vitamins, minerals, fiber, antioxidants, and phytochemicals (e.g. antioxidants). Such diverse diets are important to plantation worker families as they tend to have limited access to food and limited access to ability to purchase nutritious foods, and have limited access to health care facilities.

126. As in Activity 1.1.2., the options will be presented to planters, companies, and workers in a series of workshops and training sessions. These will include building blocks, role-playing workshops on cross-vists and field days. Best-fit options selected by plantation companies and plantation workers will be applied directly in their fields, including the development of strategically placed exemplar landscapes. Training and capacity-building activities for planters, companies, and workers as described in Activity 3.2.3 will support the knowledge and capacity-building events. This Activity will be closely linked and mutually supportive of activities under Output 2.1.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Activity 2.1.2: Provide technical support for Enterprise and institutional development to exploit green growth opportunities for smallholder farmers in the uplands (ULCEN)

### 130. Knuckles Conservation Forest (KCF) is a crucial geographical area in the proposed project site.

The Knuckles region is a breathtaking landscape consisting of mountains, valleys, and a variety of ecosystems and plant communities. The forest has been recognized for its biodiversity and ecological significance since it was declared at various times as: 1) The Knuckles Conservation Forest, together with the Peak Wilderness Protected Area (PWP), was declared a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 2003; 2) In 2010 to 2020 the Knuckles area was declared a Conservation Area under the National Environmental Act. It is also designated as an Environmental Protection Area under the National Environmental Act. Aside from its scenic beauty, Knuckles is home to a rich variety of flora and fauna, including several endemic species such as the Mahaweli catchment, a major source of water for the country's agricultural sector. Further, the region has been identified by the IUCN Green List as a climate resilience measure.

### 131. The IUCN Green List certification process requires that local communities and rights-holders are effectively engaged in the governance and management of a protected area or candidate landscape. This will be achieved through capacity building for community members, particularly women, to build their capacity and secure resilience to social, ecological and economic changes induced by the climate crisis. Once the KCF is certified as a Green List site, it will also receive support from the IUCN in terms of the support and participation of local actors in relevant sectors and value chains. It will also underpin the sustainable development of the landscape through the establishment of a Green List Site Network (GLSN) or a community enterprise producing a sustainably harvested or farmed product permitted in the buffer zone of the KCF. The GLSN will provide technical assistance to producers, including smallholder farmers and community-based entrepreneurs, through a platform facility linked to sustainable and eco-friendly products. This will enable them to market their services as compatible to a 'Green List' destination. The premium value added to the label we are developing for the KCF is that it provides more than one certification benefit, such as carbon benefits, such as entrance ticket sales, produce and souvenir outlets, and green fees, also linked to supporting the Green List.

### Activity 2.1.3: Identification and implementation of value chain upgrading options for smallholder and subsistence farmers in upland areas (ULCEN)

This involves the joint analysis (linked to Activities 2.1.2-3) and pursuit of meeting buyers' critical success factors and value of chain upgrading options with respect to products, processes, functions, inter-sectoral relationships, and market access. The aim is to identify opportunities for improving existing or new supply chains and market penetration of existing value chains, the participation in new value chains and/or the exiting from unprofitable or environmentally unsustainable value chains. For example, new products such as organic tea, honey, and other forest produce are targeted by smallholder farmers because of their sustainable and equitable production or further processing. It can also involve the establishment of a Green (Sri Lanka national brand) and obtaining appropriate third-party certification and

### Output 2.2: Payment for Ecosystem Services (PES) mechanism established

#### Activity 2.2.1: Developing a portfolio of business cases for negotiating performance-based financial transfer mechanisms (SUCN)

A robust socioeconomic and ecological baseline and supporting information will become the core for the evidence of the provision of the ecosystem service (output-based payments) and address additional concepts such as co-benefits, cost-effectiveness, and the potential to generate other ecosystem services (ES) that are economically valuable and ES provides practicing environmentally best practices. The business case will be developed in close collaboration with stakeholders and partners. Information on ecosystem services and solutions for ES provision will be linked to spatially explicit assessment of ecosystem service values and benefits at the basin scale (Activity 3.1). Performance-based financial transfer mechanisms will be designed based on the integrated land use plan at sub-basin scale (Activity 3.1.1). Payment systems will be developed in close collaboration with stakeholders, including private sector entities, using systems and other socioeconomic profiles by applying behavioral economic methods such as reverse auctions, cost-effectiveness analysis, and willingness to pay. The business case will include options and scenarios for revenue generation from ES beneficiaries, such as hydropower companies and large-scale water users. A financial model will be developed that includes the costs of implementation and a range of possible revenue collections when engaging public entities. An incentive-based scheme, such as PES, synergies with other financing mechanisms (e.g., CDM, JI, REDD+, etc.), and a Quality Assurance/Alternative sustainable finance for activities 1.1.1, 1.2.1 and 3.1 (as a Qualification of the exit strategy).

#### Activity 2.2.2: Setting up a PES intermediary body as a part of the multi-stakeholder platform, and its governance system established (IUCN)

The potential roles of PES intermediary bodies include information exchange, programme design, upscaling and replication, networking, representation and mediation, administration and coordination of PES schemes, and monitoring, evaluation, and reporting. The intermediary body will be a committee within the nested multi-stakeholder platform.

Component 3: Institutional capacity strengthened

Output 3.1: Governance mechanism for sustainable land management and productivity enhancement in the upstream catchment area established

137. Successful governance mechanisms require institutional ownership and support for inclusive and cross-sectoral planning and decision-making processes that are evidence-based, up-to-date, relevant and needs-driven. The SUCN will be supported by a multi-stakeholder coordination platform to ensure multi-stakeholder coordination and engagement that bridges diverse stakeholders and sectors in a coherent way. This will include the development of a governance team at sub-basin level with a dedicated coordination platform relevant to the needs of the location with contextualized composition according to the relevant governance structure. The SUCN will also be supported by an integrated land use planning and mapping team for cadastral mapping. These teams will develop integrated land use plans for the sub-basins using the information system and tools developed under this project. This will include the development of a Sustainable Ecosystems mechanism locally and the implementation of climate-adapted land and water management options at the basin level, with the aim to ensure that these are aligned with and enhance existing ES structures to ensure a participatory grassroots co-design and local adaptation of options.

Activity 3.1.1: Develop an integrated land use policy and planning mechanism at sub-basin scale (SUCN)

138. To build robust institutional support for integrated land use policy and planning mechanisms at sub-basin level, the Sub-Basin Integrated Development and Evidence-Based Decision Making (SIBEDDM) methodology will be used for co-designing and implementing an inclusive evidence-based planning process across non-congruent hydrological and administrative boundaries and the development of an integrated land use plan. This will include a series of multi-stakeholder participatory design and implementation workshops that bring together the relevant sectors and stakeholders, including the District Secretariat, representatives of key local agencies, private sector, plantations and farmers organizations. This will include stakeholder engagement to identify the needs, constraints, opportunities, institutional gaps and structures and the formulation of desired outputs. The workshops will be used to co-design integrated land use plans at sub-basin level with a focus on climate change adaptation and sustainable ES implementation unit. The workshops will provide effective formats for land use planning, regulatory and incentive measures and capacity development to enhance integrated, evidence-based land use planning and policy development.
