To carry out this activity and ensure long-term sustainability, three Sub-basin Integrated Delivery Teams (SIDTs) will be developed. These teams will be able to integrate across non-congruent administrative boundaries and between the public and private sectors. The SIDT members will represent a sub-basin multi-stakeholder innovation platform relevant to the needs of the location/sub-basin. They will also include representatives from the public sector, including local government and other sector actors at the location (linked to 3.2.1). ### Activity 3.1.2 Develop a shared information system to support land use planning, climate adaptation, market information and monitoring of the participatory engagement option (CRAF)

140. Information systems that can be supported integrated land use planning and adaptive implementation will house relevant information as well as being broadly virtual and virtually accessible. The information systems will be co-designed so that end users have significant input into the content and display of the information system. This will ensure that the information is relevant to the local socio-economic and biophysical baselines along with other relevant and available information. The SHARED information system will support planning and implementation in a participatory manner in innovation platforms across scales. The SHARED information system will support planning and implementation in a participatory manner in innovation platforms across scales. The SHARED information system will support planning and implementation in a participatory manner in innovation platforms across scales.

141. The information system will involve developing spatially explicit assessments of land degradation that are critical for the development of effective adaptation options and targeting interventions, while also providing an assessment of soil health. This will be achieved through the use of high-resolution maps with dashboards, establishment of a high-resolution baseline for planning and development of AgriSensing enabling technologies. The Land Degradation Surveillance Framework (LDSF) site will be established in each sub-basin implementation unit. The LDSF is being adopted by over 100 countries as part of the Global Environment Facility’s Ecosystem Health Surveillance Framework (EHSF). This will provide a baseline for assessing land degradation and soil health at high resolution in over 40 countries in the global tropics as part of the Ecosystem Health Surveillance Framework (EHSF). The LDSF provides a framework for monitoring, assessment, early warning systems, and diagnostics (models); this framework will be key to the development of spatially explicit interventions to prevent land degradation. Soil health indicators (e.g., soil organic carbon content, soil texture, soil structure and compaction) and soil health variables (e.g. soil organic carbon) will be mapped at high spatial resolution using remote sensing technologies such as MODIS imagery and other satellite data in a central database. These assessments will build on national level assessments of erosion and SOC done during project implementation. The LDSF will also provide an assessment using MODIS satellite imagery to assess changes in these indicators over the decade from 2002 to 2012.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Options (O)
The things that farmers, communities or others can do to differ. The contexts (C) are the social, environmental and institutional situations of each stakeholder.

### Option (O) Framework
- **Diagnosis:** Compiling information from available sources on the specific problems, where they are and who they affect.
- **Principles:** Assembling ideas and knowledge that can be brought to bear on the problem.
- **Local Adaptation:** Developing solutions, usually resulting from testing of alternatives using an action research or planned comparison approach.
- **Scaling:** Putting the lessons learned to work in more areas for more people.

## The CxC Framework (Core 4 Framework)
The Core 4 framework is the general scheme for implementing the OxC approach. It can be used for all aspects of the project that require intervention on the ground (water, crops, forest, value chains). The four steps are:

1. **Diagnosis:** Compiling information from available sources on the specific problems, where they are and who they affect.
2. **Principles:** Assembling ideas and knowledge that can be brought to bear on the problem.
3. **Local Adaptation:** Developing solutions, usually resulting from testing of alternatives using an action research or planned comparison approach.
4. **Scaling:** Putting the lessons learned to work in more areas for more people.

## The Outputs
The outputs at each stage inform the next and lead to the project outputs, particularly the outlook (1.1) which also contribute knowledge products that can be used beyond the project.

### Shared Information System
The shared information system will provide high-resolution landscape-level baseline information for planning and management of land use in the project area. It will include data on current land managers (smallholder farmers, plantations, local governments) and the project management. The information supplied by partners will be compiled into a preliminary list of land management options that will be utilized by the GN level multiscale stakeholder platform to develop preliminary lists of management options for each landscape unit. These lists will then be refined in light of the social, economic, and operational contexts. When compiling the preliminary menu list, emphasis will be placed on SLM suitability, cost-effectiveness, and potential impact on the livelihoods of smallholders.

### Feedback from Smallholder Farmers
Feedback from smallholder farmers (Activity 1.2.3) and plantation companies and plantation workers (Activity 1.2.4) will be collected to ensure that the preliminary list is relevant for the process used to develop the preliminary list of land management options.

## Activity 3.2.1: Establishment of nested-scale multi-stakeholder innovation platforms from sub-basin to ON scale (IUCN)

### Output 3.2: Integrated rural advisory capacity responsive to developing knowledge base, real-time weather and market information created

#### Key elements of resilience enhancement of communities and ecosystems include the engagement of multiple actors and diverse stakeholders across scales in adaptation planning.

This is aided through the utilization of online dashboards and structured engagement to facilitate stakeholder interaction and information sharing. The SHARED methodology will be applied to engage stakeholders on how they can obtain, share, and utilize information/evidence in prioritizing practices that minimize potential negative environmental impacts while maximizing positive social and economic benefits for all.

The SHARED process includes the co-development of online dashboards to provide an opportunity for stakeholders to interact with each other and with the project team. The SHARED methodology is based on three key principles:

1. Root causes of why constraints (barriers) to adoption.
2. Stakeholder networks and their connectivity, i.e., how they are linked together.
3. Trade-offs between environmental impact and increased agricultural production.

In addition, the dashboards will be used as a tool for stakeholders to identify trade-offs between environmental impacts and increased agricultural production. The SHARED methodology has been applied to engage stakeholders to integrate key insights, design suggestions, and visualizations for the appropriate end users of the dashboards (e.g., CAFES). This will allow stakeholders to make informed decisions through a design process from conceptualizing key themes, grouping them into approaches, and then selecting the most suitable approach. The SHARED methodology is an iterative process for prioritizing data, indicators and visualization options.

### Based on priorities of the location, terms of reference will be established

Each will have different components that are relevant to the specific context and diverse private sector actors at the location. Multi-scale stakeholder innovation platforms will be linked across sub-basins to share information and learning between them. The dashboards will be designed for use by multiple stakeholders through two-way apps and face-to-face. They will allow interactive learning and sharing down to the GN level.

The multi-scale innovation platforms are key to co-design and refinement of adaptation options with farmers and land users.

### The SIDT team activities will be supported by the Decision Support System (DSS) connected at all key agro-ecological zones (AEZs) in the project area. This DSS will provide information on climate, soil erosion, soils, fauna and flora including canopy structures and cover, socio-economic benefits etc). In order to ensure that the DSS is fully functional and operational by the end of this project phase, climate, soil, water and economic models will be identified, calibrated and made operational during the early part of this project. These models will also be used for scenario analysis including the impacts on land use change, biodiversity, and socio-economic benefits. It is envisaged that the DSS will have a wide range of outputs including maps, tables, graphs, a complementary web page, manuals, training, communication materials to promote the relevance, use and adoption of the DSS by all stakeholders in the project area.

The DSS will be updated at least once per year (i.e., demand-supply water balance) in SIDT areas and covering the entire project area. Users will be trained on how to access data from the DSS through a user manual, training sessions and technical coordinates. Access to data/information by agencies and public will be through a sharing data interface with the DSS. The DSS will also provide an opportunity for stakeholders to interact with each other and learning while adding value to project interventions such as project benefits as well as the extent of climate change impacts.

### Dashboards

The dashboards will be designed from the outset using open source data and hosted on the ICRF GeoScienceLab Landscape Portal (landscapeorg.org). This is to ensure scalability and to access and share information across all stakeholders. The dashboards are intended to be embedded into decision-making processes, thereby ensuring access and functionality beyond the life of the project. The input of users and local context has been a major component in the tools design phase, taking the local context of the three target countries, in terms of data access, communication

# C.2. Background Information on Project / Programme Sponsor (Executing Entity)

Description of the Ministry and its major structural components, the project sponsor (Executive Entity) and how it will support the project/programme in terms of equity investment, management, operations, production and marketing.

156. The sponsor of the project is the Ministry of Mahaweli Development and Environment (MDEE) of Sri Lanka. MDEE is the major policy making body in respect of Mahaweli development and environment management and natural resources conservation. It is mandated to coordinate and oversee environmental management activities.

157. The Environment section of the Ministry comprise five key divisions for Air Resources and National Ocean Unit, Biodiversity Division, Climate Change Secretariat, Education Training and Research Division, Environmental Health Division, Forestry Division, Forest Resources Division, Land Resources Division, Environment Planning and Economics Division etc.

158. Financial status and project support: For 2016, MDEE has received allocation for projects of a value of approximately US$3 million from the government budget*. Over the past few years, the MDEE has received financial assistance from various international agencies including World Bank, Asian Development Bank, UNDP, WFP, UNEP and EBRD Bank - Korea.

The Ministry's recurrent expenditure allocation for 2017 and 2018 were Rs. 5,274.04 million and Rs. 5,639.04 million respectively. The MDEE has been implementing various environmental and water management projects implemented throughout the country for the next two years is projected to be USD 38 million. These projects are funded by various international agencies such as GEF, CCF Adaptation Fund and EU. The ministry is the executing agency for the US$ 38 million GCF project that is being implemented with UNDP in the AE.

160. Statutory Institutions and Public Corporations under the purview of the MINEE are Forest Department, Central Environmental Authority, National Environment Protection Department, Mahaweli Authority of Sri Lanka, Central Environmental Authority, Geological Survey and Mines Bureau, State Timber Corporation, Marine Environment Protection Authority, National Water Supply and Sewerage Board, National Land Commission, Engineers Consultancy Bureau (ECB), Gem and Jewelry Research and Training Institute, Moragahakanda and Kala Ella Development Authority, Mahaweli Development Authority, Sri Lanka Ports Authority, Sri Lanka Dairy Development Project.

* http://www.treasury.gov.lk/Documents/1210-15-1077%20August%202016%20-%20pdf%20file%20size%203255-400kb%20date%2008/09/20


---
**Page 61**
---

161. **MMDE has extensive experience in executing large foreign funded projects and is also currently managing the ongoing portfolio of foreign and government funded development programs.** MAASL (Mahaweli Authority of Sri Lanka) operates under the MMDE as a semi-autonomous institution, which has completed an array of major projects including the Mahaweli Development Project (USD 10 billion), the Kuruwita Water Supply Scheme (USD 33.6 million, 2006-2011) in Kurunegala and Anuradhapura districts to empower the farming community for improved livelihoods, and the Mahaweli Irrigation Project (USD 458 million, 2007-2019), which was funded by the Japan Bank of International Cooperation. In addition, major projects such as, Uoma Yaya Multipurpose Development Project (USD 36.5 million) and the Kuruwita Dam Safety and Water Resource Planning Project (DGRWPP, over USD 70 million) are currently being implemented.

162. As the implementing partner of the project, MMDE will chair the project steering committee and provide project management support and in-kind contributions to project implementation through its technical and administrative staff. The MMDE will also provide project management support to the project through its staff as well as the dedicated PMU set up for the project.

163. MMDE will also facilitate the participation of required Government entities as executing agencies in the project, such as the Ministry of Agrarian Development, Dept. of Agriculture, Mahawali Authority, Export Development Board etc.

164. Private sector involvement is investment and promoted as executing partners to promote technology transfer, improve market linkages and transformative change in the way products are developed, packaged and value added.


### C.5 Market Overview (if applicable)

Describe the market for the product(s) or services including the historical data and forecasts.

Describe the competitive environment including the list of competitors with market shares and customer base and key differentiating factors (if applicable). Provide pricing structures, price controls, subsidies available and government policies affecting the market.


165. The project will contribute towards strengthening agriculture-based value chains in order to enhance the adaptive capacity and resilience of small holder farmers. The project will interface with several Govt. programs such as the Mahaweli Development Support to low income households (USD 20 million annually) and the USD 7 million USD annually paid to the poor through the food security fund that can be used to interact with the poor. In addition, the Govt. economic support programmes or assistance to SMEs and MSMEs through the National Bank of Sri Lanka (NBSL) will also be available for farmers depending on the type of value addition to agricultural produce.

166. The produced products in the project include crops such as rice, pepper, strawberry and medicinal plants which are projected to be prepared for high value markets with appropriate processing techniques, packaging and adoption of international labeling and standards.


167. Agricultural product related value addition will be combined with tourism infrastructure and related marketing activities to generate additional income streams that can become a complementary value addition and income generation in the project area. For example, Knuckles Conservation Forest and its Roventon area is the highest visited conservation area managed by the Forest Department.

168. There are area specific products that can be branded and sold to high value markets. For example, bee honey, vanilla, cinnamon, and the medicinal drugs, tincture drops, stock or syrup from a palm tree named Keralu where the syrup is very similar to Maple Syrup in the commercial market.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## C.6. Regulation, Taxation and Insurance (if applicable)

171. There are no applicable licenses or permits related to project implementation.

172. MMDGE will negotiate with the national Treasury, through the National Budget on annual basis, to pay the required amount on account of the project. The revenue budget allocations will be managed by the Finance Division of the Ministry of Mahaweli Development and Environment and the matching funds equivalent to 50% of the revenue budget allocation will be provided.

173. Project activities may include taxes related to services, which will be considered as part of the project costs. For example, if the services are procured directly by the GoSL implementing partners, then the national tax on service (i.e., Service Tax) and the Management of the Domestic Tax VAT (amounting to 12.5% where applicable (e.g. venue and food for training).

174. For services procured by IUCN for the use of IUCN. Government taxes are not applicable based on the host country agreement with IUCN.

175. Insurances for staff and vehicles, when applicable under Govt. regulations, will be charged to appropriate components of the project.

## C.7. Institutional / Implementation Arrangements

176. The focal Ministry for the project will be the Ministry of Mahaweli Development and Environment (MMD&E).

177. IUCN is the Accredited Agent (AE) of the project. The AE functions and its related activities will be undertaken jointly by programmes hosted at Headquarters (GEF/GCF Coordination Unit, Global Finance Unit, Global Programme on Water) and the Regional Office for Asia.

178. The project will be implemented through three Executing Agencies: IUCN, ICRAF and IUCN through its Sri Lanka Country Office. IUCN will function as an in-country entity based on its host country agreement on project management, member and advisory services.

In the AE role IUCN will provide oversight to the project consisting of a) Entering into contractual agreements with the executing agencies (EA) for implementation of the project activities; b) Submitting to EAs, after providing no objection to work plans and budgets; c) Reviewing financial expenditures and progress reports. d) Overseeing Project implementation in accordance with the Project document and Annual Work Plans agreed between IUCN and the EAs. e) Ensuring that all project activities are implemented in a manner which complies with the terms agreed in the project's respective FAA as well as the AEA signed between IUCN and the EAs.

The NSC will review progress of the project at least once before the mid-term NSC meeting and one prior to the end of the year NSC aimed to review yearly progress and approve the next year AWP.


---
**Page 63**
---

180. A project Executing Entity receives project specific GCF funding under the supervision of the GCF Accredited Entity. Executing Entities oversee the tasks within the overall project management structure concerning: a) Undertaking activities directly or through the Programme Management Unit (PMU); b) Managing and implementing activities related to the Environment and Social Management plan; c) Undertaking procurement activities directly or through the Programme Management Unit (PMU); d) Carrying out financial and technical monitoring of activities, including managing project staff relevant to the EE managed project areas; e) Implementing activities as per the work project plan; and f) Carrying out financial and technical monitoring of activities, including the achievement of results.

181. EEs will work with several Partner Agencies at the activity level based on agreements between Partner Agencies and EE facilitated by the PMU. Funds will be disbursed to Partner Agencies directly from EE financial operations based on instructions by the PMU as per the AWIP.

The Secretary, MIMDE will chair the National Steering Committee (NSC) which will provide implementation guidance for the project in terms of financial and procurement matters, including award financial contracts and transfers with actors executing the project, with consolidated financial and technical reporting through the PMU. The NSC will be responsible for ensuring that all transactions are carried out in accordance with financial transfers in accordance with requests by the PMU, governed by the GCF procedural requirements.

183. The strategic guidance and financial approvals for the project implementation will be provided by the NSC comprised of key entities appointed by the Secretary, MIMDE. The National Steering Committee (NSC) is responsible for oversight of the project from inception to completion. At the end of the PMU, NSC will act in accordance with best practices and standards for governing bodies and ensure that the project management delivers expected results within the agreed time frame and budget as well as achieving the desired outcomes at completion. The National Steering Committee will meet on a quarterly basis or at least three times per year.

184. Along with the three executing entities (MIMDE, IUCN and ICRF), the NSC will include the Department of Agriculture (DCA), Department of Environment (DoE), Department of Water Resources (DoWR), National Biodiversity Institute (NBI), Department of Project Monitoring & Management and other selected representatives from among the Department of Agriculture (DoA), Department of Export Agriculture, Mahaweli Authority of Sri Lanka (MALS), Ministry of Health, Ministry of Education, Ministry of Local Government and Community Development, National Community Water Supply (DNCWSS); Forest Department, Department of Wildlife Conservation, Department of Plantation Industries, Department of Land Management, Department of Community Service Management Centre (SCMC), Ministry of Plantation Industries (MPI), Civil Society/Community, and relevant international organizations. In addition, any other relevant institutions and agencies co-opted as and when necessary.

185. PMU may include experts from local and international agencies identified based on project needs with the concurrence of the NSC to provide adaptive programme management support. Agencies specialized in specific areas such as Rainforest Rescue International and other specialized entities will be linked to PMU through this modality.

The Project Management Unit (PMU), established under the MIMDE, will perform according to the policy guidelines for project management following the NSC. A secretariat for a secretariat is headed by the Programme Director selected from an open competitive process; a panel appointed by the NSC on a Terms of Reference approved by NSC along with other PMU staff. The selection of all PMU staff will be carried out in accordance with the relevant guidelines and procedures of MIMDE (MIMDE). Hiring of those selected will be carried out by IUCN or ICRF based on the terms of references of the positions.

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 84 OF 124

### SIDT1 - The lower part of the Sudu Ganga sub-catchment including the Ukurawa, Maita and parts of Akurana and Naula DSS. The Nalanda Oya sub-catchment embracing Palipola and Yatissamata (marked in purple) and the upper part of the Sudu Ganga sub-catchment including the Rottota and Ambon Ganga Korale DSS.

### SIDT2 - The five sub-catchments on the east of the target catchment area (Amban Ganga, Pukwetaya Oya, Thelaguma Oya, Katu Ganga and Knuckles Elowan Kanda Eastern Slope). These are well connected and embrace the large DSS of Lankatilakepitiya and Wilgampura and the larger eastern part of the Nalanda Oya sub-catchment. Most of these areas are in the upper part of the target catchment so the density of vegetation is anticipated to be lighter than in other sub-basin units. SIDT2 also includes the lower parts of the two large reservoirs in the south, comprising of the Udulamadura DSS and part of the Minpe DS (marked in blue).

### SIDT3 - Covers the downstream irrigated area (marked in yellow) where there are possibilities to integrate with several other projects such as the UNDP/GCF and World Bank Smart Irrigated Agriculture Project engaged in enhancing water storage, delivery of water and improving efficiency of water use.

102. The PMU will develop the contracts, guidelines and technical documentation to engage and support SIDs, Implementing Partners and CLIPs. PMU will ensure extensive coordination and experience sharing among all stakeholders involved in the project implementation and monitoring. The NSD will provide policy direction and guidance to improve the coherence and efficiency of this innovative approach to be developed as an up-scalable model.

103. Each SID will be managed by an Area Manager reporting to the PMU via the three component leads, who convene appropriate implementing partners for the work in the area, as directed and supported by the relevant PMU component teams. The SIDs will have different composition according to the nature of options selected.

104. The project at the local level will coordinate closely with district and divisional coordinating committees to ensure smooth local level coordination in project implementation, provide ownership and ensure successful project delivery. The Area Manager will be responsible for ensuring that the project implementation, facilitation and monitoring, which is generally an assigned function of the office and play a key role in the grievance redress mechanisms, as described in the Environmental and Social Management Plan.

105. Local stakeholders and community members have a key role and are expected to extend support in the implementation—through Community Level Innovation Platforms (CLIPs) at GN level and will be involved in project planning, implementation and monitoring. The Area Manager will convene a Multi-Milestone Entity (MME) working together with the other two Executing Entities (UCDN and ICRAF). It will consult with all stakeholders, including vulnerable community members, FBOs, CBOs, private sector players etc; and facilitate their participation in project planning, implementation and monitoring. The MME will establish structures, including reporting and communication lines, and conflict resolution mechanisms. The stakeholders will be involved in the identification of local needs and priorities for the development stage of the project and enable adaptive project management in response to the needs and priorities of the communities.

# RATIONALE FOR GCF INVOLVEMENT

## 0.1 Value Added for GCF Involvement

Please specify why the GCF involvement is critical for the project/programme, in consideration of other alternatives.

### 196.
The GCF project will de-risk the Sri Lankan economy from climate change threats, as it will contribute to climate proofing the Knuckles range areas, as one of key areas in the country for climate related hydrologic controls due to its location and elevation. The project will also help to create a conducive environment for further investments both from the public and the private side.

### 197.
The grant provided by the GCF will contribute to establishing the key infrastructures and systems needed to attract investments further, from the private sector in the value chains targeted by the project. Through output 1.2 of the project, the Knuckles range areas will be climate proofed through intensifying rice production. The project will also increase production intensity in the tea, coconut, rubber and other crops.

### 198.
This investment will support to build the regional capacity towards resilience with adequate infrastructures related to storing and processing of the crops and to strengthen the value chains and livelihoods. As such, targeted subsistence farmers and producers who will supply to the value chain more regularly will benefit from increased incomes (increased and stable income). The project will also provide the region with the necessary tools to better inform investors on opportunities. The project will allow several innovative and sustainable practices to be introduced in the Knuckles range areas, which will contribute to the economic development of the area and to attract non-traditional tourists and others without damaging the ecosystem.

### 199.
The value addition of the GCF involvement and investment is to catalyse paradigmatic changes in governance, management and practices. The project will introduce several innovative and sustainable land use and high-yield practices, with which an estimated 1.3 million people will experience greater vulnerability and deterioration of their living standards, with subsistence farmers pushed beyond their coping capacities. They occupy over more than half of the total area under cultivation in Sri Lanka.

### 200.
The GCF funding will make the Knuckles range watershed more resilient by strengthening the agriculture and water sectors' adaptive capacity. This will contribute to having the enabling environment for sustainable and resilient livelihoods, which are critical for poverty reduction and food security. Even though it is clear that GCF funding in the agricultural system will remain highly vulnerable to climate variations, with a high level of uncertainty and risks. This will not encourage investments and will propagate the risk of food insecurity on many communities in the Knuckles range areas. The project will also contribute towards the improvement of smallholder farming community.

### 201.
GCF funding will focus on interventions that reduce land degradation and its impact, improve water holding capacity of agricultural lands, increase crop yields, enhance water storage and irrigation for subsistence water supplier type farmers in the uplands is improved. Smallholder farmers' efforts for better management will be sustainably rewarded through better markets for their green products and the ecosystem services they produce will be paid. Bringing this to fruition will require a change of mindset from the government and other stakeholders on the management of water by being organized around watersheds with participation of stakeholders.

### 202.
All of these requires catalytic investments to shift practices and beliefs that Sri Lanka can at present afford for food security, poverty reduction and environmental sustainability. As a result of the civil war and subsequent measures taken to revitalize the country's economy, the Government of Sri Lanka (GoSL) is encumbered with a large national debt approximately $65 billion.


---
**Page 72**
---

203. Most of the country’s revenue is currently allocated towards debt repayment. The national government has prioritized enhancing the climate resilience of highlands ecosystems. Its related ecosystem services, and the most vulnerable communities are located in these highlands.

204. The private sector operating in the highlands, primarily plantation companies and smallholders, realize that the land base on which their livelihoods depend is degrading, but their financial conditions are restricted. Most smallholder families have limited land use or tenure rights. They are unable to make long-term investments in this restricted area, where land management practices in neighboring locations affect their land resources as much as they do themselves.

205. Smallholder families have limited land use or tenure rights. Under such conditions, families limit their investments to what is necessary to maximize productivity and profits in the near term. It is understood that the investment required for measures that support long-term sustainability of the land are not practiced for short-term family or business practices.

206. For smallholders, plantations, other private sector entities, and conservation and development organizations, the need to make investments in sustainable land management (SLM) highlands ecosystems is recognized as not having a long-term investment/finance before returns or benefits are realized. Those two issues are aggravated by the current disjointed and fragmented management of the highlands, which lacks systematic spatial planning, guidelines, and policies. A more integrated approach to landscape management that includes land use and management approach is required to attain a transformative change in landscape management in the highlands and upland areas.

207. By providing the catalytic investments outlined in this proposal, the GCF is contributing and adding value to the impacts described here. Failure to make these investments at this juncture is likely to exacerbate the situation further with much greater environmental and social costs in the future.

208. On a concluding note, the project design includes the development of a project exit strategy that recognizes the resilience of the highland ecosystems, the resilience of highland and lowland communities, governance and economic systems and the achievements of the expected results of the proposed project. The project has high impact potential in an area where there is a need for transformative change at both the community level and the national level, where a paradigmatic change in the management and governance of the highlands ecosystems is being sought.

209. The project design includes the development of a project exit strategy that will ensure project achievements and progress towards larger resilience goals will continue in the post-project period.

## D. Exit Strategy

Please explain how the project/programme sustainability will be ensured in the long run, after the project/programme is implemented with support from the GCF and other sources, taking into consideration the long-term financial viability demonstrated in 4.5.3. This should include a description of strategies for longer term maintenance of physical assets (if applicable).

### Financial Sustainability

210. The project will put in place sustainable financing (comprising Component 2 with outputs 2.1 and 2.2) to enable people to invest in catchment protection and sustainable land use, through value chain upgrading and establishment of payment for ecosystem services schemes, respectively.

211. In order to make and sustain climate-adaptation through better management of natural resources, in addition to direct benefits from the project (Component 3) and land users (Component 1), the project will establish sustainable financing for it, through upgrading value chain and the development of a PES scheme to support collaboration among stakeholders.

212. Strategic interventions proposed in the project included recognizing the current royalty payment by private sector operators of hydropower projects as Ecosystem Service (ES) payments. Enacting the legislation for ploughing them back into the project, the upper catchment and also linking the project to other welfare programmes, named “Samvadhi” will further strengthen the project. The establishment of a multi-stakeholder platform and governance programme. This process will empower the beneficiaries to understand the connection between climate change, environmental degradation and their livelihoods. It will also help in building capacity for sustainable land use. Incentives and PES mechanisms proposed will be the transformation thinking that the empowered vulnerable communities can manage the project.

213. The project will establish the governance framework and monitoring framework for PES including setting up the PES intermediary body (as part of the multi-stakeholder platform and its governance system). The establishment of the governance framework includes setting up the financial and advisory support required to sustain climate adaptive practices in the upper catchment area, as well as the lower catchment area. The project will cover the lower catchment area as the Government of Sri Lanka is committed to implementing a PES modality, initially based on renewable energy-hydro-power generation to provide incentives to maintain best practice in terms of catchment protection.

214. The inputs from a wide spectrum of stakeholders covered real climate induced issues in the project area and these inputs were used in designing possible solutions offered in this project. With the engagement of ICRAF (ICRAF) provided technical assistance for the design of the project, which is to address the challenges in the region affected by climate change using innovative approaches with technical assistance by international agencies and national agencies. The project will be implemented through a local agency led and owned project within the first 2-3 years with limited direct implementation by international agencies. This assures that local agencies and local people continue project-initiated activities beyond their project as their own.

#### Actions required are both to promote sustainable land use and profitability of farming that can sustain it in the upstream areas, as well as to promote efficient use of irrigation water and climate adapted agricultural practices.

**231. To achieve this,** actions are required both to promote sustainable land use and profitability of farming that can sustain it in the upstream areas, as well as to promote efficient use of irrigation water and climate adapted agricultural practices. These two elements are inter-connected because the downstream area receives its irrigation water from the upland catchments and the development of these catchments is critical for ensuring a reliable supply of water. The project will focus on delivering water to downstream users, provides the necessary investment to maintain catchment protection upstream, and promotes sustainable land use practices that can be scaled-up in the future. This is an important indicator to shift in Sri Lanka (national WFP estimate that 22% of people are undernourished), despite the overall progress of economic development.

**232. Catchment protection and adoption of climate adapted agricultural practice while necessary are not enough on their own to ensure long-term sustainability because they are unlikely to deliver sustainable outcomes unless coupled with value chain upgrading that generates sufficient revenue from land use to make it sustainable. The growth of national economy is also critical for ensuring the long term sustainability of these investments, as without economic land use profitable enough to be sustainable. This leads to the project focus on assisting GSoS in overcoming barriers and constraints to ensure that they can deliver change through delivering four interacting outputs each directed at overcoming one or more major constraints.**

#### The project will support a paradigm shift in the way the stakeholders (farmers, communities, government agencies, energy etc.) by transferring knowledge, demonstrating best practices, providing technical assistance, and preparing to meet the climate challenges impacting their resilience and sustainability.

**234. The project provides an integrated technical approach with multiple components to understand and manage water and natural resources with improved information sharing, technology and a phased approach to develop a long-term land use and management scenario. The processes and tools developed by the project will be used to identify the most appropriate levels during the project in a manner the best practices will be adopted and integrated into the existing systems for ensuring the paradigm shift during the project stay in the system.**

**235. The GCF investment is aimed at establishing a national level intervention that can be scaled up nationally, which addresses aspects of surface water management, capacity related rural infrastructure development, rehabilitation and restoration of tanks and reservoirs, stream-side and roadside management through community participation, engagement with Government agencies, communities, plantation companies, subsistence farmers among others. The action model on conservation agriculture for areas above 150 m (above sea level contour) to meet the climate challenges will serve as the main pilot project for the GCF funded capacity building in 2018 and 2019. The total area extent of the Central Highlands is 14,100 square kilometres approximately and the GCF investment will cover an area of around 1,500 square kilometres (above the 150 m contour) equating to 9% of the Central Highlands.**

# E.3. Sustainable Development Potential

## E.3.1. Environmental, social and economic co-benefits, including gender-sensitive development impact

### 248. Because of this project we expect shifts to sustainable, climate smart agriculture, which will require less water, pesticides and artificial fertilizers. It will restore forests and improve the tree cover while improving both productivity and resilience of smallholder farmers. This will lead to a reduction in soil erosion and sedimentation, with land degradation and erosion greatly reduced. This will have co-benefits for biodiversity as a greater number of species, more appropriate to the systems concerned are maintained and managed. Connections to self-correcting systems, such as groundwater recharge and water retention, will be strengthened. The project will also contribute to climate change adaptation and reducing vulnerability. The result will be a much more resilient system that explicitly accounts for the needs of farmers by paying attention to their roles in agriculture and green value chains for subsistence and smallholder farmers.

# E.4. Needs of the Recipient

## Vulnerability and financing needs of the vulnerable country and population

### 249. The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) for Climate Change Impact in Sri Lanka (2016-2015) recognizes that Sri Lanka is highly vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change. Projected climate change includes rising temperatures, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, and changes in precipitation patterns. These changes are forecast to have negative impacts on most of Sri Lanka's economic sectors and deplore the nation and its people's livelihoods. The highlands of Sri Lanka are the main source of water for all purposes—for the country, for agriculture, industry, and urban centers. Climate change will lead to a reduction in rainfall, coupled with current trends in land degradation, threaten agricultural productivity in the highlands and downstream areas. This will have negative impacts on food security, agricultural productivity and economic security. The NAP also recognizes that climate change will lead to increased pressure for water resources base and some services sectors of the country likewise depend on the water resource flowing from the highlands and its degradation, sedimentation of reservoirs and the reduction in water quality and use for life in the highlands, the lowlands and urban centers. This situation threatens the food security, agricultural productivity and economic security of Sri Lanka. The NAP also recognizes that climate change will lead to increased pressure on the highlands, which are both national and international public goods and part of the global commons. Protection of the Sri Lankan highlands is not only a matter for Sri Lanka's security, and country's unique biodiversity and ecosystems are not only a national but international concern.

### 250. The NAP recognizes the nation's adaptation needs in terms of nine key vulnerable sectors: identifying adaptation options, and the performance indicators for each vulnerable sector; the project directly addresses all these domains for five vulnerable sectors: food security, water resources, economic and biodiversity and export development. In the food security sector, the project will enhance the climate change resilience of smallholder systems by improving their access to climate information services, increasing their access to markets, improving access to quality germplasm, and strengthening capacity building through demonstrations and on-farm adoption of climate smart practices. In the water Resources sector resilience can be enhanced by improving water

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE E OF 124

### E.4.2 Financial, economic, social and institutional needs

**251. Sri Lanka is facing a significant strain on its economy. Growth was largely driven by reconstruction and increased commodity prices in the period between 2003 to 2012. Even though the country is categorized as a middle-income nation, there are deep regional disparities in wealth and well-being. The percentage of people living below the poverty line has decreased from 45% in 2007/8 to 26% in 2013/14 (at current prices). Real GDP per capita at PPP) declined from 22.3% in 2006/2007 to 8.7% in 2012/2013. Loans and borrowings that financed the post-war reconstruction have been a major factor for this decline, as well as the high cost of living and an onerous debt burden that needs to be addressed by new macroeconomic policies. In the short-term, therefore, GoSL is considering increasing its expenditure through domestic financing, especially the incremental investments required to build the coping capacity of vulnerable groups.**

**252. Poverty and social exclusion are most prevalent in the under-developed rural districts with social vulnerabilities. The districts where the project interventions are situated contain large numbers of smallholder farmers who are culturally and economically marginalized.**

**253. There is an urgent need to implement a new model of water management for smallholder farming communities that will demonstrate how such smallholder farmers can access modern, improved technology and services to make their livelihoods more sustainable. The project proposes to use the resources available under GCF financing to implement such a model in some of the most vulnerable populations. The adaptation interventions in this project are expected to contribute significantly towards reducing climate change vulnerability by uplifting the living standards of the climate-vulnerable smallholder farmers and estate sector communities.**

**254. Sri Lanka also faces technological and financial capacity gaps across institutions, impeding an integrated approach to water management. This is due to a lack of awareness and understanding of sectoral links when managing water supply systems also makes it difficult for institutional actors to implement the proposed activities. The project proposes to address these challenges by engaging government agencies through support to planning, coordination, and implementation of activities for water management, agriculture, and conservation. The project provides training for development of water resources management plans, the establishment of climate-resilient agriculture, water source protection and green agriculture with access to markets.**

The project builds on several country frameworks evolved over a three-year period. The geography of Sri Lanka has resulted in the central highlands playing a key role in the water supply to subsistence farmers in the hill country catchments as well as in providing continuous water for irrigation and drinking for downstream farming communities. This is further compounded by the fact that the majority of the population lives within the jurisdiction of Mahaweli Development and Environment (2016)®. The same need was confirmed during the "Land Health is National Wealth" workshop in 2015.

The Green Lanka Strategy and Action Plan (2016) related consultations were conducted during the latter part of 2016 and early 2017 and involved Government Agencies, Universities, Non-Governmental Organizations, Environment Advisors and other stakeholders. The National Adaptation Plan for Climate Change Impacts in Sri Lanka Mission 2 on "Mainstreaming Biodiversity Conservation for Sustainable Development" includes several actions to address the need for mainstreaming biodiversity conservation into land use planning and water conservation measures. Further the Mission 7 on “Water for All and Always” highlighted catchment protection, water use efficiency, sustainable management of water etc., were all aligned with project objectives and activities. The subjects of protecting catchments to reduce soil erosion, capture greater water in central highlands, enhancing soil quality to improve water holding capacity and enhancing of the ground water recharge are all critical components for a resilient water system and are addressed under the National Green Lanka Strategy and Action plan.

The project proposes MMEC identified the catchment as a critical measure to meet the challenges of climate change. In some of the most vulnerable communities, sectors and areas such as Amba Guda upper catchment and downstream areas of Upper North-Western Province and selected Maheshi H regions, by seeking to enhance the food security (agriculture in this instance), water and irrigation while driving mitigation co-benefits.

269. Prior to the project formulation several field level vulnerable groups have been consulted, including village community representatives, tea plantation communities, livestock farmers, smallholder farmers, etc., on the “Eraduwa plantations” in the project area helped to understand how climate variability (rainfall intensity and shifts) affect their livelihoods, especially the food security. The consultations also included a visit to the Maheshi H region to understand the drivers of deforestation, climate impacts on soil erosion under different vegetation etc. The consultation session also included a visit to “Mathale Sudu Ganga” area on degraded lands and “Riverbank” area on grasslands.

270. Consultation with upstream areas in the project was conducted with the “Pilawwa” community representatives (about 60 numbers) from four village communities, namely, Pilawwa, Alawawa, Rattikanda and Pujukaduwa. The consultations were held to understand how climate variability (rainfall intensity and shifts) influence in their livelihoods, primarily the impact on the subsistence Agriculture. Discussion revealed the reduced rice production due to erratic rainfall patterns and increased frequency of droughts.

271. Communities talked about the lack of price assurance, post-harvesting losses, transport issues, and lack of value addition opportunities, aggravated by climate challenge. Further the smaller land size, averaging between 0.5 to 1 hectare per household, is another major constraint in terms of production and income generation from agriculture. In addition, the climate induced high winds in the area made the farmers to restrict farming work to one season from the year. The high winds also cause damage to the crops and trees, while cold gale wind damages and sometimes the lack of water or more water can create shifts in cultivation seasons.

272. Consultations with the downstream communities in the project area occurred with the communities in the Hetipola area (including Hettipola and Lalgama-Pallagama DS divisional areas). This community reported having less rainfall than usual, which has led to a reduction of water supply for irrigation and drinking purposes. The reliable household water supplies based on harvested rainwater for irrigation and drinking.

273. Plantation communities in Knuckles conservation area consulted included community members residing in Eluwa area (including Hetipola, and Lalgama-Pallagama DS divisional areas). This community reported having less rainfall than usual, which has led to a reduction of water supply for irrigation and drinking purposes. The reliable household water supplies based on harvested rainwater for irrigation and drinking.

274. Tamil speaking populations and communities in tea plantations consulted in tea areas reported a lack of funds to invest in the tea plantation sector due to low prices received from the tea auction market, which has led some of the tea states are being converted to cinnamon and export crops leading to further land degradation. It was also observed that the farmers were not aware about the climate change impacts on their livelihoods or communities. Eradu and Cardamom are two traditional spices that bring good revenue; however, the farmers need initial investment in terms of seeds and inputs for planting. The tea plantation sector can provide value added products that will be long-lasting, nutritious, and hygienic. Potential landslides due to climate induced high rain fall is a factor of concern by the communities, in plantations.

275. During the project preparation, consultations were held to obtain inputs, understand the potential partnerships (Makalana, Maheshi H, Knuckles Conservation Area, etc.), and identify stakeholders. Consultants from various agencies and experts, such as the experts on Analog Forestry, Insurance and telecommunications, were recruited to help in the project formulation. A detailed listing of stakeholder characteristics is presented in the annexure to the FP on stakeholder analysis.

### E.5.2 Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

Please describe the capacity of the accredited entity and executing entities with respect to the activities that they are expected to undertake in the implementation programme.

#### The Accredited Agency (AA) for this project—International Union for Conservation of Nature—IUCN—is an organization that operates in Sri Lanka since 1988 based on an agreement with the Government of Sri Lanka. IUCN is a neutral, non-profit, multi-sectoral organization that works with governments and Non-Government entities, namely, Ministry of Mahaweli Development and Environment, Department of Wildlife Conservation, National Aquatic Resources Agency, Department of Agriculture, Central Bank, and Ministry of Agriculture, Agrarian Development, Minor Irrigation, Industries and Environment in Western Province, Sri Lanka Nature Conservation Society, Green Lanka Foundation, Federation of Wildlife Conservation, Green Movement of Sri Lanka and Small Fishers Federation.

Throughout IUCN’s 25 years of operation in Sri Lanka, the organization has been able to showcase its country’s unique biodiversity and natural resources through a range of conservation projects. These projects include those that range from the grassroots level to government entities. IUCN helped Sri Lanka to collect and process a range of information using multi-sector and multi-stakeholder approaches related to CBD, UNFCCC, UNEP and other conventions.

IUCN’s convening power and working modalities will allow it effectively execute its role in the oversight and governance on both technical and financial aspects of the project. IUCN Asia Regional Office in Bangkok and IUCN Sri Lanka have provided necessary technical backstopping for smooth implementation of the project and to ensure quality reporting to GEF.

IUCN in Sri Lanka has helped the Government in developing several master plans and strategies. For example, IUCN worked with the Ministry of Mahaweli Development and Environment on the development of “Strategies and Actions for Marine Pollution Control,” “Yan Oya and Upper Elahera Canal Human – Elephant Conflict Mitigation Strategy,” session to Long Term Biodiversity Action Plan to manage and conserve Ratna River Basin” to name a few.

Water resources and land use planning has been a core area in IUCN in Sri Lanka. IUCN worked with HSBC on tank restoration to arrest droughts and floods in dry zone lake cascades, worked with Brandix Private Ltd. to improve water quality of the Mahaweli River, and worked with the Department of Agriculture in tea estates, is working currently on a biodiversity accrual project in southern Sri Lanka funded by 10 private sector companies.

IUCN has been involved in GEF programmes including the current project development for IUCN/JNPD joint Project covering tourism, water resources and sustainable forest management. BIOFIN, IFAD implemented Component of the project.

In terms of evaluations UICN in Sri Lanka has conducted a range of evaluations on behalf of donors, with the most recent being the UN Environment funded ‘Lessons Learned from Five Years of Implementation of the Northern Province Integrated Strategic Environment Assessment’.

# E.6 Efficiency and Effectiveness

## Economic efficiency, appropriateness and soundness of the project/programme

### E.6.1 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

292. As indicated in the Feasibility Study, it is estimated that a sum of USD Min 1.2 could be saved from the Government budget on de-silting (approximately LKR Min 3-4 per small tank) to cope with the existing silting issue. The Government budgets on hex-de-silting could be diverted in consultation with the proposed project for PES sustainability and diversification of livelihoods.

293. On average a farmer has a land the size of 0.2-0.5 ha and the current monthly income/benefit from this land is calculated as LKR 6,000. After the project implementation, the income is expected to reach around LKR 15,000 per annum with additional support to increase the marginal benefit by LKR 8,000. The total economic benefits per annum will be USD Min 45.

294. It is estimated approximately 1.3 million water units (water units according to the National Water Supply and Drainage Board) could be collected per year through roadside drainage management. It is valued as USD 48,000.

295. The main economic crops and other field crops significantly contribute directly to the overall economy in the country. These include tea, rubber, coconut, paddy, sugarcane, etc., which are grown on large scale with irrigation and plantation companies to eliminate the drawbacks of the production and the quality due to climate change impacts. The project will provide a conducive environment for the farmers to grow these crops by providing suitable crops to interact with the economic crops, especially when cultivating the abandoned and degraded lands. These steps will benefit estate workers to enhance their living standards and the profit margins of the plantation companies.

296. Water for irrigation and domestic purposes during dry spells is an issue for both upstream and downstream areas of the proposed project area. This issue has strong linkages with gender issues created as a result of climate change impacts. The women are more vulnerable to this problem because they have to walk long distances in order to collect water from distant sources, which increases the number of hours spent by women to find water, by traveling long distances. Considering most vulnerable areas for drought and water availability, the tank rehabilitation will directly have an economic impact worth minimum USD 375,000 per annum that the women can be involved in productive economic activities during the time they spend before to find water.

297. Through the project involvement on value chain development, the marginal income per family will be approximately LKR Min 18,000 per month. In total, USD Min 1.2 could be calculated as the economic benefit created from this benefit stream.

298. The chain of issues related to upstream agriculture production by subsistence farmers is climate change and productivity – low quality and less production – disadvantages in global competition – low demand – low income for the farmers. This problem can be addressed through a number of interventions such as: intercropping/relay cropping – crop intensification – competitive advantages i.e standards, green approaches, etc. – high global demand – increased income for subsistence farmers. It is estimated approximately USD 800,000 worth of marginal economic benefits will be created.

299. Sri Lanka is importing herbs for domestic needs mainly from India and other Asian countries. The project area has an extensive forest cover that can be protected and managed by moving communities, especially subsistence farmers to the nearby areas. This will not only reduce the cost in the initial stages of the project but also facilitate the releasing of lands to communities under close monitoring of Community based Forestry Organizations and the Forest Department. In this way, a number of economic benefits could be extracted in systematic ways. These products could be coupled with the opportunities created by tourism

### E.6.3 Financial viability

301. Discounting rates: To obtain an idea of the actual or net contribution to the economy of Sri Lanka by the proposed project, it is necessary to discount future cash flows at a rate that reflects the risk associated with them. The identification of these discounting rates is that the general inflation in Sri Lanka fluctuate within the range of those two years (2015 and 2016) were 3% and 4%, respectively, while the average annual inflation was 7%. However, it is possible to keep the inflation rate at a healthy level to the economy; therefore, it is recommended that the discounting rates should be kept in the figure in single digits as per the standards of developed countries or rapidly developing countries.

302. The analysis delivered the Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) for the base case as 21%. The possibilities of cost increases for the input/project activities and deviations from the assumptions used in the computation of EIRR may occur due to factors such as inflation, exchange rate fluctuations, and estimated values, seasonal impact (weather and emergencies) and market supply and demand content etc.

303. To test the sensitivity, a perturbation of under 10% increase of base case value was introduced and yet the project generated an EIRR of 24%. The perturbation of over 10% increase in cost of base case value was also introduced primarily due to potential weak support from partners than expected, changes in external environment such as disasters, access barriers, lack of community participation etc. A 10% reduction of benefits was introduced. The project generated an EIRR of 23%.

304. In the worst-case scenario, where the cost increase and benefit reduction could be experienced simultaneously at a level of 10% on each side of the cost benefit computation, the project was able to deliver an EIRR of 15%.

305. The estimated costs are for six years and the benefits calculated are for 20 years. The Net Present Value of Benefits are higher than the NPV of costs. Therefore, it is observed that the proposed project has a very healthy economic analysis result and the investment risk is very minimal.

Please explain how best available technologies and practices are considered and applied. If applicable, specify the most innovative approaches that are made use of.

306. ICRIF and SLNCA will be introducing a series of climate smart agricultural practices, including agroforestry, Forest Landscape Restoration, land health monitoring dashboards, as well as innovation platforms such as SHARED. In addition, the project will build on a range of Sri Lankan innovators such as Analog Forestry. The following is a list of key innovations that will be introduced:

- Enhancing species diversity to reduce climate, biological and market vulnerability;
- Strengthening farmers' access to the best-available quality germplasm of priority climate-resilient species, varieties and cultivars that match local biophysical conditions and market demand;
- Developing individual and group tree nurseries to empower farmers to independently produce high quality seedlings for planting;
- Promoting intercropping with annual crops to improve overall system productivity;
- Providing best-available quality germplasm of priority climate-resilient species, varieties and cultivars that match local biophysical conditions and market demand; and
- Exploring the feasibility of drip irrigation for high-value tree crops to improve water use efficiency and increase tree vigour.

Landscape planning of estates to allocate land appropriately to intensive cultivation of export crops, subsistence agriculture, agroforestry, and other uses such as biodiversity conservation and water conservation, including the establishment of exemplar landscapes.

- Best-available quality germplasm of priority climate-resilient species, varieties and cultivars that match local biophysical conditions and market demand;
- Strengthening farmers' access to the best-available quality germplasm of priority climate-resilient species, varieties and cultivars that match local biophysical conditions and market demand; and
- Facilitation of cooperatives, value-chain innovation platforms, producer agribusiness and trade associations, including SHARED, to enhance market linkages.

The Stakeholder Approach to Risk Informed and Evidence Based Decision Making (SHARED) is a decision-making framework for the design and implementation of landscape restoration projects. It has been used in several development outcomes developed by ICRIF. It pays close attention to a) socio-ecological systems; b) creating spaces for dialogue, learning and innovation; c) building capacity for evidence-based decision making and scientific research; d) engaging and coordinating cross-sectoral relationships; e) the process of knowledge creation and its strategic use and accessibility; f) developing capacity for integration and synthesis across knowledge domains; and g) ensuring that all stakeholders are involved in the design, implementation and monitoring of projects. The relationship among social, environmental and economic dimensions is clearly taken into account within the context of landscape restoration.

Land restoration technologies. This includes an understanding of the constraints and barriers to the adoption of specific agroforestry practices and other land restoration technologies. It also includes a focus on how to design the incentive mechanisms and restoration pathways, such as in the use of multi-cropping in plantation crops is critical for the successful implementation of landscape restoration projects and other related projects and plans. These include Analog Forestry, Forest Landscape Restoration and Agriculture Architecture, as described above.

Information and Innovation platforms: Combining SHARED with the development of Dashboards tools in an "Offices of the Future" approach to provide a platform for evidence-based decision making on land use within the environment, thereby steering land management on to a sustainable "green" pathway. This will link to investment opportunities through the creation of a new business model that will be supported by private sector firms and service providers to PASA and others. Note that the platform will be executed in open source software thereby avoiding any intellectual property issues.

Land Degradation Surveillance Framework (LDF): Developed by ICRIF and successfully implemented in about 20 countries on 1 continents, this tool will use locally calibrated, global models (based on data from over 40,000 locations) to assess the extent of land degradation, including soil erosion, land cover change, tree species prevalence and vegetation degradation status. These data and spatial assessments will then be used to target interventions in areas where they are most needed. The LDF will also provide a platform for dialogue with stakeholders in Sri Lanka to enhance the evidence base for decisions, including prioritisation, land-use planning

## Methodology

### Identification of Benefits Streams:
The project consists of 18 activities. These activities are clustered into five benefit streams, which are again subdivided to 14 benefits (please refer the above diagram). The purpose of dividing these activities is to identify and quantify the various benefits that will be generated in terms of financial figures.

### Cost Stream:
The budget of the 18 project activities including GoSL and GCF contributions. This is the total cost of implementing all the activities over a period of time, which is the time period of the project. Life Span of Benefits: It is estimated that the life span of the benefits will be 20 years.

### Cost Benefit Analysis:
To determine whether the project is financially viable and to justify the investment in the project, the Internal Rate of Returns (IRR), three scenarios were used. They are calculating the IRR under the basic situation, calculating the IRR at a 10% increase of costs and finally calculating the IRR at a 10% decrease of benefits.

The assumptions made on cost benefit streams and rationale for the calculations are provided below.

### a. Planned interventions to improve land and water management to enhance area income

The landscape-level planning covering 4,100 ha less, 3,710 ha of which is in the upper catchment area, will be implemented by the country. This benefit involves Activity 2.2.

### b. Additional surface and ground water storage through tank rehabilitation (A)

Community-led partial desilting and rehabilitation of irrigation structures within the upper catchment area will improve access to irrigation for crops and livestock during dry spells in an area for both upstream and downstream communities, benefiting approximately 10,500 families.

### c. Value addition development and upgrade

GCF investments will generate an additional income to an estimated 32,000 farmers through the following activities: certification, quality assurance, processing, packaging, branding, and marketing of agricultural products in the upstream area. The "Jurisdictional Area Approach" will be used for the value addition process, with a focus on branding the products linked to agriculture, tourism, and related services.

### d. Area-based green growth opportunities

New approaches, technologies, and low-carbon practices will enhance the value of products and services in the upstream area and bring more resources into the hands of the people. This benefit is tied to Activity 2.1.

### e. Planned interventions to improve land and water management to enhance area income (cont.)

The main economic crops and other field crops are grown on a large scale in the upper catchment area of the country. There are fluctuations in the global market prices for these crops, which can be mitigated by diversifying the crop portfolio through GCF investments. This will help companies to eliminate the drawbacks of the current production system and identify suitable crops to increase with the potential to generate higher income from the same land.

### f. Area-based green growth opportunities (cont.)

The chain of issues related to upstream area development is linked to climate change & productivity – low quality of produce, high competition for low price – low income for farmers. This benefit will help to address these issues through high-quality production - crop intensification – new approaches, technologies and practices – new market approaches, etc., -- high global demand – high international prices – high economic benefits. The estimated value created is USD 10 million worth of marginal economic benefits.

For yearly cost and benefits estimates, kindly refer the chapter 8 of the project feasibility study report (Annexure 2)

### Computation of the Economic Viability of the Project

308. The project economic viability was computed (Please refer to the feasibility study) using the value of the investment as inputs and the benefits as outputs separately. Both GCF investment and GoSL co-financing were considered as investments/inputs. Similarly, the benefits were computed for the five different Benefit Streams as described earlier.

309. The computation used two discounting rates 6% and 10%, based on the prevailing Sri Lanka conditions. The rationale behind the utilization of these discounting rates is that the general inflation in Sri Lanka fluctuates within the range of those two values, i.e., in 2017 according to the Colombo Price Index prepared by the Central Bank of Sri Lanka, the annual rate of inflation was 5%. Therefore, it can be assumed that a 6% discounting level is a healthy level to the economy; therefore, the Government's fiscal policies will be aimed to keep the figure in single digits as per the standards of developed countries or rapidly developing countries.

310. The analysis derived that the Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) for the base case at 21%. The positive NPV and EIRR values indicate that the project is economically viable, and does not assume from the assumptions used in the computation of EIRR may occur due to difference between actual and estimated values, seasonal impacts (weather conditions), etc.

311. To test the sensitivity, a perturbation of under 10% increase of base case value was introduced and yet the project generated an EIRR of 18%. On the other hand, to test the sensitivity of potential changes on the benefit side, primarily due to potential weak support from partners than expected, changes in the external environment such as political instability, economic downturns, or low partner participation etc., a 10% reduction of benefits was introduced. The project still generated an EIRR of 18%.

312. In the worst-case scenario, where the cost increase and benefit reduction could be experienced simultaneously at a level of 10% on each side of the output, the computation project was able to deliver an EIRR of 15%.

The economic analysis for both costs and benefits calculated are for 20 years. The Net Present Value of Benefits is higher than the NPV of costs. Therefore, it is observed that the proposed project has a very healthy economic analysis result and the investment risk is very minimal.

# F.2. Technical Evaluation

## Key Drivers and Challenges

1. The key drivers related to the climate impacts have been identified clearly to develop the technical interventions under a multi-sectoral best management practices and support awareness, advisory, and information mechanisms by involving a range of Govt., Non-Govt., partners with different skillsets.
2. The key drivers contributing to the lack of adaptive capacity has been identified as the canopy related poor cloud capture efficiency, low soil moisture content, high evaporation rates, and increased runoff due to heavy rainfall events, all exacerbated by climate change such as erosion due to higher rainfall, salinity of ponds and tanks; land degradation due to land fragmentation, poverty and management issues etc. In addition the sustenance farmers lack of capacity to adapt to these changes.
3. The key drivers contributing to the lack of adaptive capacity have been identified as the canopy related poor cloud capture efficiency, low soil moisture content, high evaporation rates, and increased runoff due to heavy rainfall events, all exacerbated by climate change such as erosion due to higher rainfall, salinity of ponds and tanks; land degradation due to land fragmentation, poverty and management issues etc. In addition the sustenance farmers lack of capacity to adapt to these changes.
4. The project aims to support the communities to improve the efficiency of water, land and energy uses and engage in value added products, minimizing the pre-harvest waste such as fertilizer and water and post-harvest losses. While the project will also provide technical assistance through Farmer Field School (FFS) approaches to maintain infrastructure such as stream banks and roads etc. and benefits to the community through capacity building and awareness creation.
5. The three project components are designed to address different aspects of the resilience building for sustenance farmers in the upper catchment area and irrigated paddy areas downstream. The six output covers a diverse topic from climate change adaptation (1); water management (1:2); soil health (1:3); sustainable land management (1:4); sustainable agricultural production (1:2:3); value chains (2:1) and integrated rural advisory and knowledge management (1:2), not an order but all inter-woven during the planning and implementation.
6. Technically the project is a complete package that involve multiple stakeholders providing their best to add value to the climate resilience in a way that it is sustainable and replicable.

# F.3. Environmental, Social Assessment, Including Gender Considerations

## IUCN's ESMS

1. IUCN has established an Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) that is an integral part of IUCN’s project cycle management.
2. The ESMS is designed to ensure that the environmental and social performance of projects implemented or supported by IUCN, including those funded through GCF, are managed in a way that avoids, mitigates, and/or compensates for these impacts. It also ensures that the implementation and effectiveness of mitigation measures are monitored.
3. The ESMS and its procedures, four Standards and tools have been reviewed by the GCF accreditation panel and validated as being compliant with GCF requirements for analyzing low and medium risk projects.

## Project Completion

1. The project has completed the screening processes of the ESMS and has been classified as a moderate risk project (category B) based on the IUCN internorm Environmental and Social Policy—based on the Performance Standard 6 (PS-6) of the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The results of the ESMS screening are summarized below, the full ESMS Screening Report is attached (Annex 5).
