# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 124 OF 124

321. The aim of the project is to generate resilient livelihoods by increasing the capacity to adapt to climate induced changes in water availability and downstream river communities (DRCs) on Landscapes through restoration of the ecosystem services flow that connect them. This project includes activities around land management for irrigation agriculture, plantations and forest reserves (component 1), promotion of sustainable/green value chains and payment for ecosystem services (Component 2) and strengthening institutional capacity for land management (component 3).

322. Main ESMS relevant activities of component 1 include vegetation management to control run-off and enhance infiltration; soil conservation and water harvesting structures; the introduction of drought tolerant crops, including for rice production, increasing efficiency of irrigation, fertilizer and integrated pest control, promoting agroforestry, home gardens and analog forests through a menu of services including crop diversity, access to germplasm, nurseries, training and extension services. Component 2 includes the promotion of sustainable land management under-performing crops into food gardens, agroforestry practices including intercropping with high-value short rotation crops, and other value chains.

Under Component 2, the project will strengthen the capacity of farmers and collective groups as enterprises through advice and training in areas such as agri-processing, product development, branding, certification. Component 3 is ESMS relevant in the sense that it aims to promote inclusive, multi-stakeholder and evidence-based land – use planning processes.

The project is expected to have highly positive environmental impacts as restoration, reforestation and sustainable land management practices are expected to improve the biodiversity of natural land cover and increase carbon sequestration. The project is also expected to have significant socio-economic benefits which are expected to be highly positive as it will improve ecosystem services relevant for local communities including water, agriculture, food security, health and income generation opportunities for both smallholder farmers and users. However, some risk issues have been identified by the ESMS team and the findings presented in Annex A The Screening Report, main issues include:

- Potential loss of biodiversity due to the introduction of natural resources which might trigger livelihood impacts.
- Risks related to conservation actions fall disproportionately on disadvantaged or vulnerable individuals or groups and /or that such groups might be disadvantaged or discriminated with regards to access to land.
- Potential of insufficient attention to the needs of vulnerable groups when providing alternative livelihood options and/or training.
- Introduction of climate proofed tree and crop species might require the use of non-native species with low risk of developing invasive behavior.
- Potential for negative impacts related to agri-processing.
- Risks related to a potential use of pesticides when addressing pest infestation triggered by climate change. It is expected that some pesticide applications may cause significant adverse environmental and/or social impacts; most of the risks issues are preliminarily judged as low risk issues, very few as moderate, and it is expected that these risks will be mitigated through the implementation of appropriate mitigation measures. Because priority areas for interventions and further details of project activities will be defined only during the project’s design phase, a detailed land-use planning process, an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) has been developed.

The ESMS delineates the process of assessing risks and identifying suitable mitigation measures; spells out responsibilities and roles in implementing these measures. The ESMS also provides guidance on how to identify financial resources needed for ESMF implementation. It further specifies ESMS relevant socio-economic data to be collected during project design, implementation and monitoring phases as well as other topics identified in the section B and C of the questionnaire. The ESMS also provides detailed guidance for ensuring compliance with the ESMS standards. As such it includes elements of an Access Restrictions

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## Mitigation Process Frameworks (including SIA elements to be included in the social baseline analysis) and guidance on management needs for risk related to the introduction of new technologies and/or methodologies will be planned. It also specifies requirements for the project-level grievance mechanisms to be set-up at project start and which will dovetail with IUCN's overarching institution-wide ESMS Complaints Management System (CAMS).

### Gender Analysis

A Gender Analysis has been carried out that provides an overview of existing gender inequalities in Sri Lanka, with emphasis on the agricultural and natural resource sectors. It further identifies gender related issues that are likely to be encountered during project implementation as well as those areas where specific actions need to be implemented by the project. The assessment is based on key available information (listed in the references), observations made during field visits, and discussions with agriculture and natural resource professional working in Sri Lanka.

### Gender Analysis

The Gender Analysis will be further expanded during the project's inception phase through in-depth consultations and focus group discussions to be held in the project site with local communities and other relevant stakeholder groups. The objective of these consultations is to identify gender-related issues that need to be addressed to ensure that these consultations also serve to capture concerns and assess potential project risks on women and men.

### Gender Analysis

In rural Sri Lanka, women and men have different roles and responsibilities regarding agricultural and natural resource management. There are tasks that are shared by both genders, those that are dominated by women, and those that are dominated by man. This results in unique gender-based knowledge, experiences, and strategies for managing resources. To ensure the maximum success of project activities and to make sure these project activities achieve maximum success and impact, it is imperative to have equitable involvement, input and allocation of responsibilities between men and women at all levels of project implementation. It is important that both genders are involved in all activities. To ensure that women have equal access to project resources, services and activities the following approach will be applied:

- **Women's Empowerment:** Increase women’s representation and priorities from women community leaders and partners who are respected locally, by government agencies and other partners;
- **Joint Planning:** Jointly plan and implement all project activities and specific project priorities; and
- **Organize women's groups and women only activities.** It is necessary, to provide conditions conducive for women’s empowerment and participation in the project.

It should be noted that this approach will strengthen women’s involvement and knowledge, as well as, enhanced their confidence regarding their own knowledge of agricultural and natural resource management options, build their capacity through training and awareness raising sessions, and increase their visibility with public discussions.

### Project Activities

Project activities will have positive social and environmental impacts through the adoption of multi-species climate-resilient crop and water management systems, including adopting strip farming land technology and natural vegetative strips, among poor climate-vulnerable smallholder farmers in both the highlands and the lowlands. Compared to monocultures, multi-species systems diversify farmers' livelihoods, increase, reduce risk (including climate change) and improve biodiversity by incorporating multiple species and varieties on the same piece of land.

### Gender Action Plan

A draft Gender Action Plan has been developed which will be further enhanced during the inception phase in-depth consultations with local communities. The Gender Action Plan provides a time-bound framework to implement the project's activities and achieve the project’s outputs, results and outcomes. It is expected that the implementation of this plan should further contribute to reducing climate change-induced social, economic and environmental vulnerabilities.

### F.4. Financial Management and Procurement

#### 329. The Gender Action Plan also provides gender disaggregated targets, indicators and achievement against all project activities and outputs that contribute to the achievement of project results, outcome and impacts.

##### 330. The financial management between the As and EEs of the project will be governed by IUCN's financial manual and procurement procedures on procurement of Goods and Services. Further, IUCN comply with Swiss Accounting Law as well.

- **IUCN has a comprehensive procurement policy** (also available at https://confluence.iucn.org/display/IEP/IUCN+Procurement+Policy+and+Guidelines+as+of+1+3+February+2018) which outlines formal procurement standards and guidelines across each phase of the procurement process, and they apply these policies for all purposes of the project to ensure that IUCN obtains value for money from its procurement activities and that the procurement is carried out in a fair and cost-effective manner that respects sustainability, the environment and ethical principles.

- **In addition to the above policies**, delegation of authority plays an integral part of each policy. The policy manual is available at https://confluence.iucn.org/display/IEP/IUCN+Policy+Manuals?preview=%2F589029%2F160663%2FIUCN+Delegation+of+Authority+August2017.pdf

- **IUCN carried out the capacity assessments for all MIOE, ICRAF and IUCN Sri Lanka**, the three EEs associated with the capacity building project and satisfied with the outcomes as provided in the letter from IUCN Regional Director for Asia.

#### 332. Funds will be transferred to IUCN according to the Financial Master Agreement (FMA) and the Funded Action Plan (FAP). Under this FMA, IUCN Head Office Global Finance Unit will manage and disbursements to the Project Management Unit based on semi-annual work plans agreed by IUCN supervision team. The Project Management Unit is responsible for disbursing funds to the executing entities. IUCN is then responsible for transferring the funds to the executing entities and the services providers or suppliers that would have been contracted as selected. PMU will also responsible for the accounting and fiduciary management of all fund disbursed. Further, IUCN will be responsible for selecting the project management unit and the entities selected through a competitive bidding process where TDRs are approved by IUCN Global Finance Unit on a yearly basis.

# G.1 Risk Assessment Summary

The main environmental and social risk factors are:
- Potential need for short-term restrictions on the use of natural resources which might trigger livelihood impacts
- Social risks related to potentially perceived unfair treatment or discrimination with regards to access to productive land
- Potential of insufficient attention to the needs of vulnerable groups when providing alternative livelihood opportunities, particularly if these are not accepted by communities
- Introduction of climate proof free and crop species might require the use of non-native species with low risk of developing invasive behavior
- Risks related to pest infestation associated with post-inoculation
- Risks related to a potential use of pesticides when addressing pest infestation triggered by climate change

Technical/operational and financial risk factors are:
- Conflict between communities and governments or levels of government (local, provincial, and national) over land use and natural resource planning may hamper implementation. Instability, conflict, and/or tension in the area could also affect project implementation
- Stakeholders adopt patterns of corruption, collusion and nepotism, endangering the introduction of good governance practices
- A "project" mentality develops where local people expect monetary compensation to participate in activities that are not directly linked with their livelihood or other partners
- Partners maybe unwilling or unable to follow project planning and/or project reporting protocols

# G.2 Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Potential need for short-term restrictions on the use of natural resources which might trigger livelihood impacts | Social and Environmental/Social and Economic/Technical and Financial | Medium (5 - 20% of project value/Medium to high level of project value) | Medium/Medium |

### Mitigation Measure(1)

Reforestation of degraded lands in catchment areas will require restricting the access to those areas which are used by local communities for cattle grazing, firewood and other uses but is a driving deformation, until the vegetation is stable. It will only be during project implementation that the need of such restrictions is decided. The Environmental and Social Management Standard (ESM) provides relevant guidance on the use of the Environmental Evolutive Resettlement and Access Restrictions component of a Project Document(s)/provides guidance on assessing the risk to local communities and developing suitable mitigation measures to be agreed with project affected people or communities. It also provides guidance on how to manage risks associated with access restrictions. The role of co-management (shared management between communities and the respective government agency) hence sharing not only decision-making but also responsibility for implementation, monitoring and evaluation of the Standard's requirements are expected to reduce the probability of the risk occurring to low.

**Page 102**
---

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (PAGE 100 OF 124)

### Selected Risk Factor 2

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Social risks related to potentially unjustified preferential treatment or discrimination with regards to access to project benefits. | Social and environmental/Social and environmental | Low (<5% of project value) | Medium/Medium (≤10% of project value) |

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The project will adopt an inclusive and participatory planning approach when planning the details of project activities based on the stakeholder engagement strategy. Wherever possible, a range of support activities (e.g., training on agriculture practices, business services such as certification, quality assurance, processing and collective marketing) need to be selected, implemented and monitored in order to reduce the risk of unjustified preferential treatment or perceived/unintended discrimination. For some activities the project will use a first read, first served approach to identify beneficiaries who are best prepared to succeed; while at the same time assisting new or less developed groups to become better prepared for success.

### Selected Risk Factor 3

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Risks that impacts from conservation actions fall disproportionately on marginalized or vulnerable individuals or groups and / or that such groups might be unfairly disadvantaged with regards to access to project benefits. | Social and environmental/Social and environmental | Low (<5% of project value) | Medium/Medium (≤10% of project value) |

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The social baseline study carried out at the outset of the project will need to generate a comprehensive overview about the social diversity in the project site considering ethnicity, gender, religion, locality, language, class; and how social diversity is reflected in the land use. Marginalized or vulnerable individuals or groups in the project sites will be identified and the risk of conservation actions falling disproportionately on such groups will be assessed. Further guidance for ensuring that the needs of vulnerable groups are protected is given in the ESMP. This is expected to reduce the probability of the risk occurring to low.

# Risk Assessment and Management

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (PAGE 101 OF 124)

### Selected Risk Factor 4

#### Description
Risk related to tensions between ethnic groups, between the Tamil ethnic minorities and the Sinhalese majority.

#### Risk category
Social and environmental/Social and environmental

#### Level of impact
Low (<5% of project value)/Medium (5-20% of project value)

#### Probability of risk occurring
Medium/Medium

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
This is essentially an external risk where the project's influence is limited. However, the social baseline study needs to analyze risks related to ethnic diversity and tensions between ethnic groups. The risk of this occurring in the project's area of influence, including the risk of the aggravating the situation. Suitable strategies for mitigating needs to be identified.

Land use planning mechanism promoted under component 3 should ensure that ethnic minorities are appropriately represented on the multi-stakeholder platforms created by the project. This is expected to reduce the probability of the risk occurring to low.


### Selected Risk Factor 5

#### Description
Introduction of climatically limited tree and crop species might require the use of non-native species with low risk of developing invasive behaviour.

#### Risk category
Social and environmental/Social and environmental

#### Level of impact
Medium (1-20% of project value)/Medium (5-20% of project value)

#### Probability of risk occurring
Low/Low


### Selected Risk Factor 6

#### Description
Potential local environmental impacts related to ag-processing (e.g. waste or effluence issues).

#### Risk category
Social and environmental/Social and environmental

#### Level of impact
Low (<5% of project value)/Low (<5% of project value)

#### Probability of risk occurring
Low/Low


#### Mitigation Measure(s)
Capacity building and technology programmes of the project will introduce good industry practices to minimize waste using Cleaner Production principles; enhanced quality standards using GMP and other global standards and best practices. The project will also provide training on good record keeping so they themselves may compute the changes/benefits they achieved to minimize foot prints as well as to improve their own business. This is expected to reduce the probability of this risk occurring to low. Further guidance for avoiding risks from

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (PAGE 102 OF 124)

Selected Risk Factor 7

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Risks related to a potential use of pesticides when addressing pest infestation triggered by climate change | Social and environmental | Medium (5-1% of project value) | LowLow |

Mitigation Measure(s)

The project will not fund the actual application of pesticides or chemicals mostly on the work on vegetation and education side of pesticide use. E.g promotion of weed control through simple mechanical hand weeding instead herbicides, which is more environmentally friendly and sustainable. The reduction in the use of pesticides is expected to lead to a reduction of quantities of biocides applied by farmers and plantations. Nevertheless, as climate change may increase pest infestation, it will be necessary for the project team to monitor the situation and projects might need to advice on targeted application of pesticides. The adherence to the IUCN Guidance Note on Pest Management Planning as described in the ESMF will further reduce the low probability of the risk occurring.

Selected Risk Factor 8

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Conflict over project activities and resources may arise within and between communities and stakeholders, and between beneficiaries/stakeholders and project implementation. | Technical and operational/Technical and operational | Medium (5-1% of project value) | LowLow |

Mitigation Measure(s)

Initial project implementation will be focused in areas where communities are committed to the project outcomes and have a history of collaboration and conflict resolution. ICRWF will use its Stakeholder Approach to Risk Informed and Evidence-Based Decision Making (SA-RIDEM) to ensure that all stakeholders are aware of the project goals, expectations of the project and a vision of sharing benefits with all beneficiaries and stakeholders. Continuous monitoring and engagement in project areas will be enabled by the presence of project teams. These mitigation measures will further reduce the low probability of the risk occurring.

Selected Risk Factor 9

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Conflict between communities and governments or levels of government (local, provincial, and national) over land use and natural resource planning and management implementation. Inhibited conflict, and/or tensions may then constrain project implementation. | Technical and operational/Technical and operational | Medium (5-1% of project value) | Medium/Medium |

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## SELECTED RISK FACTOR 10

### Description
Stakeholders adopt patterns of corruption, collusion and nepotism, endangering the introduction of good governance practices.

### Risk Category
Financial/Financial

### Level of Impact
Medium (5.1-20% of project value)

### Probability of risk occurring
Medium/Medium

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The project will promote participatory and equitable practices. Project will conduct due diligence at all levels, ensures transparent fiscal management, and adheres to international procurement standards adopted and implemented by IUCN.

## SELECTED RISK FACTOR 11

### Description
A project mentality develops where local people expect immediate benefits from the project rather than activities. A similar mentality may develop with government or other partners.

### Risk Category
Financial/Financial

### Level of Impact
Medium (5.1-20% of project value)

### Probability of risk occurring
Medium/Medium

### Mitigation Measure(s)
An strong emphasis will be placed at the outset on communication and establishing an exit strategy that makes it clear to participants that this project is a step in a programmatic, transformative process and there is therefore a need to look beyond project outcomes. Secondly due attention will be paid to creating project 'ownership', particularly for individuals and organizations who are directly involved with the project. This will ensure that they have a vested interest in the project outcomes. Project staff will be few in number and project assets (offices, vehicles, etc.) will be modest. These mitigation measures will reduce the probability of the risk occurring to low.

## SELECTED RISK FACTOR 12

### Description
Partners may unwilling or unable to follow project planning and/or project reporting protocols.

### Risk Category
Technical and operational/Technical and operational

### Level of Impact
Low (<5% of project value, >0% of project value)

### Probability of risk occurring
Low/Low

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The project will be initiated with partners that are committed to the project outcomes and have experience in implementing similar projects. In addition, all partners will be engaged through performance-based annual agreements which focus on results delivery rather than inputs. The project will also establish a monitoring and evaluation framework for all activities. Results-based management will be the norm. The project will provide capacity building activities to partners regarding planning and reporting protocols. These mitigation measures will further reduce the probability of the risk occurring.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 104 OF 124

### Other Potential Risks in the Horizon

Risks that may emerge and need to be monitored, include:

- Project implementation may strain the capacity and resources of local government agencies.
    - Due to limited availability of local expertise, it may prove difficult to access qualified staff to effectively implement the project.
- The procurement of the supplies and materials required to implement activities and support partners may be difficult or expensive to procure, thus hampering project implementation.
- Trade-offs with private sector stakeholders may be reluctant to work with farmers in an equitable and transparent fashion.
- Off-farm income generating opportunities may reduce farmers' investment in management options promoted by the project, hampering activities under all three outcomes.

## Activities Description Sub-activities Deliverables

### 1.1. Streamside protection and erosion management along roads
These are conservation measures focused on streambank stabilization supporting action at both community and institutional levels. The intervention will be determined on the basis of:
* Vegetation management to reduce soil erosion and sediment inflow (this has been identified as a priority in the upper catchment of the project area)
There are 2,375 km of stream banks and roads within the upper catchment of the project area


---
**Page 118**
---

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (PAGE 115 OF 124)

### Sub-basin planning process in 3.1.1
- **Information portal:** A web-based information portal will be established and funded by the GCF in 2021.
- **Initial funding:** Initial funding will be provided to support the development of a sub-basin plan for each project intervention area, including initial catchment protection and improved water management practices on smallholder farms while the PES is gradually phased in as part of the overall project implementation.
- **Project completion:** Project completion will be defined by the achievement of water quality and control across the sub-basin implementation areas.

### To be selected for project interventions
- Drainage management along with other streamside interventions will be implemented by the Road and Bridge Authority in the Ministry of Agriculture.
- There is 7,368 km² of types of land use within the catchment to be selected for project interventions.
- The drainage management activities will be implemented by the Road and Bridge Authority in the Ministry of Highways and the Provincial Councils in the Provincial Councils.

### 1.2 Rehabilitation and establishment of village tanks, ponds and irrigation networks
- This will involve rehabilitation and development of village level ponds and irrigation systems to provide water for rainwater harvesting and tapping stream flow.
- Priority areas for intervention: water flow and quality, stream erosion and ground water recharge monitoring at GN level.

### Actions will focus on:
- Thematic mapping of areas suitable for practicing various types of agriculture such as village tanks and ponds.
- Construction and rehabilitation of dams and village ponds and the infrastructure connecting them to fields and agricultural lands by channeling water from roads in 1.1.3.
- Water flow, quality, stream erosion and ground water recharge monitoring at GN level.

### 2.0 Rehabilitation of forest mosaic landscapes
- This activity focuses on restoring degraded forests within protected areas and forest fragments in the catchment area for the supply of ecosystem services, especially carbon sequestration by planting trees outside forests for reforestation.
- The project will employ a Forest Landscape Approach to restore degraded areas adjoining protected areas.

### 3.0 Rehabilitation of natural vegetation and planting of diverse tree species on degraded lands
- Assisted natural regeneration of degraded forests.
- Restoring the ecosystem services provided by forested land through under story shrub and grassland planting with species more suitable for the environment and people's needs, such as eucalyptus that was planted in 1.1.3.

### 4.0 Implementation
- 41,911 ha dense forest
- 25,906 ha open forest
- 62,204 ha scrub (degraded grassland)

### Implementation will involve:
- Environmental Authority.
- Ministry of Environment, Department of Primary Industries, Wales.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (PAGE 117 OF 124)

### H

This activity focuses on development of smart farming technologies to grow crops and livestock more efficiently using less water and fertilizer, while also reducing waste and recycling, as well as employing sustainable practices. This will enhance soil health and pest resistance data collection and crop modeling.

The project will focus on the following key intervention areas:

- **Make better use of irrigation water (MIRW) through better irrigation management and technology choice**
  - Irrigation efficiency greater than or equal to 2.5 L/kg
  - Store, process, including drying and ensure quality to reduce post-harvest losses
  - Delay droughts (through enhanced knowledge of what is available in the watershed) and managing water issues

- **Integrate use of automatic weather stations to obtain real-time rainfall, temperature and wind data to improve decision-making and understand the scale and extent of drought conditions across the project area**

### T.2.1: Increasing irrigated rice in both upstream and downstream areas

This will focus on options for sustainable intensification of home gardens, enabling farmers to grow a wider range of horticultural crops in the upstream areas. This will be achieved through control distribution of water to maintain quality and agronomic management.

### T.2.2: Interfacing of Southern semi-arid production

The project will provide customized advice for enhancing rice productivity through improved irrigation, fertilizer use efficiency, and better inputs and water.
- Enhancing diversity species to reduce climate change impacts
- Strengthening farmers' capacity in quality promotion of priority crops, including vegetables and varieties that match the local market demand and requirements
- Developing individual and group enterprises to empower farmers to increase their yields of priority crops and achieve recommended

There are 28,457 ha of home gardens in the upper watershed area to be considered in the project area

### 2.1.1 Conduct Domestic Value Chain Mapping and Market Assessment for Smallholder and Subsistence Farmers

This involves the detailed gap-referenced mapping of existing agricultural value chain (e.g., from farm gate to retail fruits) to capture their current lengths, strengths, weaknesses, opportunities, and threats (horizontal links in input and output markets). It also includes a system of innovation (NDZ) as well as the institutional arrangements for the respective sub-national governance structures.

### 2.1.2 Provide Technical Support for Enterprises and Institutional Development to Exploit Green Growth Opportunities for Smallholder Farmers in the Upstream

This involves the development of capacity to operate farms and collective groups as businesses, including support to actors to set up soft institutions where needed (based on Activity 2.1.1).

### Subtasks and Deliverables will be identified and classified into:

- **Stimulate the development of new businesses and build partnerships with retail chains and service providers (e.g., logistics companies) to market their products**
- **Strong relationships with financial institutions as local banks**
- **Use in marketing and modern options to supply products**

### Establishes a model of regular market analysis and forecasting for the potential of green production and processing enterprises as well as products

Identification of all of Lanka's major agricultural value chains at national, regional and global level

### Directories/tables needed for green market development

- **Information on potential green market options including type of product (e.g., organic agriculture, tourism and other)**
- **Guidelines on opportunities and directions to engage in marketing activities for subsistence farmers**

### Opportunities for rent appropriation and cooperative achievements with regard to agricultural value chains

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (PAGE 121 OF 147)

### National Adaptation Plan (NAP)

Three integrated SHARED implementation processes covering three sub-basin information portals, regional multiactor information portals and basin-wide stakeholder engagement and planning mechanisms.

A financial and resource mechanism will be put in place to support the operation of the three sub-basin information portals consisting of Government, Project Implementing Agencies and other relevant actors.

A support platform for users will provide modern communications and other tools for basin-wide planning and implementation including a knowledge base for best practices.


## 3.1.1: Develop an integrated land-use policy and planning mechanism at sub-basin scale

To carry out this activity and ensure long term sustainability, three level governance structures (implementation teams) will be developed to manage the process of integrating across non-congruent boundaries (e.g., administrative, DSS ecological, hydrological, sub-catchment boundaries).

1. The five sub-catchments on the east side of the basin are: the Saba Ganga, Punta Gorda, Oya Tengara, Elmenteita Kamba East Strips), and parts of the lower part of the Laguta Ganga catchment (the rest is part of the Nairobii DS).
2. The five sub-catchments on the west side are: the Saba Ganga, Maua DS (the rest is part of 3 basin), and parts of the upper part of the Oya Tengara.
3. Intervention is anticipated to be lighter in the western sub-basin catchment.

2. The five sub-catchments including the Rattana Ganga catchment are: the Saba Ganga, Maua DS (the rest is part of the Nairobii DS), and parts of the upper part of the Oya Tengara.
3. The lower part of the Saba Ganga catchment is included in this sub-basin.
4. The National DS sub-catchment is included in this sub-basin.

5. The five sub-catchments including the Laguta Ganga catchment are: the Saba Ganga, Maua DS (the rest is part of Nairobii DS), and parts of the lower part of the Oya Tengara.
6. Five LIDSP baseline surveys with associated satellite imagery will be conducted.

The activity will also involve developing spatially explicit assessments of land-use change and vulnerability to inform development of effective adaptation options and strategies, including by providing a framework for monitoring of changes in land use and land cover and the information system will be key to support explicit interactions between basin function and ecosystem services at landscape degradation hotspots (e.g. soil erosion, water quality, biodiversity loss) or soil health variables (e.g. soil fertility, soil organic carbon). The data collected from the LIDSP will also be used in global databases.

### 3.1.2: Development and refinement of SLAM framework

This Activity will synthesize information to develop a general framework for adaptation, market information and policy analysis at basin scale (SLAM) with nested-scale multi-stakeholder engagement and planning mechanisms.

Collation of local and expert knowledge on climate change vulnerability and variation in contextual factors across the sub-basin catchments is recommended practices

Five LIDSP baseline surveys with associated satellite imagery will be conducted.

### Innovation platforms for collaboration and co-creation (30-60 week)

It will promote an organized set of innovative approaches to risk management tailored to the range of local contexts occurring on the project area.

#### 3.2.1: Establishment of nested scale multi-stakeholder innovation platforms from sub-basin to DN scale

A key element of resilience enhancement and environmental impact mitigation is the engagement of relevant and diverse stakeholders in co-creation and utilization of online dashboards and social media platforms for enhanced stakeholder interaction and interrogation where information flows between stakeholders interact with each other, as well as with the environment. Information/decisions in promoting sustainable development and environmental impacts is key to developing context-appropriate innovation platforms.

#### 3.2.2: Develop local capacity for adaptive and resilient land management

This activity addresses key capacity development needs of project managers to connect bottom-up methods that ensure the sustainability of the proposed land use advisory RAS model with top-down approaches, make them easily available. This requires strengthening the capacity of the land use advisory service that incorporates information on changing conditions in the short (such as outbreaks) and long term (such as long-term changes in species in the context of climate change).

### Training of project managers who will be responsible for implementing concepts and approaches developed under this activity over a one day face-to-face training session followed by online posted subsessions. The training will involve the executing partners: 1,2,3,4,5,6 as listed above.

The training will involve the following:

- Trainers/mentors
- Trainer events, requiring sufficient time for participants (with distance-learning support) to attend all events per year and access to long-term mentoring.

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal (Page 124 of 124)

### H.2 Arrangements for Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

#### 331. Project level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with the IUCN 2015 Monitoring and Evaluation Policy, Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between GCF and IUCN and the IUCN Access to Information Policy.

#### 332. Projects will establish a performance indicator framework for the performance indicators and report on the implementation progress of GCF project while it is being implemented. Performance indicators are defined in the Project Document and are aligned with the GCF Strategic Plan. The Framework links Time-bound sets of activities to a set of agreed adaptation results. IUCN maintains a standard list of technically well-informed indicators aligned to its donor core indicator framework. Indicators will be selected based on their relevance, reliability, and ease of measurement. Projects will use different project monitoring tools to ensure effective data collection and harmonized reporting across projects. The GCF Secretariat will provide guidance for the development of project information disclosure.

#### 333. A Project inception workshop will be held after the project document is signed by all pertinent parties and stakeholders to the project strategy and discuss any changes in the overall context that influence project implementation; b) discloses the roles and responsibilities of the project team members, including the GCF Secretariat; c) review the results framework, re-assess baselines as needed, and discuss reporting, monitoring and evaluation procedures and methodologies; d) agree on the annual report format and procedures and mandatory requirements, and agree on the arrangements for the annual audit; e) plan and agree on the project implementation schedule and milestones; f) prepare a draft inception report no later than one month after the inception workshop. The final inception report will be cleared by the National Steering Committee before submission to the GCF Secretariat.

#### 334. A Project Implementation Report (PIR) will be prepared for each year of project implementation. Each report is cleared by the National Steering Committee before submission to the GCF Secretariat. The Programme Director of the project, the IUCN Country Office, and the IUCN Regional Office will ensure that the indicators included in the project results framework are monitored annually with a focus on gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) and climate change adaptation progress. The annual implementation report will be shared with the National Steering Committee and other stakeholders to the implementation report. The final project implementation report, along with the terminal evaluation report, will serve as the final project report package.

#### 335. Project mid-term MTE (and final FE) evaluations will be commissioned and managed by the IUCN Planning, Monitoring, Evaluation and Risk Management Unit’s independent evaluation office. Independent evaluations are conducted in accordance with IUCN’s independent evaluation policy and guidelines for evaluations. The evaluation criteria are aligned to the GCF IEU Evaluation Criteria. The MTE will assess the project’s progress against its objectives and results framework; (2) the coherence of impact and sustainability; (3) gender equality and social inclusion dimensions, and (4) replication and scalability areas. The evaluation process will apply a process tracing methodology. Data collected during the evaluation will be used to inform the project management response which will be reviewed by the National Steering Committee. Evaluation reports

## Proposal name: Strengthening Climate Resilience of Subsistence Farmers and Agricultural Plantation Communities residing in the vulnerable river basins, watersheds areas and downstream of the Knuckles Mountain Range Catchment of Sri Lanka

### Accredited entity: International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN)

### Country(ies): Sri Lanka

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| --- | --- |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum IX, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations".

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

3. The “Strengthening climate resilience of subsistence farmers and agricultural plantation communities residing in the vulnerable river basins, watersheds areas and downstream of the Knuckles Mountain Range Catchment of Sri Lanka” project aims to strengthen the adaptive capacity of these communities.

# Component-by-component analysis

## Climate-resilient sustainable land management (Total cost: USD 21.72 million, GCF cost: USD 16.74 million)

### 7. This component aims to improve land and water management with two key outputs:

(a) Safeguarding the production of environmental goods and services in the upstream catchments through improved land use practices and climate-resilient agricultural systems. This includes providing farmers and other stakeholders with relevant information on climate change, land and water management practices to foster adaptive capacity that decreases water availability for downstream irrigation; and

(b) Increasing the productivity and climate resilience of primary production in both upland and lowland areas through improved land use practices: this will be achieved through supporting the local stakeholders and land users, including farmers, foresters


---
**Page 135**
---

### Proposed Interventions

The proposed interventions under this component take an integrated approach where the downstream catchment area is to benefit through interventions in the upstream catchment area by ensuring a continuum between upstream and downstream hydrological and ecosystem benefits.

#### Component 2: Secure financing mechanisms for sustainable land management (total cost: USD 1.6 million; GCF cost: USD 0.98 million)

This component focuses on sustaining improved land management through the establishment of payment for ecosystem services (PES) mechanisms, including upgrading green value chains with increased efficiency and linkages to new markets and establishing a payment for ecosystem services (PES) mechanism.

- The project will establish PES mechanisms to enhance the sustainable outcomes of interventions under Component 1 by establishing systems that create business cases and ensure profitability. A market analysis and regular market analysis will be conducted to provide the basis for value-chain upgrading options for the smallholder and subsistence farmers in the upstream catchment area.
- Through the establishment of a PES mechanism, financial incentives are expected to continue climate adaptive catchment management activities where needed, such as cooperatives; innovation platforms; producer, agri-business and trade associations; and other stakeholders.

#### Component 3: Strengthen institutional capacity (total cost: USD 12.7 million; GCF cost: USD 0.98 million)

This component aims to address the existing constraints in governance structure as well as the hydrological boundaries. Interventions will promote an integrated governance mechanism at the national, provincial, and departmental levels to support the development of land use policy, planning, and intervention options at the sub-basin level.

Under this component, the project aims to improve land-use policy and planning with increased institutional capacity for basin-wide management. Information will be shared through online dashboards to support the identification and implementation of best practices in land use and management.
- To this end, training guides, guidelines, and tools will be developed.

Project management (total cost: USD 2 million; GCF cost: USD 1.6 million)

GCF grant financing represents 77 percent of the total project management costs. The requested project management costs amount to 49.1 percent of the total grant requested from GCF.


---
**Page 136**
---

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium-High

- The proposed project has multiple impacts at the project sites and important additional benefits downstream of the project area.
- In the project area, current unsustainable land use practices will be made more sustainable through improved land-use planning and management and the adoption of land-use practices that favour the conservation of biodiversity. Improved land-use management practices favoured the clearing of trees on agricultural lands and mono-cropping of annual crops such as paddy rice and maize.
- The project will introduce a form of perennial land use practice based on indigenous tree species, which are more drought-resistant than annual crops grown below the highlands.
- The project will improve water use efficiency in the project area through improved irrigation systems and sedimentation in downstream reservoirs, and an increased streamflow for a longer period throughout the year.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

- The project area is characterised by large commercial estates – mostly tea plantations – and smallholder farmers who are predominantly engaged in subsistence agriculture.
- Land use practices that are geared towards maximising (removal of trees, upscaling), even though the geographical location of the project area is not conducive to climate change adaptation. The project will introduce a form of productive land-use based on the local ecosystem potential, reintroducing native trees and other perennials.
- This system of perennial land use has several benefits: it reduces soil erosion (increased infiltration) and runoff (reduced storm run-off) and a more diverse production system that reduces the risk of crop failure.

#### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

- The project will introduce analogue forestry as an integrated land-use system revolving around agroforestry practices, which are known to provide maximum ecosystem productivity yielding a variety of commercially interesting crops such as, vegetables, fruits, herbs, tubers, medicinal plants and other products.
- These systems have several benefits for the smallholder farmers: they can harvest smaller quantities at any one time; the economic return on these crops is higher than the ordinary annual crops and the exposure to catastrophic crop loss due to climate variability or pests (which may themselves be driven by climate change) is much reduced through the production of multiple crops.


---
**Page 137**
---

21. The project introduces a PES scheme that makes a sustainable flow of financial resources available to the project beneficiaries in the project area.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: Medium

22. Sri Lanka has experienced a remarkable economic development since the end of its civil war. GDP per capita has increased exponentially, and poverty levels have dropped significantly with this. That notwithstanding, Sri Lanka has limited fiscal space to undertake projects of this kind due to a high level of indebtedness.

#### 3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

24. The funding proposal aligns with all the national policies on climate change and the national strategy for sustainable development submitted to the Global Environment Facility Climate Change secretariat. During a country visit of the GCF Secretariat to Sri Lanka, the delegation was received by the President of Sri Lanka, also acting as Minister of Environment; the President has expressed his strong support for the project, which he considers essential as an ecological cornerstone for all other areas of the island.

25. The funding proposal was developed largely with national counterparts - the main intervention of agroforestry was developed by an internationally recognized Sri Lankan academic.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium-High

26. The project requires a substantial investment in the form of grants, but there are many beneficiaries, including in downstream project areas. The overall cost-effectiveness of the project is therefore high. The project will be implemented over a period of approximately 10 years.

The benefit streams result from the reduced costs as a result of erosion and sediment; increased water availability for irrigation; improved soil fertility; increased biodiversity and marketing of products, community forestry and tourism; and reduced consumption due to fewer droughts and floods. The total project budget is USD 25.30/tonne of carbon dioxide equivalent.

The output 1.1 is the reduction in costs caused by market failures and the provision of public goods. Output 2.2, the establishment of a PES scheme, provides a continuous source of income for the beneficiaries and ensures that they have an incentive to safeguard the investment. This output directly addresses market failures, and outputs 3.1, 3.3 and 3.2 have characteristics of public goods. Outputs 1.2 and 2.1 concern private enterprise development and are therefore not considered as public goods. However, they are instrumental in producing the public good of preserving the upstream catchment.

Further review of the economic analysis shows that the project meets all three of these instances of public good justifying the requested grant element for these activities.


### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards


---
**Page 138**
---

The AEU has completed a screening procedure and classified the project as having moderate risk. The Secretariat confirms this is equivalent to GCF’s environmental and social category B. The project aims to generate resilient livelihoods by increasing the adaptive capacity to climate change in communities living within and around the Knuckles mountain range areas in Sri Lanka. The activities are focused towards land management, organic farming, agroforestry, plantation of forest reserves; promotion of sustainable and green value chains; payment for ecosystem services; strengthening institutional capacity for land management.

The ESMP provides a framework for environmental and social management throughout the project’s development and operation stages to effectively and efficiently manage risks. The ESMP is based on the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) developed as the specific sites that have to be identified and developed in detail together with the relevant local-level stakeholders will only be decided during the project implementation stage. This document provides an overview of the project, including its objectives, key activities, and risks related to water resources, soil and forest areas. It has described the socio-economic context, including population distribution, livelihoods, land use patterns, and other relevant information.

The ESMP is provided in English and a Non-Technical Summary was disclosed in Sinhala and Tamil languages.

The ESMF provided an analysis of the relevant national policy and regulatory framework, including the relevant laws and regulations. It also identified the AEU’s GCF environmental and social safeguards. It also identified potential environmental and social risks associated with the project activities. These include increased erosion and sediment movement during streamside protection and drainage management along roads constructed for access to the project sites; construction of check dams, tanks, ponds and irrigation networks could also lead to loss of vegetation and top-soil causing erosion and subsequent sedimentation in water bodies; increased use of fertilizers and pesticides might increase soil salinity and reduce crop yields. The possible environmental and social risks associated with the project activities are being managed through a preliminary risk management plan. While landscape forest retention interventions could lead to some temporary displacement, this will be mitigated by providing alternative livelihoods for those affected.

The ESMF included procedures for identifying and managing environmental and social risks of sub-projects. This comprises having criteria that will guide in the site selection, reference baseline development, and project design. The AE undertook baseline surveys to identify the presence of Veddha people in the project area’s influence zone. The AEU will also take account the impacts to communities of any restrictions planned as part of the reforestation process and access arrangements.

The GCF Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan (GAP) are aligned with the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG5 on gender equality. The policy and action plan aim to ensure that women’s empowerment, voting and education rights are well-established in the country. The national policy and action plan for gender equality is based on international instruments for ensuring women's rights, which have been ratified through the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). The Ministry of Women and Child Affairs has established a Gender Policy Committee to oversee implementation.

The gender assessment also provides an analysis of gender issues in agriculture, natural resource management, and forest sector. In the early activities, it indicates that women have more access to land for agricultural production than men do. However, women seem to have less access to capital inputs, extension services and market information compared to men. Women are involved in tree planting but not in harvesting or collection of other high-value forest products while men collect non-timber forest products for household consumption.

Regarding activities related to forestry, men are more involved in timber harvesting and collection of other high-value forest products while women collect non-timber forest products for household consumption. Women play a greater role in monitoring, and they also participate in forest rehabilitation works. However, women have limited access to land for forest protection and therefore, in fact, have limited direct power in forest protection activities.

In relation to the gender norms and practices that influence women's participation in forestry, negative norms and practices, limited mobility and unequal share in household responsibilities are observed. Women face challenges such as lack of education, illiteracy, language barriers, for example, diminished access to water or driver conditions. The design of the project has mainstreamed gender issues across the various components. The mainstreamed activities have considered women's needs and interests by providing training on forest management practices as well as increasing opportunities for women to engage in other important forestry and agricultural activities that are not only beneficial to their livelihoods but also to communities and families. An additional and important expected benefit that the assessment points out is the empowerment of women through increased access to resources, education, and decision-making power within the forest sector.

positive nutritional returns to women’s enhanced engagement. The assessment clearly demonstrates these considerations.

which rights are to be further assessed. GCF provided the government of the Democratic Socialist Republic of Sri Lanka with a draft privileges and immunities agreement on 18 March 2016. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities in relation to any claims against the GCF or its entities, unless they are provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made subject to the following conditions: (a) The recipient has been granted protection against hijacking and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP124

## Proposal name: Strengthening Climate Resilience of Subsistence Farmers and Agro/Oral Plantation Communities in a regionally vulnerable area through the Knuckles Massif and downstream of the Knuckles Mountain Range Catchment of Sri Lanka

### Accredited entity: International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN)

### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: Medium

#### 1.1 The funding proposal is a small-scale project with a lifespan of six years that was developed and submitted to GCF for financial support by the Government of Sri Lanka together with the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN). The project aims to address a USD 39.78 million grant has been requested by the country from GCF for climate change adaptation activities, and to leverage USD 9.2 million in co-financing (10% of the total project cost) from other sources.

#### 1.2 This is the second submission from the AE elaborated based on the findings identified by the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) in a previous submission. The revised funding proposal has been developed with the aim to address the limitations and concerns raised on rainfall deficit; 14(6) – project site climate analysis focused on the trends of potential exportation of water from the Mahaweli catchment. Additional information and clarifications are provided in this submission. Improvements made are highlighted throughout the report.

#### 1.3 The proposal targets ecosystems (biodiversity and water resources) in the Knuckles Mountain Range Catchment (KMRC) and downstream areas within the Mahaweli catchment). According to the information provided in the funding proposal, the Knuckles region features a unique biodiversity and is considered one of Sri Lanka’s most important sites. This is reflected in the level of protection and international recognition that has been accorded at various times. The Knuckles Mountain Range Catchment (KMRC) is located within the boundaries of Horton Plains National Park (collectively referred to as the Central Highlands of Sri Lanka) was declared a Conservation Forest by the Department of Wildlife Conservation in 2010. Aside from its scenic beauty, the Knuckles region is a vital watershed in the Mahaweli drainage system. It comprises 30 percent of the forested waters of the Mahaweli catchment, a major source of water for Sri Lanka’s largest city Colombo and surrounding areas. The unique features make the Knuckles Conservation Forest an ideal site to introduce the IUCN Green Listing process.


---
**Page 147**
---

1. The feasibility study showed that two monsoon seasons determine the climate seasonality of Sri Lanka (the monsoon and inter-monsoon seasons). Sri Lanka’s territory is characterized by three climatic zones: “wet zone,” “dry zone” and “intermediate zone.” According to the feasibility study, a significant change in rainfall distribution between these regions has been observed in the period from 1960 to 2010, making the downstream catchment of the project area more vulnerable to water scarcity during the inter-monsoon season. This shifting of the climate zones will further aggravate the existing water deficit in the area.

   The feasibility study also showed that rainfall, temperature and wind are key climatic parameters impacting the territory of Sri Lanka, particularly the agriculture and water sectors. The project area is characterized by a high population density, high agricultural productivity, and industrial use, and electricity generation. Rainfall in the wet and intermediate climate zones of the country is stored in an array of reservoirs and delivered to the dry zone agricultural lands. The water collected from these reservoirs is used for irrigation purposes during the monsoon seasons.

   As regards the causes of salination, a concern was raised by the independent TAP during the first submission. Specifically, information about the potential share of climate change in increasing the salinity levels in water reservoirs and ineffective land management practices was lacking on the potential impact of planned measures (e.g., direct more effort and resources to land management practices such as soil conservation and improved irrigation systems). Such information would have an impact on proper planning of the project cost, especially with regard to future sustainability of the results, thereby increasing the impact of the funding proposal.

2. In the new submission, the AE provided additional information on soil erosion causes and reservoir salinity levels in Sri Lanka. The AE also provided additional information and experience from the adjacent Upper Mahaweli area for which more data are available (Yeldawala et al., 1987). This information is relevant to the project because it clearly separates the share of sediment yield attributable to more erosive rainfall (driven by climate change) as opposed to soil erodibility (influenced by land cover and human activities). As a result of this additional information, the AE has revised its estimates from 100% relative to rainfall erosivity to erodibility, indicating that between 56 and 67 percent of increased sediment yield is attributable to climate change. Given the limited data available for the spatial and temporal scale of the modeling with available data, it is not unreasonable to categorize this as a reasonable estimate of the impact of climate change.

3. The target activities of the funding proposal are: (1) land and water; (2) land degradation (erosion, leading to sedimentation); and (3) lack of water resources (in particular in the dry zone). The rationale for these three target areas is described in paragraph 73 of the funding proposal. In particular, the paragraph states:

   *The project makes interventions in the upstream area to help people adapt to climate change by improving their livelihoods through improved agricultural practices and infrastructure improvements. It also improves livelihoods of people in the upstream catchment (208,131 female and 183,759 male population) by maintaining the irrigation water supply to the downstream areas (264,000 female and 266,254 male) so that they can continue their livelihoods. The project also works with advice on climate resilient agriculture to ensure that efficient use is made of the water in downstream areas. The project will benefit a total population of 1,389,710 (490,604 female and 669,442 male) which could be enhanced by some population (409,508 female and 469,442 male). The project therefore has an overall reach of 672,510 ha and 1,343,216 people.*


---
**Page 148**
---

The Independent TAP is of the opinion that concentrating most of the activities in an upstream area might not be the most effective approach, taking into consideration that the downstream part of the catchment is much drier and has higher exposure to climate change. In its response to the Ministry of Environment’s request for a technical assessment, the Independent TAP notes that the sedimentation problem is a priority area decided by the government because the country has two major rivers with high sediment loads. The proposed measures are to be implemented through the Ministry of Mahaweli Development and Environment, and the World Bank funded project (Mahaweli Development Project). Both projects contribute mainly to the downstream catchment area, improving rice agro-ecosystem through more efficient use of irrigation water in rice cultivation and increasing the capacity for flood control and drought resistance.

The funding proposal has three components, and each has its subactivities. For increased transparency and clarity of the concept and intervention considered, the measures planned in the funding proposal at the subsector level are provided below:

(a) Comprehensive climate-resilient sustainable management of land and water (44 per cent GCF financing):

Subactivity 1.1.1: stress-boost protection and drainage management along roads. The objective is to reduce soil erosion, sedimentation, and flooding in the downstream area as the upper catchment from which the project intervention segments will be relieved.

Subactivity 1.1.2: rehabilitation and establishment of village tanks, ponds, and irrigation networks. The output of this subactivity includes 32 abandoned village tanks rehabilitated (40 ha) and new tanks established, increasing the irrigation water area by 685 ha in upstream areas.

Subactivity 1.1.3: restoration of forest mosaic landscapes. This activity focuses on the following: restoring degraded forests within protected areas and forest fragments; planting trees outside protected areas to improve ecosystem services, especially watershed protection; and the planting of trees outside forested areas (e.g., 20 ha of planted trees in a village tank). The objective is an open forest of 13,965 ha, and a scrubland (degraded grassland) of 64,204 ha will be rehabilitated. The project interventions will also improve the canopy structure, increase soil moisture retention, reduce runoff, enhance water interception and increased rain capture.

Subactivity 1.1.4: establishment of sustainable farming techniques to grow appropriate adapted varieties and make efficient use of irrigation water and fertilizer (maximizing use of organic inputs and recycling) as well as energy-efficient cooking stoves. The objective is a reduction in the disease incidence data (connected to the information system developed in subactivity 3.1.2) by at least 50 per cent, and an increase in the yield of crops both in the upstream and downstream catchments from 8.6 to 17 (a paddy of 12.5 kg/ha).

Subactivity 1.2.2: intercropping of sustainable smallholder production in the upstream catchment (home gardens, agroforests, *p* gardens and annual

## Component 3: institutional capacity strengthened (29 per cent of GCF financing)

### Subactivity 3.1.1: development of an integrated land-use policy and planning methodology for the three sub-basins

The project will develop a risk-informed and evidence-based decision-making methodology to be used for developing a basin-wide, multi-sectoral, and participatory land use planning process across non-congruent hydrological and administrative boundaries for three sub-basins. Stakeholder engagement processes include workshops aimed at land users and other stakeholders in the three sub-basins to support land-use development to enhance integrated, evidence-based land-use planning and policy-making.

### Subactivity 3.1.2: development of a shared information system to support land use planning

The project will develop an integrated land degradation surveillance framework baseline surveys with associated satellite image analysis and soil sampling for the three sub-basins (land cover/land use, land compaction) and soil variables (e.g., soil organic carbon). Soil data will be mapped as high-resolution layers in a shared information system to support the land degradation surveillance framework sites with data from the global database.

### Subactivity 3.1.3: development and refinement of a sustainable land management framework

This involves an iterative process of co-design and participatory engagement using the “core 4 framework” set out in the programme description in section C2).

#### Subactivity 3.2.1: establishment of nested-scale multi-stakeholder innovation platforms for each sub-basin

Each platform will be established with representatives from government, civil society and private sector actors at the location. Platforms will be linked across sub-basins to share information and learning in the Grama Niladhari level.

#### Subactivity 3.2.2: development of local capacity for adaptive and sustainable land management practices

This involves training managers, field officers, farmers and others who will adopt new practices; etc.

#### Subactivity 3.2.3: development and production of simple-to-use guidelines, manuals, and other materials to support the implementation of sustainable land management, sustainable intensification and value chain upgrading options

12. In a new submission the updated analysis (Annex 14b) has been provided by the AE. This submission includes an update on the results of monitoring carried out by 2058, which shows that, while the dry zone projects area will get drier in the key crop growing months of January and May (resulting in a reduction in water demand), there are corresponding increases in the moisture index in the upstream project area from August to October. This suggests that the proposed project area may not be able to fully compensate an increase in irrigation requirements if rainfall intensity on sedimentation is reduced and storage capacity is increased. ## Sustainable development potential: Scale: High

### Sri Lanka’s national adaptation plan identifies various adaptation options and performance indicators for the nine identified most vulnerable sectors.

This funding proposal directly contributes to the achievement of several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 15, Life on Land. This goal would probably be the one SDG which is focused on forests, desertification, and biodiversity. One of the target areas of this SDG is to ensure the conservation of mountain ecosystems, including their biodiversity.

The funding proposal aims to contribute several SDGs and their target areas:

- **SDG 15**: Life on Land: The funding proposal would probably be the one SDG which is focused on forests, desertification, and biodiversity. One of the target areas of this SDG is to ensure the conservation of mountain ecosystems, including their biodiversity.

*Water Evaluation and Planning* system is a user-friendly software tool that takes an integrated approach to water reservoir planning.

### Needs of the recipient

**Score: High**

1. Sri Lanka is facing a significant strain on its economy. Despite significant economic success, largely driven by reconstruction and increased consumption, there are deep regional disparities in wealth and well-being.
2. Loans and borrowing that financed the post-war infrastructure development have led to an unsustainable fiscal position, a balance of payments crisis, and an enormous debt burden that need to be addressed by new macroeconomic policies.
3. The key development priorities for Sri Lanka include poverty reduction, food security, and economic development.

#### 1.4 Short-term needs

- **Short term:** Therefore, the Government of Sri Lanka is committed to fund resilience-related investments through domestic financing, especially the incremental investment required to build the coping capacity of vulnerable, rural communities.
- **Longer term:** The country has prioritized adaptation initiatives that drive mitigation co-benefits. Restoration of forest mosaic landscapes is one of the planned adaptation activities, which can also contribute to biodiversity conservation and improved livelihoods.

#### 27.1 Focus areas

One of the focus areas of the funding proposal is sustainable water management practices for the central highlands and other marginal areas to minimize land degradation and improve food security in these regions.

20. For the agriculture sector, the NDC plans to re-demarcate the agro-ecological region maps of Sri Lanka with current and future climate patterns as well as identifying/recommending appropriate crops for different areas to reduce vulnerability.

21. It is the opinion of the independent TAP that to improve the effectiveness of the funding proposal, the focus should be shifted from re-demarcating agro-ecological zones to a more holistic approach. The funding proposal should be re-directed to re-demarcate agro-ecological zones and, at a minimum, assess climate change impacts on the productivity of traditional crops in Sri Lanka. Based on the results of this reassessment, the risks of the planned measures should be evaluated and recommendations made for the adoption of traditional crops or, alternatively, their replacement with other crops, should be issued.

The first funding proposal focused on rehabilitation and irrigation water systems in the dry zone (pp. 41-43). The second funding proposal focuses on climate-smart agriculture, namely "Strengthening the resilience of smallholder farmers in the Dry Zone to climate variability and extreme events through an integrated approach to water management" (FP0016).

### 1.5 Country ownership
Scale: High

The funding proposal was developed together with the Government of Sri Lanka, and the Government of Sri Lanka is responsible for the implementation of the funding, which will mainly contribute to activities 1.1.1, 1.1.2 and 3.2.2 as well as cost sharing during programme management.

The Ministry of Mahaweli Development and Environment of Sri Lanka is the executing agency for the funding proposal. Subactivities where different governmental structures are involved:

(a) Soil health and streamside management will be led by the Department of Agrarian Development (pp. 40-41), which contributes to activities 1.1.1.

(b) The Road Development Authority in the Ministry of Highways and the Provincial Roads Authority in the Provincial Councils will be directly involved in drainage management implementation, contributing to activity 3.2.2.

(c) The monitoring system on water flow and quality, stream erosion and groundwater recharge at the Grama Niladhari level will be established by the Department of Agrarian Development (pp. 40-41), which contributes to activities 1.1.1 and 3.2.2.

(d) Under subactivity 1.1.3 for tailoring biodiversity restoration approaches, the Forest Department, Central Environmental Authority, Ministry of Mahaweli Development and Environment, National Biodiversity Conservation Authority, Sri Lanka Wildlife Department, Ministry of Sustainable Development, Land Reform Commission, and Mahaweli Development and Environment along with the International Centre for Research in Agroforestry in Nairobi, Kenya.

(e) The Ministry of Mahaweli Development and Environment of Sri Lanka is responsible for activity 3.2.1 on water quality improvement in upstream areas as well as activities related to downstream areas. This activity directly contributes to the governmental programmes for improved water production (pp. 40-41 of the funding proposal include information on irrigation).

(f) The complete picture of the Government of Sri Lanka's participation and contribution to the implementation process is provided in the funding proposal (p. 54).

1.
