2. **1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium**
3. Based on the information provided in the previous submissions of this funding proposal, the independent expert panel (IEP) has reviewed the irrigation sector as part of the overall water demand supply side, mostly driven by upstream interventions. In addition to this clarification potential for increasing water use efficiency is limited due to the fact that there are plans to extend irrigation systems in this region by 20 per cent; consumption by industry is increasing and government policy requires a reduction in domestic water consumption from 150 litres per day per person (in order to be in compliance with National Drinking Water Policy).
4. Risks identified within the funding proposal are mainly related to coordination among government agencies, lack of clarity on roles and responsibilities for different stakeholders to engage in the required activities; lack of cooperation among farmers and forest authorities; lack of a clear understanding of the benefits of the project; lack of participation in training by key stakeholders. For these and most of the other risks presented in the sections “output, risks and assumptions” and “risk factors and mitigation measures” (respectively), there is no evidence that they have been adequately identified or are sufficiently treated. Most mitigation measures should be identified during the stakeholder consultation process. The IEP has also noted that the project does not clearly identify the needs of different stakeholder groups and does not clarify their roles and contributions to the planning, implementation and monitoring of the project. This is a risk in itself as poor implementation modalities/management as a risk mitigation measure is not reliable and would not be effective if it were implemented.
5. The IEP has also noted that there are no clear guidelines for the identification of the roles and contributions from each stakeholder, and their willingness to contribute to different activities. According to the response from the AE, this issue will be closed when discussions with stakeholders have been completed. However, given that only three years of implementation but elements of the preparatory process are already provided in the project document, it is not clear how this will be achieved.
6. The effectiveness of this proposal would be much improved if component 3 (institutional capacity strengthened) took into consideration the country’s priorities as identified by the AE. For example, the proposed institutional strengthening, particularly re-demarkating of agro-ecological zones according to the current and future climate patterns could significantly improve the project’s effectiveness in terms of its ability to promote climate resilient crops for each agro-climatic zone. At the very least, a reassessment of agro-climatic

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP considers the country's need for sustainable management of its water resources, which are under pressure from climate change, to be "high" and recommends approval of the funding proposal.

# Introduction

Climate change impact women and men differently. Women are more often hit by dramatic shifts in climatic conditions as in addition to working in the formal or informal sector women in rural Sri Lanka are responsible for water collection, cooking, cleaning, and other household chores that require a lot of firewood, water, etc. The burden of meeting those responsibilities increases substantially with various manifestations of climate change. Women are also more likely to be affected by poverty, in particular women-headed households. In addition, the gendered division of labor within the household and their role in their households and communities influences their knowledge and access to resources, both of which are key factors for adaptation.

There is ample evidence to demonstrate that climate policies and actions which fail to meaningfully address gender issues, pose risks undermining gender equality while jeopardizing efficiency and long-term sustainability. Gender inequality can also exacerbate the vulnerability of women and girls to poverty, inequality and environmental objectives. This makes the integration of gender a priority for the project.

The importance of gender in the Fund has summarized the five objectives of the Green Climate Fund's Gender Policy:

1. Ensures that through a gender-sensitive approach, the Fund will achieve greater and more sustainable climate change results, outcomes and impacts, in an efficient manner;
2. Ensures that women’s voices are heard and their needs and priorities are addressed by the Fund;
3. Addresses assessed potential project/pogram risks to women and men associated with adaptation and mitigation activities financed by the Fund;
4. Combines a gender perspective on the causes of climate change-induced social, economic and environmental vulnerabilities; and
5. Builds capacity for women’s participation in climate change.

Women, as well as men, can significantly contribute to combating climate change. Shifting the paradigm towards low-emission and climate-resilient development pathways, which is the Fund's mandate, requires a large number of individual and collective decisions by communities, institutions, and individual women and men. This is particularly true in Sri Lanka, where part of the paradigm shift sought through Fund investments (GCF, 2014; GCF, 2018).

The purpose of this document is to describe the findings of the gender analysis carried out during the development of the project and to present the gender action plan that has been developed based on the findings of the analysis.

The document sets off with a background chapter on IUCN's Gender Policy (chapter 2). The actual gender analysis is presented in chapter 3. It is divided into three sections and is based on literature review (listed in the reference section), interviews, focus group discussions, key informant interviews, consultations and discussions with agriculture and natural resource professionals. The first sections gives an overview of the context of women's participation in climate change adaptation and mitigation activities within economic and cultural rights and patterns, policies and institutions. The second section examines the main sectors relevant for the project with regards to the economic activities and role women play within each sector, and

2. IUCN Gender Policy

With a comprehensive rights-based framework underpinning its conservation and sustainable development efforts, IUCN recognizes that protecting and promoting women's rights and advancing gender equality, including through women's empowerment, are not only fundamental imperatives in their own right, but also essential for the success of IUCN’s mission. Integrating gender equality into IUCN’s conservation and sustainable development programming offers the potential significant to accelerate progress toward gender equality.

The promotion of gender equality and women's empowerment is integral to the achievement of IUCN’s mission and sustainable development, as well as fundamental to realizing human rights and social justice. IUCN has been a leader on gender and environment considerations for decades. Since 1984, women's issues are included in all IUCN resolutions. This has been reflected in the work of IUCN Members Assemblies and World Conservation Congresses and, in 1998, IUCN formally approved its first Gender Policy. The policy was revised in 2006 to reflect the growing body of evidence supportive of an enabling a gender-responsive approach to the implementation of IUCN’s Programme and project portfolio, including planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluating, and closure phases, as well as in related training, communication and advocacy efforts.

To operationalize this policy, IUCN Secretariat and Commissions are required to apply the following principles:

- i) Proactively and publicly champion gender equality and women's and girls' rights and empowerment as fundamental to the realization of human rights and as preconditions to achieving conservation and sustainable development goals.
- ii) Promote and demonstrate equitable and inclusive decision-making, at all levels, and throughout the project cycle, especially increasing the voice, participation, representation and leadership of women in IUCN’s work. This includes ensuring that women have equal access to information and benefit from resources, services and governance, regardless of anyone's background, age, race, sexual orientation or other identity.
- iii) Actively embrace knowledge systems that are curated by women across different societies, highlighting the importance of indigenous and local knowledge, practices, values and innovations of women and men for natural resource management.
- iv) Facilitate active and meaningful engagement of women and girls, who are often the most marginalized groups in society, with respect to social and environmental safeguards that require specific steps to ensure that impacts are understood, avoided or mitigated.


---
**Page 162**
---

minimised to every extent possible and agreed with affected people—such as indigenous women and girls—to ensure human rights standards are met.

Proposals must ensure equal access to use of, control over and benefits from resources, technology, knowledge and services for women and men, in all their diversity.

Follow the IUCN Gender Equality and Women Empowerment Policy Framework and Procedures, including but not limited to promoting women's powerful agency for change, as well as engaging men and boys as allies and partners so that all individuals understand, value and realise gender equality as a benefit for all.

IUCN has put in place an Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) which provides a systematic procedure to check IUCN projects for potential adverse environmental and social impacts to assure that the project is consistent with IUCN's policies on gender equality and women empowerment. The procedures for screening projects on social risks make explicit reference to:

- **risks of creating, exacerbating or aggravating existing gender-related inequalities,**  
- **risk of inadvertently discriminating against women or other groups based on gender, with regards to participation in project activities, the provision of goods or services and access to opportunities and benefits,**  
- **risk of reducing women's ability to use, develop and protect natural resources,** and  
- **risk of gender-based violence.**

The procedures for identifying and managing environmental and social risks of sub-projects are presented in the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF – see Annex 5) as the specific sites for field implementation of IUCN projects. Only those sub-projects that have a high potential to generate and upgraded value chains will only be decided during the project. Once only the sites are identified can the interventions be designed. The ESMS also provides guidance on how to identify, assess and manage local-level stakeholders identified for each of the site activity. It is the purpose of the ESMS is to serve as a tool to ensure that IUCN projects are consistent with IUCN's policies on gender equality and women empowerment. This includes identifying potential environmental and social impacts and, where risks have been identified, that impacts are avoided by design changes or measures have been put in place, in consultations with affected groups, for reducing or mitigating impacts.

### 3. Gender Analysis

#### a. Gender-Relevant Contextual Situation and Existing Gender Inequality in Sri Lanka

According to the Gender Inequality Index (GII), compared to other South Asian countries, Sri Lanka possesses one of the lowest degree of gender equality. Sri Lanka's Human Development Index (HDI) in 2013 was 0.750, with a gender inequality adjusted HDI of 0.661. In the same year the country's Gender Inequality Index (GII) was 0.489. The GII is an index that measures gender gaps in three dimensions: political participation and education), and labor market participation – was 0.383. This score denotes that inequality between women and men across those three broad social aspects is relatively low in Sri Lanka, however the country still has a long way to go before achieving gender parity (ADB and GIZ, 2015; UNDP, 2014; Ratnayake, undated).

* See EMS Manual available at <https://www.iucn.org/sites/dev/files/iucn_esms_manual.pdf>


---
**Page 163**
---

Women represent slightly over half (51.0%) of the Sri Lankan population. Sri Lanka has provided free universal education for the last 70 years, resulting in an increased life expectancy at birth: 76.93 years and 72.1 years for women and men respectively (in 2017). Universal healthcare has enabled the country to greatly reduce the prevalence of some health issues that was previously more common. HIV infection is very low (less than 1% in 2015) compared with other countries in South Asia. Maternal, neonatal, childhood births, postnatal care and immunization are all now standard. At the national level, female feticide and infanticide are not a problem; however, there is a significant gender gap at birth in some districts where male mortality rates are reported to be poorer and other disadvantaged areas. Additionally, the lower nutritional status of women has been linked with higher infant mortality rates in these poor and disadvantaged areas – most of which are rural agricultural areas (ADB and GIZ, 2015).

Women in Sri Lanka have had voting rights since the 1930s. Free schooling and related support for textbooks, uniforms, scholarship, subsidized transport and school meals have facilitated the achievement of gender parity at primary education level. The country has also achieved a high female literacy rate (87%) and graduation rate in secondary education, resulting in better performance on national examinations. This trend continues into higher education where 40-50% of students are women. In tertiary education, females represent 80% of those enrolled in arts; 50–70% in medicine, dentistry, veterinary science, agriculture, management, and commerce; and 20–50% in the computer, information and communication technology (ICT) areas. The country has a high female participation rate in tertiary education, which is conducive to Sri Lanka transitions to an information and service-based economy: strategies should be developed to increase women’s access to ICTs and to ensure that they are not left behind by technological change. This enabled some university graduate women to reach high levels of success in their professional fields (ADB and GIZ, 2015). Nevertheless, the country still has a long way to go.

While the legal and education rights of women are well established and strong in Sri Lanka, the legal status of women still requires improvement. The Women’s Rights Bill has not yet been approved by the Parliament. Although international instruments that assure women’s rights have been ratified, they are not yet incorporated into national law. Women continue to be discriminated against in terms of property rights, and personal laws remain entrenched. The main cause of gender inequality in Sri Lanka is the patriarchal nature of society which perpetuates a culture of male dominance and discrimination against women from girls. Women’s long exercised right to vote has not transformed into participation in the political process. Women are underrepresented at all levels of government. Women’s representation appears to be lower in elected local assemblies. This lack of women advocates in the legislature perpetuates the existing subordinate legal and cultural status of women by not challenging the common beliefs, perceptions and stereotypes (ADB and GIZ, 2015).

While in today's Sri Lanka can still be found largely patriarchal of the nuclear family unit of husband, wife and children, in rural areas it is very common that a few extended family members (e.g. uncles, aunts, cousins and grandparents) live with them. The female head of household is not uncommon as the head of the nuclear family, but also female-headed households are relatively frequent. According to the Department of Census and Statistics the latter is considered a household in which a female adult member is the only person who performs all or most of the responsibilities for the maintenance and welfare of the household by the other members of the household. Meeting the family's social responsibilities is given a high value, and it is generally the woman who performs the majority of duties in this regard (FAO; 2018).

Sri Lanka has experienced strong economic growth since the cessation of the civil war in 2009. The annual growth rate averaged 5.3% from 2009 to 2017, and 6.4% for the period 2010-2017. Despite this impressive growth, pockets of poverty continue to exist, with women not benefiting equally of this economic growth. The labour force participation rate is low at only 35%, which is one of the lowest in South Asia. Even professional women face horizontal and vertical barriers that limit their access to decision-making.


---
**Page 164**
---

## Common held beliefs, perceptions and stereotypes related to gender include:

a. women should be responsible for household management

b. women’s role is to take care of children

c. women should maintain and nurture relationships with friends and relatives

d. women are more emotional than men

e. women should ensure water is there for the family

f. women should not work outside the home, especially if they cannot work on certain jobs

g. women are more mentally resilient at a time of distress than men

h. women are more likely to be victims of violence than men

i. women are more likely to be victims of sexual harassment than men


Although this stereotyping is changing rapidly with more women taking up skilled positions with the help of technological advantages (for example, women as container operators in Colombo Harbour), women’s


---
**Page 165**
---

participation in politics and in decision-making processes is still low. These and other hindering factors have been largely attributed to the shift policy on women (2017)6. As an institutional measure for supporting and addressing gender matters the Ministry of Women and Child Affairs (MWCA) has been established as per the Gazette Extraordinary No. 1933 of 9th September 2015, it deserves being noted; though, that the MWCA is not a single ministry but rather an umbrella organization for all women-related departments under a variety of different names. The Sri Lanka Women’s Bureau, the National Committee on Women, the Department of Probation and Child Welfare, the Department of Social Services, the Department of Family Planning, and others are the main Departments and Statutory Institutions under the Ministry. The goal behind the establishment of Ministry is to ensure that all women-related departments work in a manner that is sensitive to the needs of women and children would work for their betterment to achieve this goal7.

In addition, number of progressive measures have been adopted by the Government to improve the safety and wellbeing of women8. The Government plans to develop new laws to prevent abuse of women and Children and to protect them from all forms of violence. The government is also taking steps to address fear in Sri Lanka and aims to enforce the laws effectively. A Technical Committee has been appointed to advise the government on the implementation of these laws. The committee will also consider the issue of consent and legal aspects of sexual relations of girls between the ages 16-18 years, focusing on social, legal and cultural issues. The committee is expected to submit its report by March 2017. A recommendation proposed at the forum was to strengthen the reproductive health education in schools.

A committee appointed to work on increasing women’s participation in politics recommended a number of proposals including the establishment of a Women’s Commission9. The government has given its approval for the amendments. A media programme has been launched as part of advocacy for promoting a stronger inclusion of women in politics10.


The Sri Lanka Women’s Bureau conducts programmes in key areas to empower female-headed families economically and socially, prevent gender-based violence and foster gender equality. Their work includes the following activities which are relevant for the project: home gardening programmes; a livelihood development programme (e.g. poultry farming); a microfinance programme; a women’s entrepreneurship development and market promotion (e.g. revolving credit scheme, trade fairs, business training). The field programmes are implemented at three levels of the division: district level, sub-district level and village level to implement field programmes including 24 Women’s Federations, which are active at the district level; and 310 at the sub-district level. The women’s federations are organized into a network of 500 branches. Branches are guided by the MWCA through the Women’s Bureau. Women’s Development Officers operating at the divisional level primarily conduct awareness-raising campaigns for women on a range of topics (e.g. livelihood environment, violence prevention, laws) and provide some amount of credit for self-employment (FAO, 2018).


### b. Women in Agriculture, Natural Resource Management and SME in Sri Lanka

As in most rural based local economies, Sri Lankan women and men have different roles and responsibilities with respect to agriculture and natural resource management. There are tasks that are shared by both genders, those that are dominated by women, and those that are dominated by men. This results in the evolution of unique gender-based knowledge, experiences, and strategies regarding resource management. The importance of recognizing these differences is critical for the development of equitable resource management from a balanced and informed basis, it is imperative to ensure equitable involvement of both genders in all aspects of agricultural production and project implementation11.

The following section of the assessment summarizes gender roles in sectors relevant to the key ecosystems:  
the proposed project will address - irrigated rice sector, annual horticultural systems, home gardens, livestock production, plantations and forest and natural resource management. Gender roles in rice production are similar to those in the irrigated rice sector. Women play a significant role in all these areas and for this project, this chapter also highlights women's role in the sector of small and medium industrial enterprises (SMEs) and their contribution to household income generation. This is consistent with stakeholder during project design. A general finding drawn from the consultation and also supported by FAO (2018b) is that while women are heavily engaged in agriculture, their contribution is not recognized, and they are empowered to improve their situation.

### Irrigated rice systems

Rice is the single most important crop in Sri Lanka, occupying approximately one-third of the country's arable land. Rice is important for both food security and income generation. Rice provides approximately half of the total calories consumed by the population and 40% of its protein intake (FAO, undated). Together, women and men are responsible for the land preparation, field clearing, field pond maintenance, sowing, transplanting, weeding, pest control, harvesting, threshing, transporting, and selling rice yields. Women also play a significant role in post-harvest activities such as drying, polishing, packaging, sales. Women make these important contributions to rice production, while also being responsible for household management and child caregiving. Sri Lanka is blessed with favorable growing conditions for rice with well-drained soils, adequate rainfall, and long daylight hours. Unfortunately, inadequate water supplies has reduced the 'uproot intensity' in most rice production areas of 1 or less. This results in under-utilization of land, low yields due to lack of family labor and lack of food security for subsistence farm families and communities (Rathnayake, undated).

### Annual horticultural systems

The climatic conditions of the Sri Lanka Highlands are conducive to the cultivation of a broad range of annual horticultural crops. The most commonly cultivated in these systems include kidney beans, beets, chilies, onions, green gram, cabbages, bitter gourd, pumpkin, tomato, okra, eggplant, Luffa, and long beans. These crops are grown for subsistence consumption as well as for sale to local markets or at the subsistence level. Each farm cultivated a diversity of crops under low input and rain-fed conditions; irrigation may be used if water is accessible. Crop selection is based on household preference and perceived market demand. The horticultural sector has been growing in importance due to its high profitability and sales in local markets. Gender roles in annual horticultural systems are similar to those in irrigated rice systems. Women play a significant role in all these areas and for this project, this chapter also highlights women's role in the sector of small and medium industrial enterprises (SMEs) and their contribution to household income generation. This is consistent with stakeholder during project design. As with rice production, women contribute to annual horticultural production while also being the primary household manager and family caregiver (Rathnayake, undated).

### Home gardens

Homegardens are diverse multiple-crop, intercropping systems that combines horticultural crops, perennial crops, livestock, and occasionally fish, in areas adjacent to rural homes. These systems produce multiple products and environmental services. The composition, structure and management of home gardens vary greatly depending on the socio-economic status of the household and the type of land use system. In a subsistence agricultural system, most home gardens has evolved into a commercial operation with products used for both home consumption and sale in local markets. Women play an important role in all aspects of home garden maintenance is part of the household work, giving women greater influence over the system. Generally, women are responsible for most management decisions and implementation, including species preference,


---
**Page 167**
---

# Livestock production

Livestock production is a substantial component of smallholder farming systems in Sri Lanka with animals such as buffaloes, cattle, buffaloes, pigs, goats, sheep and ducks. In particular, the poultry sector has experienced strong growth. While poultry production can be done within the household premises and managed by women, it is more often done on a larger scale by men. Women are involved in activities such as marketing eggs and broiler chickens. Livestock production is also carried out with the involvement of women engaged in small-scale backyard poultry production often based on buy-back agreements with poverty alleviation schemes. The livestock sector has been dominated by men, particularly in cattle raising which is mainly done by men who due to a number of underlying causes related to the cultural aspects of the patriarchal community, which is by nature patriarchal. In traditional extensive grazing systems, women are involved in activities such as collecting firewood and water for animals.

# Plantations

The plantation sector in Sri Lanka cultivates a large number globally traded perennial commodities, including tea, coconut, rubber, timber and spices - cardamom, cinnamon, pepper, cloves, and nutmeg. These crops are export commodities that make important contributions to the Sri Lankan economy. The plantation sector is dominated by foreign companies with a small proportion of local participation contributing to the sectors, but are paid lower wages, worked longer hours, and have minimal access to facilities. Women are employed in various roles within the plantation sector such as workers out of plantation laborers, further stressing these workers who has been already at the lowest level of the formal economy. The majority of women working on plantations are domestic servants and housewives. In an effort to improve their economic conditions, female laborers are seeking employment beyond the plantations, primarily as domestic servants within and outside the country (ADB and GIZ, 2015). However, those jobs are limited. Additional options are needed to provide attractive opportunities for plantation laborers, both women and men.

# Forest and natural resource management

Besides their significant role in agricultural production and household management, women also have a critical role in forest and natural resource management. Many of the household's basic needs (water, fuelwood, fruits, vegetables, spices and other non-timber forest products-NTFPs) are collected in or near forests. Women play an important role in protecting these resources from overexploitation, degradation and deforestation. Recognizing this fact, project managers should take steps to ensure meaningful participation of women in activities related to community forest and natural resource management.

Experience demonstrates that while men often organize and lead community-based forest management and protection groups, women are more likely to be involved as group members. Women share the role of forest monitoring with men, often conducting the monitoring task when collecting non-timber forest products (NTFPs). Women also play a significant role in protecting forests from fire, timber harvesting or for collecting other high-value forest products. Since women prioritize the NTFP and environment service roles of the forest, they feel a strong moral obligation to protect the forests and monitor


---
**Page 168**
---

## Story from: Sri Lanka Community Forest Program, Forest Department

Ms Thushala, [29], of Srilanka, grew up in 2006 and lives with her husband who works as a Carpenter, and their two sons - of ages 4 and 5. In a house which had been constructed after the marriage, they have a small garden where they grow vegetables for their own use. But Thushala's family did not receive CBO membership, as the husband was always going out of the village to work.

After a period of time, Thushala began attending the CBO meetings, also representing her father-in-law who had been a member in the past. She was elected as Secretary for the CBO and she did not want to accept the position as she had never worked in any positions before. However, she felt that it would be good if someone from her family could represent them on behalf of their family, she accepted the position and started her work as the Secretary in the CBO.

"When they nominated me, I did not think that I could do this, and it was not an easy task. But once I started working with the CBO, I found out that women can also be good at leadership roles up to Ordinary Level and I am a woman; like to keep everything in perfect order. And being Secretary of the CBO is a very important role as we have to manage all the activities using minutes, and the work pattern of the CBO. Now, as the CBO, we have teams and know how to work with each other. We are able to plan our activities well and we can also be able to show our work, and how we work in our village and also obtain loan facilities for the community."

"I feel that we - are women members are behind the success of this CBO," she finally said.

### Women play a greater role in forest rehabilitation

They dominate nursery and seedling production activities, including seed and other germplasm selection and collection; soil preparation; sowing and seedling production. They also carry out planting activities, such as tree planting and maintenance. Most men in nursery management are site preparation and soil/silt transportation. Women do not hesitate to take the lead role in forest rehabilitation activities because they have more time for these activities due to their availability and attention to detail. Enrichment planting activities are generally shared by women and men. Men conduct heavier sapor activities, site clearing, site preparation and seed/seedling transportations. The planting and maintenance of trees is usually done by women.

The design, development and management of farmers' woodlots (FWLs) is an important community forest activity that serves public environmental goals of landscape restoration and addresses farmers' livelihood objectives such as income generation and food security. FWLs are planted on degraded land or fallow sites. Community groups can secure a 25-30 year lease of degraded forest land; often Chena land, from the Forest Department for FWL development and management. The lease is renewable every five years after subsequent and management. Both genders share the role of intercropping FWLs with annual horticultural crops during the first 4-5 years. As with forest rehabilitation activities, women take the lead in seedling production and planting activities while men are more involved in site preparation and soil/silt transportation. Involvement in management decisions can undermine women's participation in FWLs. To increase the effectiveness and efficiency of FWL development and management, it is important that both genders participate from the beginning of activities to acknowledge the equitable involvement of both women and men. Selection of species, land preparation and planting should be shared by women and men to assure all priorities are addressed (Forest Department 2016).

# Story from: Sri Lanka Community Forestry Program, Forest Department

One of the notable activities of the Community Forestry Program of the Forest Department is the Farmers Woodlots (FWLs), and there had been 19 members [27male +12female] were given FWL. Among the FWL receivers, who are randomly selected from the community, 50% of them are women. The farmers have encroached on their own land in an irrigation tank reservoir and very recently obtained a piece of land from the government. All have encroached around two and half acres of forest land and each one has earned over LKR 36,000 per year from the FWL by planting trees and selling timber. The women farmers are also involved in the harvesting and marketing of timber with the consent and encouragement of their husbands.

The most significant finding was that the women who have been given FWLs are from a landless family and lives in an irrigation tank reservoir, and had lost a few LKR 13,000 to the year 2012 on forest land encroachment. Sonal while trying to get her FWL, she was beaten up by the police. She is now a member of the Forest Department's women group, she ran into the thick forests but got caught in the police net, and her husband was produced before the court and fined LKR 15,000. Receiving an FWL has changed their lives; they are no longer in fear of arrest, but also has a piece of land for cultivation for a long time.

In addition to this, there were two women who own FWLs on their name had not pay fee to the court for encroachment and both paid fine LKR 10,000 in 2007 and another had paid LKR 35,000 in 2012.

## Small and Medium Enterprises (SME)

Small and Medium Enterprises can create employment but also offer income to the business owner. It is estimated that about 80 percent of the economy in Sri Lanka is driven by SMEs out of which women led SMEs are estimated to account for only about 10 percent. It is further estimated that a majority of women entrepreneurs are engaged in small scale enterprises and most of them are involved in informal sector, with a particular focus on food processing and textile (Atthigalle, 2014). SME development and in particular entrepreneurship among women has been identified as an important area for policy intervention. The initiatives linked to tourism are expected to provide important income opportunities for women in rural Sri Lanka, both as entrepreneurs and employees.

The project area is located in the Knuckles Mountain Range. It includes 15 key sub-catchment (watershed) areas as described in the feasibility study report (2013). The baseline information related to gender has been provided below.

The feasibility study had examined the biophysical characteristics and vulnerability situation of the catchment area, including the socio-economic conditions of the communities living within the catchment sub-projects for achieving improved land and water management, enhanced primary production and upgraded value through sustainable forest products. However, further in-depth assessment is required to determine the sites other sub-projects are demand driven and as such will depend on the interest articulated by the relevant stakeholders. Because of this, in-depth baseline information related to gender has not been done after the sites have been selected and the baseline data presented below is only relatively cursory.

It is critical to understand that employment does not necessarily prevent poverty as poverty can also manifest if the income derived from the employment is not sufficient for satisfying basic needs. The Department of Census and Statistics defines a person as being in poverty when their monthly per capita expenditure to official poverty line (OPL)1)2). If the per capita monthly real expenditure is less than the value of the official poverty line, then that individual is considered to be in poverty. From 2002 to 2016, the long-term national poverty rate has been around 35%. However, despite the overall reduction in poverty disparities still exist across the provinces and districts as well as between gender groups. This is for instance the case for labourers in the sector asector as the average income of estate sector households

There are significant disparities between women and men in terms of poverty. The main differences in the poverty situation for women and men are associated with gender inequality in wages for similar work, differences in the estimated earned income per capita for women versus men and the estimated female-headed households being more likely to be poor than male-headed households. Because of these disparities in income, female-headed households are more likely affected by poverty, by food insecurity and lack of access to basic services such as water supply, sanitation and electricity (Department of Census and Statistics, 2017).

The most significant gender indicators relevant in this context are:

a. Provision of water to households will be mostly a women responsibility in Sri Lanka context and shortage of water will adds to the time taken to fetch the water as the tanks and other natural sources dry up.

b. When the income is low due to climate and other impacts the family nutrition suffers as indicated by the data from both urban and rural areas. They choose to feed themselves last. Women have reported loss of appetite (over 36%) after disasters with compared to less than 30% in men.

# Gender-Sensitive Project Design

## a. Integrating a Gender Perspective into Project Activities

In order to ensure the project to be gender-sensitive and to effectively contribute to reducing gender gaps in terms of climate change-induced social, economic and environmental vulnerabilities, the project has been designed with a gender perspective that is reflected in all aspects of the project design including the respective project activities. However, not all activities present similar relevance for gender mainstreaming at the same level or extent.

It should be noted that the selection of sites and the detailed design of the field interventions will take place during the implementation phase of the project. It is important to ensure that the project team ensures that sufficient time is allocated for the detailed design of the interventions to enable meaningful and culturally appropriate engagement of diverse stakeholders and beneficiaries in this process – women and men. This will be achieved by ensuring that gender equality and empowerment principles are mainstreamed so that activities are designed in a way that women and men have equal access to project services and benefits.

The activities where gender mainstreaming plays a key role are described below and the project’s strategies for gender-sensitive design are illustrated in the green boxes. The numbering reflects how the activities appear in the project proposal.


---
**Page 180**
---

Activity 1.1.2: Rehabilitation and establishment of village tanks, ponds and irrigation networks

Approximately 100 village level ponds and irrigation channels will be rehabilitated or established including both direct rainwater harvesting and tapping stream networks. The feasibility study had examined the biophysical characteristics and vulnerability situation of the catchment areas, but the final selection of sites and the prioritization for rehabilitation and establishment is yet to be done. This is because these interventions require further in-depth assessment and prioritization to determine the sites. Priority to be given will be to those villages where there are existing tanks/ponds and irrigation channels that can be rehabilitated or established at a low cost. The objective of this activity is to establish an efficient, sustainable management systems, degree of community participation, command area to be cultivated as well as an estimation of costs and benefits.

A GIS-based participatory approach will be used to develop thematic maps for prioritizing interventions in the target upstream areas through multi-criteria analysis. The tool employs basic data including administrative and infrastructural information, land use and soil information (land use, land cover, geology, etc.), climate information (temperature, rainfall, evaporation), topography (elevation, slope, aspect), hydrology (flow direction, connectivity, hydrogeology) and will quantify rainwater harvesting potentials across the target upstream area in order to guide the prioritization of interventions. The multi-criteria analysis is a decision support method that considers relevant biophysical and socioeconomic parameters, the land with the best capability for agricultural production, and the most appropriate location for the intervention based on the condition within the landscape/watershed. The entire process is undertaken in full consultation with land users, local authorities and other stakeholders, men and women.

The prioritization of interventions at village tank/ponds and irrigation structures will be informed not only by biophysical information but also by socio-economic parameter including current agricultural use of land (e.g. crop or livestock). The consultation process will be set up in a way that equal opportunities for participation are provided to all stakeholders, including women. In particular, the need to ensure water demand is given equal consideration. In order to guarantee that the village tanks/ponds and irrigation structures are sustainable over time, it is important to consider the role of local communities in the use of these infrastructures by forming new management structures or advising on existing community-based organizations. The involvement of women in the design and implementation process as well as the inclusion of women in the governance of communal infrastructure is an effective means not only for ensuring gender priorities to be taken into account but also for increasing the permanence and sustainability of the systems. The project will include additional support measures to empower women and youth leadership in these community organizations.

1.1.3: Restoration of forest mosaic landscapes

This activity focuses on restoring degraded forests within protected areas and forest fragments, re-growing forests in priority areas in the supply of ecosystem services, especially watershed protection, and planting of trees for timber production. The project will also include the establishment of a Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR) approach in the forest mosaic landscape areas adjoining protected areas.

Key actions will include:

- Assisted natural regeneration and plating of diverse tree assemblages to restore degraded forests
- Enhancing the ecosystem services provision of existing timber plantations through industrial rehabilitation and under-planting with species more suited to the local environment and people's needs than the pines and eucalyptus that were planted previously.
- Restoring forest mosaic landscapes by re-establishing native tree species in deforested restoration areas
- Ensuring forest resilience through the use of climate profiling in selection of species and genetic compositions of any plantings.


---
**Page 181**
---

Empowering women in the forestry sector and formalizing their role, especially in a community forestry program, is seen as a strategic opportunity for strengthening women’s economic role in the upper watershed of the Kudremukh National Park. This project will be implemented through a number of other programs (e.g., Sri Lanka Community Forest Program, SLCFP), where women realized tangible benefits from forest resources and were able to participate actively in decision-making processes.

Initially these payments will be ensured by the project, but with this time will be supported via a Payment for Ecosystem Services (PES) mechanism. The PES mechanism is expected to ensure that the project will ensure the needs of women are integrated in the restoration strategies to be supported by the project. This will be achieved through the development and implementation of forest management plans and management of farmer’s woodlots (FWLs). This will ensure that women’s livelihood objectives in terms of priority species (both annual and perennial) for household use and market sales are taken into consideration, while also ensuring that the restoration strategies take into account the consideration of women's preferences for trees that can be harvested for non-timber forest products (NTFP).

### 1.2.1: Increasing cropping intensity of irrigated rice in both upstream and downstream areas

This activity focuses on the development of smart farming techniques to grow appropriate climate adapted varieties, and make efficient use of irrigation water and fertilizer (maximizing use of organic inputs and recycling) as well as employing integrated pest control based on real time weather and pest incidence data (connected to the information system developed in 3.1.2).

Because of the high evapotranspiration rate (ET), frequent rainfall, frequent flooding (FF) and continuous flooding irrigation (CI) connected to high water loss, the project will promote water saving irrigation methods and improve water use efficiency (WUE). The integrated farming (IF) and CI system in this project is a prototype for a yield increasing system that is being utilized by more than 10 million smallholder farmers over in 50 countries. The innovation combines several agronomic practices to boost yields while reducing the use of purchased inputs and water.

The project will also provide customized advice for enhancing rice productivity through use of appropriate varieties for each context, storage and processing, maintaining quality as well as supporting the enhanced production of non-rice income generating crops.

Due to prolonged dry spells and inefficient water for irrigation, farmers in the project area hardly cultivate two seasons per year, resulting in considerable pressure on family income and food security. Field consultations with women farmers revealed that they are more vulnerable than men because they live in rural areas and that the lack of income opportunities is an important trigger for women migrating to seek employment elsewhere. The project will provide training and support to improve their livelihoods which will enhance the economic conditions of rural households, increase food security and reduce the economic pressure on the woman. The project will further strengthen women’s economic position by supporting them in activities such as crop diversification, improved post-harvest handling, storage, transportation, processing, harvesting and farm gate sales but by increasing their productivity and business skills.

### 1.2.2: Intensification of Sustainable smallholder production

This activity will focus on options for sustainable intensification of home gardens, analog forests, spice gardens and annual horticultural crops in the upstream catchment. Interventions include climate proofing of tree species and varieties to withstand the effects of climate change while ensuring that they provide both quality and agronomic management.

Home gardens are species-rich, tree-based systems using land immediately surrounding or adjacent to a household for production. They produce a diverse array of fruits, vegetables, and other products from perennial and annual species. Tree components of these systems yield fruits, vegetables, medicines,

24


---
**Page 182**
---

### 1.2.3: Restoration and intensification of sustainable plantations

This intervention will focus on cocoa, rubber, timber and coconut scale cultivation of spices and includes landscape restoration at the level of the entire food forest to address food security for estate worker families, some of whom have entered contract-farming arrangements with estate companies.

The activity will target both the plantation sector and plantation workers by offering a menu of management practices that can be adopted on individual plantations. These include: (i) improving soil fertility through crop rotations and intercropping plants into food gardens of nutritious and high-value superfood species that target local markets; (ii) increasing yields, productivity and profitability through the introduction and development of agri-processing technology for post-harvest value-adding treatment that targets the production of higher-value green economy markets.

The objective is to contribute towards strengthening their food security and livelihoods by establishing food forest production systems on land allocated to the workers by the plantation companies where they are employed. The project will promote the establishment of diverse multispecies systems that combine timber, fruit trees, spices and other crops with livestock and aquaculture.

As pointed out in chapter 2, women play a significant role in the plantation sector, in particular in the production of tea, rubber and coconut crops and especially in Malawi. However, due to the low wage levels, poverty, lack of access to land and credit, and gender-based discrimination, women are often marginalized. Recognizing that plantation companies should allocate underutilized land to plantation workers, in particular to female-headed

# Activity 2.1.2 Enterprise and institutional development to exploit green growth opportunities

## 2.1.2.1 Identification in the upscaling and Activity 2.1.3 Identification and implementation of value chain upgrades options for smallholder farmers engaged in climate smart agriculture

These activities involve the development of capacity to operate farms and collective groups as business enterprises, including the identification of new markets (producer-and business-users) and the upgrading of value chains with respect to products, processes and new (green) technologies. The goal is to identify bottlenecks and constraints that need to be addressed in order to increase the quality and effectiveness of protected area management and to stimulate the development of new businesses and business relationships to exploit green growth opportunities.

Given the site's outstanding natural values, which are reflected in a high level of protection and international recognition of Knuckles Conservation Forest (together with the Peak Wilderness Protected Area and Horton Plains National Park), the project will focus on increasing the quality and effectiveness of protected area management and provide additional values to the communities including from increased market recognition of the area as tourism destination.

The starting point for these activities is the mapping of existing agricultural value chains (cash crops, spices, herbs and other minor products) in the project area. The project will also map the constraints that operate within the value chain which will be identified as well as opportunities mapped out for the development of new SMEs that can be established by smallholder farmers to exploit green growth opportunities.

The project will devise capacity-building measures tailored to the aspirations, needs and capacities of women and men in agriculture-based industries. The project will also focus on increasing the role of women in enhancing the role of women in agriculture-based industries is developing their entry-level entrepreneurial skills and providing them with access to new markets and value-added products, as well as skill opportunities as well as their skills in marketing. Support measures will focus on opportunities linked to innovative green markets and on developing value-added products, including establishing linkages with the tourism sector.

## b. Gender risks and opportunities

### Site-level gender analysis

The project preparation phase has already provided first opportunities to study the socio-economic conditions of men and women in Sri Lanka and in the project area, the relationships between men and women, access to resources, their activities, and the constraints they face relative to each other as well as development priorities. The rapid baseline analysis will focus on the field level indicators for achieving improved land and water management, enhanced primary production and upgraded value chains are defined by gender. This will provide a basis for identifying gender risks and opportunities.

This will happen as part of the rapid social baseline analysis planned for each selected site and involve in-depth consultations and focus group discussions with local communities and other relevant stakeholders. The rapid baseline analysis is expected to be completed within 12 weeks from the start date, a sample template outline of the topics covered by the rapid analysis is provided in Annex 10.3 of the ESMP.


---
**Page 184**
---

This site-level analysis will enable an in-depth understanding of the key socio-economic and cultural features of the target location, including prevailing livelihood patterns and sources of income, dependent on natural resources, differences in capabilities, know-how and access to resources as well as risks and challenges faced by social groups, including issues of discrimination and marginalization. The data will also be used for identifying gender-based vulnerabilities, such as unequal distribution of power, on rights and power (including influence on decision making) as well as access to and control over resources. Specific attention is given to female-headed households.

Having applied a gender lens in the analysis, it was critical when designing the field interventions to ensure they are gender-responsive and, wherever possible, gender-transformative, and that potentials for the prevention of violence against women and girls (VAWG) and their negative impacts avoided. The latter is the focus of the ESMIS screening process further elaborated in this document.

### i. Access to technology and extension services

According to FAO (2018), there is a significant gap between men and women with regard to access to agricultural technologies. It is noted that while women have a lower level of farming knowledge and complexities used who use technology. There are advisory and support systems in agriculture implemented by the Ministry of Agriculture through the Department Agriculture (DCA) and the Department of Agrarian Development (DAD). The DCA has offices at district level, which are responsible for extension services. However, coverage of extension services is not sufficient as the range instructors must cover too large an area. The DAD has a similar structure to that of the DCA with offices in each district and a bank, which is run by the DAD, has some programmes that target women; these programmes do not ensure equitable access to technical knowledge, capital, and subsidies (FAO, 2018).

Another challenge is the promotion ranking on the long-standing former Extension programme, which is managed by the Provincial Agriculture Department and implemented by 'subject matter officers' at the district level. It generally includes training in agricultural processing and entrepreneurship. As a result of this system, women are discouraged from starting their own businesses due to lack of access to sufficient size and geographical range.

The project will capitalize on the experience of the latter program and ensure that services and training provided by the DAD are gender-sensitive. This is ensuring that the delivery of service is geared toward capacity needs experienced by women, respect the cultural conditions of women and attentive of restrictions placed on women's participation in agriculture. These include: a) time constraints due to day-to-day household work not allowing time for practice; b) certain ethnic practices that discourage women to participate (e.g., in the case of the Moors); and c) age and mobility restrictions.

### ii. Participation of women and men in decision-making processes

The participation of women at all levels of government in Sri Lanka, including boys and withholds opportunities for women and girls is a serious challenge for women empowerment and achieving greater representation of women in institutions across the political and economic sphere. The project will address these challenges by promoting women's leadership through capacity building programmes, training and informal local institutions and promoting leadership of women in the institutions' governance structures.

This includes strengthening the role of women in decision-making at all levels including national level management groups (SUCF). Experience of the Sri Lanka Community Forestry Program (SLCFP) are very encouraging. The SLCFP has been successful in establishing a number of small formal forest management focused CBFs. It was noted that in 2014, 52% of the total membership of CBFs were women and 40% of the total number of office bearers were females (Tacconi, L., Damage, D., 2017).


---
**Page 185**
---

The project will also seek balanced participation of men and women in the local governance structures of the project, encourage active participation of women when line-lunging the field interventions in order to ensure that their specific needs and priorities are well reflected. Enhancing women’s activities related to project monitoring and evaluation will be another important angle of the project.

### iv. Protecting the needs of vulnerable groups (women and men)

The Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) is based on eight overarching principles, one of which is that the project should respect the rights of indigenous peoples. The principle requires that vulnerable groups or individuals that are or may be affected by a project, are identified as early as possible, and assessed for their vulnerability to adverse impacts from the project. Where appropriate, targeted mitigation measures are incorporated so that risks and impacts do not fall disproportionately on vulnerable individuals or groups. In particular, it is important to ensure that women and men in these groups do not face discrimination or prejudice in accessing benefits and resources provided by the project.

As already mentioned above, once the sites for field interventions have been selected, a rapid social analysis will be conducted at each site (see Annex 10.3 of the ESMP for a sample template only). The analysis will allow the identification of vulnerable groups and sub-groups within each site and a good understanding of their livelihood conditions, causes of vulnerability, and potential impacts from project interventions. Vulnerable groups include women and men with disabilities, people with disabilities, people living in poverty or living just above the poverty line who are at risk of impoverishment, and those who have been displaced by previous development projects. The analysis will be used to identify specific needs of vulnerable groups when fine-tuning the field interventions to ensure consideration of the specific needs of vulnerable groups.

It needs to be pointed out that the consideration of the needs of vulnerable groups is already central to the project’s theory of change as the project seeks a strengthening role for women in enhancing food security and subsistence livelihoods, with women explicitly identified as beneficiaries of the project. Hence, the focus in applying the ESMS principle is to avoid unjustified preferential treatment of specific groups (e.g., women) or individuals (e.g., people with disabilities), or any form of discrimination (even inadvertent) in regard to accessing services, resources or benefits. This will be important when selecting sites for field interventions and when planning activities at the site level, such as establishing seed as forest plantations. Wherever technically possible, affirmative action in favor of women or of vulnerable men and women should be considered, e.g., by providing training opportunities specifically targeted with women or allocating underperforming plantation land as home gardens to female-headed households.

The project will apply the following approach to ensure that women have equitable access to project resources, services and activities:

- Seek input regarding project implementation and priorities from women.
- Special attention will be paid to female-headed households due to their increased vulnerability to climate change impacts and poverty.
- Plan interventions with regard to resource availability, location restrictions, and specific priorities;
- Organize women’s groups and women only activities, if necessary, to provide an environment conducive for women to participate, share their knowledge and learn more effectively; and
- Facilitate and promote women's access to key resources, such as land and technology and technical assistance.

Experience demonstrates that this approach will strengthen women’s involvement in the project, as well as lead to better understanding of local conditions, improved planning and implementation of natural resource management options, build their leadership skills and strengthen their motivation to contribute to public discussions.

# Gender Action Plan

The purpose of the project’s Gender Action Plan is to provide a time-bound framework for guiding the implementation of gender-sensitive project activities in order to ensure that project's outputs and results contribute to reducing the gender gap in climate change-induced social, economic and environmental vulnerabilities. This approach will facilitate the achievement of the GCF impacts the project is designed to address, namely:

- Increased resilience of the most vulnerable communities
- Increased access to productive resources, services, and markets
- Increased resilience of health, well-being and water and food security.

The Gender Action Plan provides all implementing partners, accredited entity, national designated authority with a clear framework for implementation of the project’s:

- commitment to gender equality and equity;
- gender inclusiveness in terms of activity implementation;
- accountability for gender equality impacts; and
- equitable use of resource allocation so that both women and men benefit from project implementation.

The Gender Action Plan, which is presented on the following pages in a tabled format, provides a systematic approach to the identification of gender equality objectives and indicators for each of the project activities. This will provide a basis for gender mainstreaming. The Plan describes the gender equality objective(s) that will be focused on or strengthened in each of the project activities. For each of these objectives an indicator is formulated to measure progress towards achieving this goal. In addition, the table provides information about one- mid-term and final targets for these indicators have already been proposed in the table; others will be developed during the implementation phase. The Gender Action Plan will be reviewed at least once a year by the project team and will be revised and fine-tuned once the sites for field interventions have been identified, based on the subsequent rapid social analysis and the in-depth consultations with the respective stakeholder groups (men and women) carried out for detailing the on-the-ground project interventions. These consultations will be crucial to identify the gender equality objectives and indicators that are most relevant and useful for finalizing those that have been indicated in the Plan as “TBD”. This will ensure that the targets are meaningful, achievable and measurable. The Gender Action Plan will also provide a basis for identifying what are needed to achieve the established gender-equity objectives and targets. It should be highlighted that the steps described above (i.e., rapid social analysis, in-depth consultations) will be carried out after the Gender Action Plan is finalized.

It is important to understand that the indicators and targets presented in the Gender Action Plan complement those of the project’s M&E plan. The gender equality objectives at both the output level and output-level, the Gender Action Plan breaks down the output level indicators by monitoring relevant gender aspects for each activity.

The Plan is drawn up with overall responsibility for ensuring gender-sensitive project implementation and the execution of the Gender Action Plan. The ToRs for the Project Director explicitly require experience with gender-differentiated responses in project execution. In addition, a gender specialist will be contracted to carry out the gender analysis and provide technical support on gender issues throughout the project and to support monitoring of the M&E gender-disaggregated indicators and targets. ICRF as implementing partners will specifically draw on the expertise of its Gender Implementing Team in the services it will provide.


---
**Page 187**
---

The PMU and gender specialist will provide gender specific training to the relevant executing agencies' project teams ensuring these are fully versed in gender-sensitivity and inclusivity and how to reflect this in their respective work plans.

An amount of USD 200,000 has been allocated for supporting the implementation of the GAP. As such it will cover the costs for the gender specialist, for the rapid social baseline analysis, for the further engagement with women stakeholders and for the development of a Gender Action Plan (GAP) for gender-specific activities that will be identified through the above mentioned consultation process with women stakeholders. Decisions on how to allocate these funds among the various components of the GAP will be made by the PMU based on the results of the rapid social baseline analysis, which will be completed in June 2018. The decisions will be based on these very consultations. It is important to understand that a more fundamental investment in gender equality and women empowerment is required for the overall success of the project as described in the Gender Action Plan below. In order to monitor the effectiveness of how the gender-aspects are addressed in each of the project activities will be monitored through the indicators and targets specified in the Gender Action Plan.

The Gender Action Plan will be monitored on a semi-annual basis. The general timeline of the implementation of the gender aspects within each activity is determined by the overall timeframe for project implementation as well as the specific timeframes for the rapid social baseline analysis, consultations mentioned above. These consultations will also establish the timeline for the gender-specific activities identified in these meetings as well as roles and responsibilities.
